Hierarchies in relative Picard-Lefschetz theory
Marko Berghoff (1,2), Erik Panzer (1)

Abstract
. We prove a relative version of the Picard-Lefschetz theorem, describing the variation of relative homology groups H d ​ ( Y t ∖ A t , B t ∖ A t ) H_{d}(Y_{t}\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t}) in the fibers of a smooth fiber bundle Y → T Y\to T of complex manifolds with A ∪ B ⊂ Y A\cup B\subset Y transverse. From this we derive the vanishing of certain iterated variations, a system of constraints dubbed “hierarchy”. As applications, we rederive the known analytic structure of Aomoto polylogarithms and massive one loop Feynman integrals. Moreover, we introduce the “simple type” to prove hierarchy constraints in degenerate cases where the Picard-Lefschetz formula does not apply, e.g. the massless triangle or the ice cream cone Feynman diagram. We compare our findings with a “classical” hierarchy of iterated variations (from 1960’s S S -matrix theory) and show how our setup not only explains, but also refines the latter. In order to do so, we need to further resolve the geometry of Feynman motives: We boldly blow up what no one has blown up before.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Monodromy

A holomorphic surjection
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
of connected complex manifolds defines a family
Y_{t}=\pi^{-1}(t)
of analytic varieties that depend on a parameter
t\in T
. The set
L\subset T
of critical values has measure zero, and the smooth fibres
Y_{t}
over the complement of
L
glue into a smooth fibre bundle if
\pi
is proper. Such local trivializations
\pi^{-1}(U)\cong Y_{t_{0}}\times U
over
t_{0}\in U\subseteq T\setminus L
show that the homology groups
H_{d}(Y_{t};\mathbb{Z})
of the fibres
are locally constant over
T\setminus L
.
The monodromy representation

\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})\longrightarrow\Aut H_{d}(Y_{t_{0}};\mathbb{Z})

is induced by parallel transport along paths. It is an important tool to study periods of algebraic varieties and variations of Hodge structures
[
26
]
.

More generally, two analytic subvarieties
A,B\subset Y
give rise to a family of pairs
B_{t}\setminus A_{t}\subset Y_{t}\setminus A_{t}
where
A_{t}=A\cap Y_{t}
and
B_{t}=B\cap Y_{t}
.
We abbreviate the associated relative homology groups as

H_{d}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}=H_{d}(Y_{t}\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t};\mathbb{Z}).

To describe their monodromy, we seek:

(L)

a subset
L\subset T
such that the pairs
(Y_{t}\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
vary as a pair of topological fibre bundles over
T\setminus L
, and

(V)

the representation
\rho\colon\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})\longrightarrow\Aut H_{d}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}}
.

The
Landau variety
of the pair
(Y\setminus A,B\setminus A)
with respect to
\pi
is a closed analytic subvariety
L
of
T
with property
(L)
. We will describe the monodromy
\gamma_{*}=\rho(\gamma)
along a loop
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})
in terms of the
variation

\Var_{\gamma}=\gamma_{*}-\id\in\End H_{d}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}}.

1.2.
Parameter integrals

The monodromy data encodes the analytic continuation
(V)
, and a bound
(L)
on the singularities, of parameter integrals

\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\sigma_{t}}\omega_{t}

of integrands with poles on
A
, over domains with boundary in
B
. Let

•

n
the complex dimension of the fibres
Y_{t}
,

•

\omega\in\Omega^{n}(Y\setminus A)
a holomorphic differential form,

•

\omega_{t}=\omega|_{Y_{t}\setminus A_{t}}
its restriction to a fibre,

•

\sigma_{t_{0}}
an
n
-chain in
Y_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}}
with boundary in
B_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}}
.

The class
[\sigma_{t_{0}}]
extends to a multivalued section

[\sigma_{t}]\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
over
T\setminus L
, representable locally by a continuous family
\sigma_{t}
of chains.
Since
\omega_{t}
is smooth on the compact support of
\sigma_{t}
, the integrals
\mathcal{I}(t)
converge and define a multivalued holomorphic function on
T\setminus L
. As restrictions of a global form
\omega
, the integrands
\omega_{t}
are single-valued. Analytic continuation
\gamma\cdot\mathcal{I}
along a loop
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t)
is thus completely determined by the monodromy:

\gamma\cdot\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\gamma_{*}[\sigma_{t}]}\omega_{t}=\mathcal{I}(t)+\int_{\Var_{\gamma}[\sigma_{t}]}\omega_{t}.

By Riemann’s extension theorem,
\omega
extends holomorphically over any irreducible components of
A
with complex codimension
\geq 2
. We hence drop all such components and assume that
A
is pure of codimension one. Locally,

\omega=\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{n}}\frac{P_{i_{1}\!\cdots i_{n}}(z)}{Q(z)}\ \mathrm{d}z_{i_{1}}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}z_{i_{n}}

can be written with holomorphic functions
P
and
Q
such that
A
is the vanishing locus
\{Q=0\}
of the denominator.

Similarly, the singularities of
\mathcal{I}
are covered already by only those components
\ell_{i}\subseteq L
with complex codimension one.
Items
(L)
and
(V)
above translate into finding an upper bound
L
on the singularities
\bigcup_{i}\ell_{i}
of
\mathcal{I}
, and studying the behaviour of
\mathcal{I}
near each
\ell_{i}
. This purely homological approach is blind to the specific choice of the integrand
\omega
, and some integrands produce integrals with fewer singularities (consider e.g.
\omega=0
). We are not concerned with identifying precisely which components
\ell_{i}\subseteq L
of the Landau variety are genuine singularities of the integral
\mathcal{I}
for a given
\omega
—instead, the homological approach provides insights that hold for all integrands.

1.3.
Picard-Lefschetz theory

Let

\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2},\ldots\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{B}=\{B_{1},B_{2},\ldots\}

denote the finite sets of irreducible components of
A
and
B
. We assume that these are smooth hypersurfaces with transverse intersections.
Suppose that
\ell\subseteq L
is a component such that
A\cup B
has a unique, non-degenerate critical point
p(t)
over
t\in\ell
. Let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{i},B_{1},\ldots,B_{j}
denote the hypersurfaces that contain
p(t)
. Then for a small loop
\gamma
in
T\setminus L
, that is based at
t_{0}
and winds around
\ell
, the Picard-Lefschetz formula

(1.1)

\Var_{\gamma}(\sigma)=(-1)^{(n+1)(n+2)/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle\cdot\nu_{p}

determines the variation of
\sigma\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}}
in terms of an intersection number
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle
and vanishing cycles
\nu_{p}\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}}
,
\widetilde{\nu}_{p}\in H_{n}(Y\setminus B,A)_{t_{0}}
. The latter are constructed out of a sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-i-j}
localized near
p(t)
and embedded in the submanifold
A_{1}\cap\ldots\cap A_{i}\cap B_{1}\cap\ldots\cap B_{j}
.

For a single hypersurface
\lvert\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}\rvert\leq 1
, the Picard-Lefschetz theorem is well-known
[
60
,
61
]
. A proof, allowing for arbitrary isolated critical points, can be found in
[
34
, §5]
.
There are even generalizations for isolated critical points on complete intersections
[
28
,
40
]
.
However, the case with
multiple
hypersurfaces has not been studied as much. Arrangements with
\lvert\mathfrak{A}\rvert>1
are treated in
[
21
]
, but without boundary (
B=\varnothing
). The generalization to
B\neq\varnothing
is briefly mentioned in
[
51
]
, but important details are left out.

The first result of this paper is a detailed, self-contained proof of (
1.1
) in the general case where
A,B\neq\varnothing
. The main new insights are:

•

We correct an error in the literature:
All sources claim that all linear pinches (
i+j=n+1
) have zero variation.
1
1

1

See the last paragraph in
[
60
, §I.9]
or
[
61
, §I.8]
, and footnote 11 on
[
51
, p. 95]
.
As
section

1.8.1
shows, this is not the case. Only the linear pinches of “pure
\mathfrak{A}
type” (
j=0
) or “pure
\mathfrak{B}
type” (
i=0
) have zero variation, whereas linear pinches on
mixed
intersections of
\mathfrak{A}
’s
and

\mathfrak{B}
’s have non-zero variation.

•

We prove a fairly general vanishing statement for iterated variations, see
section

1.4
.

The main point of this paper is therefore that it pays off to keep track of which elements of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
contain a given critical point.
The distinction between
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
is important, despite the fact that the Landau variety
L
depends only on the combined arrangement
A\cup B
.

1.4.
Hierarchy principle

Consider an iterated variation around two components
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
of the Landau variety. The
hierarchy principle
is a combinatorial criterion that ensures
\Var_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\gamma}=0
.
Suppose for example that the fibres over
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
have a single non-degenerate critical point
p
and
p^{\prime}
, respectively.
Then according to (
1.1
), we are asking if
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\nu_{p}}\rangle=0
.

The set of all hypersurfaces in
\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}
that contain
p
encodes a pair

(I,J)=(\{i\colon p\in A_{i}\},\{j\colon p\in B_{j}\}),

called the
type
of
p
. Let
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})
denote the type of
p^{\prime}
.
The variation around
\ell
localizes in a small neighbourhood of
p
, which meets only those
A_{i}
and
B_{j}
with
i\in I,j\in J
. Hence the vanishing cycle
\nu_{p}
is in the image of

H_{n}\Big(Y\setminus A,\bigcup\nolimits_{j\in J}B_{j}\Big)_{t_{0}}\longrightarrow H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}},

and therefore the boundary of
\nu_{p}
remains forever confined to the
B_{j}
with
j\in J
—even after continuation from
t_{0}
to any
t\in T\setminus L
.

Furthermore,
\nu_{p}
is an iterated Leray coboundary (tube) around each
A_{i}
with
i\in I
. It follows that for any
i\in I
,
\nu_{p}
is in the kernel of

H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t_{0}}\longrightarrow H_{n}\Big(Y\setminus\bigcup\nolimits_{k\neq i}A_{k},B\Big)_{t_{0}}.

So if some
i\in I
is not in
I^{\prime}
, then
\nu_{p}
becomes zero in the localization of
\Var_{\gamma^{\prime}}
near
p^{\prime}
. A dual argument for the vanishing cycles
\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}
shows that
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\nu_{p}}\rangle=0
holds also whenever there exists
j\in J^{\prime}
that is not in
J
.

To summarize, the iterated variation
\Var_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\gamma}
can only be nonzero if
p
and
p^{\prime}
are
compatible
(denoted
p^{\prime}\leqslant p
), that is, if

(1.2)

I^{\prime}\supseteq I\quad\text{and}\quad J^{\prime}\subseteq J.

Therefore, a non-zero iteration of variations
\Var_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\Var_{1}
requires that the sets of participating
\mathfrak{B}
-elements get smaller (
I_{1}\supseteq I_{2}\supseteq\ldots
), whereas the sets of
\mathfrak{A}
-elements can only get bigger (
J_{1}\subseteq J_{2}\subseteq\ldots
).

In
section

4
we generalize this property in various ways:

•

For
linear
simple pinches (
3.13
), compatibility requires strict containment:
I^{\prime}\supsetneq I
and
J^{\prime}\subsetneq J
.

•

When there are multiple critical points in a fiber, we call two components of the Landau variety
compatible
(
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
), if all critical points over
\ell^{\prime}
are compatible with all critical points over
\ell
.

•

We can allow critical points that are not simple pinches, or critical
sets

p\subset A^{I}\cap B^{J}
that are not even isolated. Then (
1.2
) becomes

(1.3)

I^{\prime}\supseteq I_{\mathsf{s}}\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}\subseteq J,

in terms of the
simple components

I_{\mathsf{s}}\subseteq I
and
J_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}\subseteq J
(
4.9
).
Here a component
A_{i}
or
B_{j}
is called simple, if
p
remains critical even if that component is removed from the arrangement.

The upshot is a relation
\leqslant
on the components
\ell
of
L
(
4.17
). The hierarchy principle (
4.18
) states then that

(1.4)

\Var_{\gamma^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\gamma}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell^{\prime}\nleqslant\ell.

As an application, we show in
sections

5.1
and
6.1
how to quickly derive the known Landau variety and hierarchy for Aomoto polylogarithms and massive one loop Feynman integrals.

1.5.
Feynman integrals

The application of homological methods to Feynman integrals has a long history
[
31
,
23
]
.
Approaches using the
momentum representation
, like
[
20
,
63
]
, are complicated by the need to compactify the integration domain, which poses unsolved challenges beyond one loop
[
46
]
.

This problem does not appear in the
parametric representation
. However, in this representation, the integration domain has boundary. An analysis of (iterated) variations therefore requires a relative version of the (hierarchy) Picard-Lefschetz theorems.
Using this setup, Boyling derived constraints of the form (
1.4
) for massive one-loop graphs
[
10
, §2]
.

The Picard-Lefschetz formula (
1.1
) is however not usually applicable to more general Feynman integrals, because the critical sets are often not simple and isolated.
Using our generalized hierarchy principle, we can nevertheless obtain constraints on iterated variations in such cases. We carry this out in detail for Feynman graphs with two loops (sunrise and ice cream cone) or one loop and zero masses (triangle), see
section

6
.

We find that the distinction in (
1.3
) between the type of a singularity
\ell\subset L
vs. its simple components is indeed crucial: applying (
1.2
) to singularities that are not simple pinches leads to wrong conclusions. This need for a refined analysis was noted for Feynman integrals in
[
36
]
, correcting a too optimistic expectation for the
\mathfrak{B}
-hierarchy from
[
37
]
. In
sections

6.1
and
6.4
we demonstrate that also the
\mathfrak{A}
-hierarchy is interesting for Feynman integrals.

1.6.
Outlook

The techniques developed in this paper provide a framework to explain variation constraints of the form (
1.4
). We hope that these methods will be useful to prove constraints for infinite families of Feynman integrals, and thereby scattering amplitudes. For example,
extended Steinmann relations

[
15
, §3]
are applied with great effect in high order perturbation theory. For now, these relations and generalizations thereof
[
18
]
are conjectures.

Remark 1.1
.

Originally, Steinmann relations
[
38
,
55
]
refer to amplitudes—not individual Feynman diagrams—and they only apply in a restricted (“physical”) region of parameter space. This translates into the statement
\Var_{\gamma^{\prime}}\Var_{\gamma}\sigma=0
for certain chains
\sigma
, but not all. In the physical region, first type singularities have simple pinch type and can thus be studied with Picard-Lefschetz theory
[
49
,
50
,
29
]
. The resulting hierarchy has a simple formulation, but it does not apply outside the physical region, ignores singularities of second type, and requires generic masses.
2
2

2

For example, external particles are not allowed to all have the same mass.

We do not make any such assumptions.

1.7.
Outline of the paper

In
section

2
we review the solution of problem
(L)
, that is, how to find the Landau variety of a pair
(Y\setminus A,B\setminus A)
with respect to a smooth map of complex manifolds
\pi\colon Y\to T
. We discuss (canonical) stratifications, Thom’s first isotopy lemma (including a smooth version of it), how to compute
L
and how this applies to the study of parameter integrals.

Section

3
deals then with problem
(V)
via Picard-Lefschetz theory. We review the classical Picard-Lefschetz theorem and prove its generalization to relative homology. In the process, we give a concise account of all necessary technical tools: Definition of the monodromy and variation operators, localization arguments, characterization of
simple pinch
critical points, Leray’s residue and (co)boundary maps, and definition of the
vanishing chains
. After the stage is set, we state and prove the (relative) Picard-Lefschetz theorem,
3.21
, then discuss refinements for linear pinches (
3.22
) and generalizations to arbitrary simple pinches (
eq.

3.22
).

In
section

4
we introduce our hierarchy principle. We first present it for simple pinches (
eqs.

4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
and
4.6
), then for certain components of the Landau variety (
4.7
), and finally state it in its most general form (corollaries
4.16
and
4.18
) which allows for non-isolated critical sets.

The following two sections apply the previously developed theory to two families of examples. We rigorously derive their Landau varieties and discuss implications (and limitations) of the hierarchy principle. In
section

5
we consider polylogarithms: While
section

5.2
deals with a specific (slightly degenerate) example, the dilogarithm,
section

5.1
discusses a tame family, the Aomoto polylogarithms, in full detail. Finally, in
section

6
we study Feynman integrals. After introducing some notation, we start with massive one loop graphs (
section

6.1
) where our setup works out of the box. Then we consider some specific examples where this is not the case, the massless triangle, the massive sunrise and the ice cream cone. We show how additional blow ups (beyond the Feynman motive) turn
A\cup B
transverse, so that we can apply our methods. This allows us to explain and refine what is called
breakdown of the hierarchical principle
in
[
10
,
36
]
.

The appendices provide several technical details, that are hard to find in the existing literature:

•

Appendix

A
calculates all
relative
homology groups of the arrangement of hypersurfaces at a linear or quadratic simple pinch, generalizing results of
[
21
]
. To be fully self-contained, we also compute the classical Picard-Lefschetz formula for the variation of a quadric.

•

Appendix

B
explains the construction of the long exact sequences for partial boundaries and relative residues from
[
39
, Chapitre 2]
, in the general case of transverse arrangements of smooth closed submanifolds with arbitrary codimensions.

•

Appendix

C
gives a general definition and the key properties of relative intersection numbers and the duality swapping
A\leftrightarrow B
.

•

Appendix

D
shows that the codimension one components
\ell
of
L
already determine the full fundamental group
\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)
, and that the kernel of
\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(T)
is generated by so-called
simple loops
(
3.1
).

1.8.
Two examples

We illustrate our findings with two very simple examples: The first example observes that the non-trivial monodromy of the logarithm arises from a linear pinch; the second example illustrates the hierarchy principle for the bubble Feynman integral. In both cases,
Y=X\times T
is a product with
\pi(x,t)=t
and compact fibre
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

1.8.1.
The logarithm

For
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
consider the integral

\log(1-t)=\int_{\sigma}\frac{t\,\mathrm{d}x}{tx-1}.

For
t\neq[1,\infty]
, the interval
\sigma=[0,1]
defines a class in
H_{1}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
, where the hypersurfaces
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
and
\mathfrak{B}=\{B_{1},B_{2}\}
of
Y
are given by

A_{1}=\{xt=1\},\ A_{2}=\{x=\infty\},\ B_{1}=\{x=0\},\ B_{2}=\{x=1\}.

These four hypersurfaces are smooth and transverse to each other.
Each hypersurface submerses onto
T
. Thus, the critical strata are the points (codimension two) where two hypersurfaces in
\mathfrak{A}
, or one in
\mathfrak{A}
and one from
\mathfrak{B}
, intersect (see
fig.

1
):

\begin{cases}\{xt=1\}\cap\{x=\infty\}=\{(\infty,0)\},&\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathfrak{A}\\
\{xt=1\}\cap\{x=0\}=\{(0,\infty)\},&\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathfrak{B}\\
\{xt=1\}\cap\{x=1\}=\{(1,1)\},&\mathfrak{A}\cap\mathfrak{B}.\\
\end{cases}

\scriptstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{x=\infty}
\scriptstyle{x=0}
\scriptstyle{x=1}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{x={t}^{-1}}

Figure 1.
The arrangement
D=A\cup B\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
associated to the integral
\log(1-t)=\int_{\sigma_{t}}\frac{t\,\mathrm{d}x}{tx-1}
.

These critical points are called
linear pinches
and their projection along
\pi
determines the Landau variety
L=\{0,1,\infty\}
. At the points
t=1,\infty
, the function
\log(1-t)
has logarithmic singularities. In contrast, at
t=0
, this function is smooth and takes values in
2\mathrm{i}\pi\mathbb{Z}
. In particular, the variation at
t=0
is zero. We will see in general that linear pinches of “pure
\mathfrak{A}
-type” (or “pure
\mathfrak{B}
-type”) always have zero variation, whereas “mixed
\mathfrak{A}
–
\mathfrak{B}
-type” linear pinches have non-zero variation.

In order to describe the monodromy associated to small loops winding around
L
we note that for generic
t
the first relative Betti homology of the fiber is
H_{1}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}=H_{1}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. It is spanned by the interval
\sigma=[0,1]
, and a small loop
\nu
around the point
x={t}^{-1}
. This cycle is constructed as follows (see
fig.

2
):

(1)

Let
\ell\in L
. For
t
close to
\ell
we consider the pair of fibers
(X,A_{t}\cup B_{t})
. The
vanishing cell

\zeta_{\ell}
is the unique (real)
n
-dimensional cell (
n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}X
) in
X
that is bounded by
A_{t}\cup B_{t}
and vanishes as
t\to\ell
.

In the present case there are three such cells, given by paths from
x
to
{t}^{-1}
for
x=0,1,\infty\in X
(corresponding to
\ell=\infty,1,0
). Each represents an element in
H_{1}(Y,A\cup B)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
. Note that, if we restrict to a small neighbourhood
U
of a critical point
x={\ell}^{-1}
, then each class is the unique generator of
H_{1}(U,(A_{t}\cup B_{t})\cap U)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

(2)

The
vanishing cycle

\nu_{\ell}
is defined as the
Leray coboundary
(see
section

3.4
) of the
partial boundary
of the vanishing cell (both with respect to the relevant hypersurfaces of
\mathfrak{A}
-type),
\nu_{\ell}:=\delta_{A}\partial_{A}\zeta_{\ell}
.

Here
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{\infty}
coincide, both given by small loops around
x={t}^{-1}
. They represent classes
[\nu_{1}]\equiv[\nu_{\infty}]
in
H_{1}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
, unique after localisation. The vanishing cycle
\nu_{0}
is zero, because
\partial_{A}\zeta_{0}=\partial_{A_{2}}\partial_{A_{1}}\zeta_{0}=0
.

\mathbb{P}^{1}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{\nu_{1}}
\scriptstyle{{t}^{-1}}

\mathbb{P}^{1}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{1}

Figure 2.
Geometry of
\log(1-t)
: The left hand side shows the vanishing cell
\zeta_{1}
and vanishing cycle
\nu_{1}
for
t
close to 1, the right hand side sketches the monodromy of
\sigma
associated to a small loop winding around
t=1
; the resulting relative cycle
\widetilde{\sigma}
is homologous to
\sigma+\nu_{1}
.

The Picard-Lefschetz theorem states that the variation
\Var_{\ell}
associated to a small loop winding around
\ell
maps a homology class
h\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
to a multiple of the vanishing cycle
N[\nu_{\ell}]
where
N\in\mathbb{Z}
is determined by a (relative)
intersection index

\langle{\partial_{B}h}\,|\,{\partial_{B}\zeta_{\ell}}\rangle

with
\partial_{B}
denoting the iterated partial boundary with respect to
B
.
3
3

3

We discuss intersection indices in
appendix

C
.

In the present case we have
\nu_{0}=0
, so the variation associated to
0\in L
vanishes. For
\ell=1,\infty
one calculates
\langle{\partial_{B}\sigma}\,|\,{\partial_{B}\zeta_{\ell}}\rangle=\pm 1
, while
\langle{\partial_{B}\nu_{\ell}}\,|\,{\partial_{B}\zeta_{\ell}}\rangle=\langle{0}\,|\,{\partial_{B}\zeta_{\ell}}\rangle=0
. In summary, the variation of
[\sigma]
is non-trivial around
\ell=1,\infty
, the variation of
[\nu_{1}]=[\nu_{\infty}]
is trivial around all points of
L
. We recover thus the well-known analytic structure of the function
\log(1-t)
.

1.8.2.
The bubble Feynman integral

As a second example we consider the parametric Feynman integrals (cf.
section

6
) of the bubble graph:

\mathcal{I}(p^{2},m_{1}^{2},m_{2}^{2})=\int_{\sigma=\{[x_{1}:x_{2}]\colon x_{i}>0\}}\frac{P\Omega}{\mathcal{U}^{a}\mathcal{F}^{b}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\left.\frac{P}{\mathcal{U}\mathcal{F}}\right|_{x_{1}=1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}.

Here
a
and
b
are integers,
P
is any homogeneous polynomial in
x
with degree
a+2b-2\geq 0
, the projectivized measure is
\Omega=x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}-x_{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}
, and

\mathcal{U}=x_{1}+x_{2},\qquad\mathcal{F}=(x_{1}+x_{2})(m_{1}^{2}x_{1}+m_{2}^{2}x_{2})-p^{2}x_{1}x_{2}.

The parameters are
T=\{(p^{2},m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{3}\}
.
The arrangements
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{1},A_{2}\}
and
\mathfrak{B}=\{B_{1},B_{2}\}
consist of the boundary points
B_{1}=\{[0:1]\}
,
B_{2}=\{[1:0]\}
, a point
A_{1}=\{\mathcal{U}=0\}=\{[1:-1]\}
, and a pair of points

A_{2}=\{\mathcal{F}=0\}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}=\big\{\big[p^{2}-m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}\pm\sqrt{\Delta}:2m_{1}^{2}\big]\big\}

where
\Delta=(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}-p^{2})^{2}-4m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}=\Delta_{+}\Delta_{-}
with
\Delta_{\pm}=p^{2}-(m_{1}\pm m_{2})^{2}
.

The Landau variety
L=\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{\Delta}\cup\ell_{p}
has four components, determined by collisions of the points
Q_{1,2}
—with each other, or with one of
B_{1},B_{2},A_{1}
:

•

a linear pinch of
A_{2}\cap B_{1}
(
Q_{1}=B_{1}
) over
\ell_{2}=\{m_{2}^{2}=0\}
,

•

a linear pinch of
A_{2}\cap B_{2}
(
Q_{2}=B_{2}
) over
\ell_{1}=\{m_{1}^{2}=0\}
,

•

a quadratic pinch of
A_{2}
(
Q_{1}=Q_{2}
) over
\ell_{\Delta}=\{\Delta=0\}
,

•

a linear pinch of
A_{1}\cap A_{2}
(
A_{1}=Q_{1}
or
A_{1}=Q_{2}
) over
\ell_{p}=\{p^{2}=0\}
.

In physics terms,
\ell_{1,2}
are called “reduced singularities”, whereas
\ell_{\Delta}
and
\ell_{p}
are “leading singularities” of the “first type” (
\ell_{\Delta}
) and “second type” (
\ell_{p}
). The components
\Delta_{+}
and
\Delta_{-}
of
\Delta
are also referred to as “normal threshold” and “pseudo threshold”, respectively.

The vanishing cells of
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
, and
\ell_{\Delta}
are paths

\zeta_{1}=[B_{2},Q_{1}],\quad\zeta_{2}=[B_{1},Q_{2}],\quad\zeta_{\Delta}=[Q_{1},Q_{2}]

where the branch of
\sqrt{\Delta}
is chosen such that
Q_{1}\rightarrow B_{2}
for
m_{1}\rightarrow 0
and
Q_{2}\rightarrow B_{1}
for
m_{2}\rightarrow 0
.
The respective vanishing cycles
\nu_{\bullet}=\delta_{A_{2}}\partial_{A_{2}}\zeta_{\bullet}
are

\nu_{1}=\delta_{A_{2}}[Q_{1}],\quad\nu_{2}=\delta_{A_{2}}[Q_{2}],\quad\nu_{\Delta}=\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}.

The second type singularity
p^{2}=0
is a linear pinch on the intersection of two elements of
\mathfrak{A}
. In this “pure
\mathfrak{A}
’’ case, the vanishing cycle is zero and so
4
4

4

This shows that the integral
\mathcal{I}
is meromorphic at
\ell_{p}
, but not necessarily smooth. For example, the integral
\int_{\sigma}x_{1}\Omega/(\mathcal{U}\mathcal{F})
with
P=x_{1}
develops a pole at
\ell_{p}
(on some sheets).

\Var_{\ell_{p}}=0.

The dual vanishing cycles
\widetilde{\nu}_{\bullet}=-\delta_{B}\partial_{B}\zeta_{\bullet}
over
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
have intersection numbers
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{1}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle=\langle{\partial_{B}\zeta_{1}}\,|\,{\partial_{B}\sigma}\rangle=\langle{-[B_{2}]}\,|\,{[B_{1}]-[B_{2}]}\rangle=1
,
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{2}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle=-1
, and
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{i}}\,|\,{\nu_{j}}\rangle=0
for all
i,j\in\{1,2\}
because
\partial_{B}\nu_{j}=0
. So we determine

\Var_{\ell_{1}}[\sigma]=-\nu_{1},\qquad\Var_{\ell_{2}}[\sigma]=\nu_{2},\qquad\Var_{\ell_{i}}\nu_{j}=0

from the Picard-Lefschetz formula (
1.1
). For example, if
m_{1}^{2}\rightarrow 0
, then
Q_{1}=[1:m_{1}^{2}/(p^{2}-m_{2}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(m_{1}^{4})]
so that, in the
x_{2}/x_{1}
chart,
Q_{1}
encircles the origin (the starting point
B_{2}=[1:0]
of the integration path) in the same sense as
m_{1}^{2}
goes around the origin, say
counter clockwise
. Deforming
\sigma
to avoid
Q_{1}
winds up a
clockwise
loop around
Q_{1}
(cf.
fig.

2
).

Assume that
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
are real and positive. Then at the normal threshold, the degeneration of
A_{2}
into
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=[m_{2}:m_{1}]
pinches the straight path
\sigma
, whereas at the pseudo-threshold, the collision
Q_{1}=Q_{2}=[-m_{2}:m_{1}]
happens away from
\sigma
:

\Var_{\ell_{\Delta_{+}}}[\sigma]=\nu_{\Delta},\qquad\Var_{\ell_{\Delta_{-}}}[\sigma]=0.

In both cases,
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
get swapped,
\delta_{A_{2}}[Q_{1}]\leftrightarrow\delta_{A_{2}}[Q_{2}]
, and thus

\Var_{\ell_{\Delta}}\nu_{1}=-\Var_{\ell_{\Delta}}\nu_{2}=\nu_{2}-\nu_{1}.

Since the group
H_{1}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
is generated by
\sigma
,
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
, we have completely determined all variation operators.
The hierarchy on
L
can be expressed in the following diagram:

(1.5)

Only iterated variations that follow arrows may be non-zero, for example

\Var_{\ell_{\Delta}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{2}}[\sigma]=\Var_{\ell_{\Delta}}\nu_{2}=\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}.

All other iterated variations vanish. For instance,

\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{\Delta}},\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{p}},\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{i}},\Var_{\ell_{p}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{p}}=0.

36
]
to our attention.

2.
Stratified maps and their Landau varieties

2.1.
Stratified sets

Let
Y
be a smooth manifold and
D\subset Y
a closed subset. We require that
D
can be decomposed into smooth pieces as follows.

Definition 2.1
.

A
stratification
of a topological space
D
is a sequence

(2.1)

\varnothing=C_{-1}\subseteq C_{0}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq C_{k}=D

of closed sets
C_{i}
such that

•

the successive complements
C_{i}\setminus C_{i-1}
are smooth manifolds of dimension
i
. Their connected components are called
strata
, and

•

for each stratum
S
, its boundary
\partial S=\overline{S}\setminus S
is a union of strata of smaller dimension than
S
.

We write
\mathfrak{S}=\bigsqcup_{i}\pi_{0}(C_{i}\setminus C_{i-1})
for the set of all strata. The stratification can be recovered from this set as
C_{i}=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}\colon\dim S\leq i}S
.

Definition 2.2
.

A finite collection
\{D_{1},D_{2},\ldots\}
of closed smooth submanifolds
D_{i}\subseteq Y
is called
transverse
5
5

5

Some authors, e.g.
[
21
,
51
]
, call it “in general position”.
, if every intersection

D^{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}=D_{i_{1}}\cap\ldots\cap D_{i_{m}}

can be covered with charts

(x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{m})\colon Y\supset U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r_{0}}\times\cdots\times\mathbb{R}^{r_{m}}=\mathbb{R}^{n}

such that
D_{i_{k}}\cap U=\{x_{k}=0\}
for each
k=1,\ldots,m
.
Here we denote
n=\dim Y
,
r_{k}=n-\dim D_{i_{k}}
and
r_{0}=n-\sum_{k=1}^{m}r_{k}
. See
B.1
for a coordinate-free formulation.

In this situation, all intersections
D^{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}
are themselves closed submanifolds. Thus we obtain a canonical stratification of the union
D=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}
by setting
C_{d}=\bigcup_{I\colon\dim D^{I}\leq d}D^{I}
.

We consider the special case where
Y
is a complex manifold with complex dimension
n
, and each
D_{i}
is a smooth complex hypersurface. Then “
D
is transverse” is also called “
D
has
simple normal crossings
” and means that locally there exist
m\leq n
and a holomorphic chart
x\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
D\cap U=\{x_{1}\cdots x_{m}=0\}
. The canonical stratification is then given by

(2.2)

C_{2d}=\bigcup_{\lvert I\rvert=n-d}D^{I}\quad\text{with}\quad n=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}Y.

The topological closure
S\mapsto\overline{S}
defines a bijection between the strata of dimension
2d
(connected components of
C_{2d}\setminus C_{2d-2}
) and the irreducible components of the analytic variety
C_{2d}
.

If the hypersurfaces are not smooth, or smooth but not transverse, then (
2.2
) is not necessarily a stratification—and even if it happens to be a stratification, it may not be sufficiently regular in order to apply the isotopy theorems of the next section. This issue can be resolved in at least two ways:

(1)

Apply resolution of singularities, that is, construct an iterated blow up
\widetilde{Y}\rightarrow Y
such that the total transform
\widetilde{D}
of
D
has simple normal crossings in
\widetilde{Y}
. This preserves the complement
Y\setminus D\cong\widetilde{Y}\setminus\widetilde{D}
.

(2)

Replace (
2.2
) by a more subtle stratification and substitute transversality by a weaker notion of regular incidence.

We will use the first option and discuss this procedure specifically for Feynman integrals in
section

6
.
For completeness, we briefly address the second option.

Definition 2.3
.

A stratification is called
Whitney regular
if every pair
S,S^{\prime}
of strata that are incident (
S\subseteq\partial S^{\prime}
) satisfies
Whitney’s conditions A and B of regular incidence
: Embed
S
and
S^{\prime}
in some Euclidean space. Then at every point
x\in S
, we require that for all convergent sequences
x^{\prime}\to x
of points
x^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
:

(A)

if
T_{x^{\prime}}S^{\prime}
converges as
x^{\prime}\mapsto x
, then its limit
\tau\subseteq T_{x}Y
(understood in the Graßmannian bundle of subspaces of
TY
) contains
T_{x}S\subseteq\tau
,

(B)

for any local retraction
r\colon S^{\prime}\to S
, the angle between
T_{x^{\prime}}S^{\prime}
and the vector
\overrightarrow{x^{\prime},r(x^{\prime})}
converges to zero as
x^{\prime}\to x
.

Condition (A) is implied by (B) and hence redundant. For details we refer to
[
51
,
41
,
24
,
59
]
.
Whitney showed
[
64
]
that every (analytic or algebraic) subvariety
D
admits a Whitney regular stratification.

Note that a (Whitney regular) stratification of
D
always gives rise to a (Whitney regular) stratification of
Y
. Simply add
Y\setminus D
as an additional stratum; conditions (A) and (B) are satisfied automatically, since for
S^{\prime}=Y\setminus D
we have
T_{x^{\prime}}S^{\prime}=T_{x^{\prime}}Y
.

In the following we will assume that
D
is normal crossing in
Y
. In this case (and more generally for transverse arrangements of smooth submanifolds) the canonical stratification described above is automatically Whitney regular. In fact it is minimal in the sense that every other Whitney stratification is a refinement of it.

2.2.
Isotopy theorems

Given a smooth map
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
of manifolds, the fibres
Y_{t}=\pi^{-1}(t)
vary with
t\in T
. Let
T
be connected. Then
Ehresmann’s fibration theorem
gives a sufficient criterion for all fibres to be diffeomorphic:

Theorem 2.4

(Ehresmann)
.

Every proper submersion is a smooth fibre bundle.

Hence, if a closed subset
D\subset Y
is smooth and the restriction
\pi|_{D}
a proper submersion, then
D
is a smooth fibre bundle over
T
. Namely, each
t\in T
has an open neighbourhood
U
over which
D
trivializes into a product of
U
with the (smooth) fibre
D_{t}=D\cap\pi^{-1}(t)
. That is, we have commutative diagrams

(2.3)

31.27847pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.27847pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 92.11897pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 91.89813pt\raise-6.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 109.52313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 109.52313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D_{t}\times U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 90.24252pt\raise-21.18082pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.35982pt\raise-28.76541pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-6.61632pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 51.44179pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces U}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.44179pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.61633pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 124.52103pt\raise-31.98666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

where
\phi
is a diffeomorphism and
p
denotes the projection onto the factor
U
.

If
D
is singular, we cannot apply
2.4
to
D
itself. Thus, stratify
D
, so that we may apply the theorem to each (smooth) stratum
S
. If
\pi|_{S}
is a submersion for each
S
, we conclude that all strata are smooth fibre bundles.

However, it is not always possible to glue trivializations of the strata
S
into a trivialization of
D
.
Thom’s first isotopy lemma
guarantees that this
does
work, provided that the stratification is sufficiently nice.

Theorem 2.5

(Thom’s first isotopy lemma)
.

Let
\pi\colon Y\to T
be a smooth map and
D\subseteq Y
a closed subset with a Whitney regular stratification, so that

•

\pi|_{D}
is proper, and

•

\pi|_{S}
is a submersion for each stratum
S
of
D
.

Then
\pi|_{D}
is a stratified fibre bundle.

Proof.

See
[
24
, §1.5–1.7]
for a sketch, and
[
41
]
for a detailed proof.
∎

By a
stratified fibre bundle
6
6

6

also called
locally trivial stratified fiber bundle

[
51
]
or
locally trivial stratified map

[
24
]
we mean a topological fibre bundle
D\rightarrow T
with the property that the trivializations
\phi
in (
2.3
) can be chosen such that they simultaneously trivialize all strata
S
of
D
:

(2.4)

\phi(S\cap\pi^{-1}(U))=S_{t}\times U,

where
S_{t}=S\cap\pi^{-1}(t)
.
For connected
T
, it follows that all fibres
D_{t}
are homeomorphic, in a stratum preserving way.

Thom’s first isotopy lemma shows only that
\pi|_{D}
is a
topological
fibre bundle: the trivializations
\phi
are homeomorphisms, but not necessarily smooth. To achieve smoothness, we need even stronger regularity of
D
. Transversality will be sufficient for our applications
[
51
, §X, Lemma 2.1]
:

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
\pi\colon Y\to T
be a proper submersion and
D=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}
for transverse smooth closed submanifolds
D_{i}\subset Y
. Suppose that the restriction of
\pi
to
D^{J}=\bigcap_{j\in J}D_{j}
is a submersion, for each
J\subseteq I
.

Then
\pi
is a
smooth
stratified fibre bundle, which means that
Y
has
smooth
local trivializations
\phi\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\cong Y_{t}\times U
with the property that
7
7

7

This condition is equivalent to (
2.4
) for all strata
S
of the canonical stratification of
D
.

\phi(D_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(U))=(D_{i}\cap\pi^{-1}(t))\times U\quad\text{for all}\quad i\in I.

Proof.

It follows from
2.2
that
Y
has a cover by charts
x^{V}\colon V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the property that if
J\subseteq I
denotes the indices
i
with
D_{i}\cap V\neq\varnothing
, then

x^{V}(D_{i}\cap V)=\bigcap_{\alpha\in K_{i}}\{x^{V}_{\alpha}=0\}

for each
i\in J
, with disjoint subsets
K_{i}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
. Permute the coordinates so that the first
r=\dim D^{J}
of them parametrize
D^{J}
, whereas
\bigcup_{i\in J}K_{i}=\{r+1,\ldots,n\}
. Since
\pi|_{D^{J}}
is a submersion, we can furthermore arrange that the first
k=\dim T\leq r
coordinates form a chart of
T
. This way, every smooth vector field
u
on
T
lifts to a smooth vector field
u^{V}
on
V
such that:

•

u^{V}
is a lift of
u
:
\mathrm{d}\pi_{y}(u^{V}_{y})=u_{\pi(y)}
at every
y\in V
, and

•

u^{V}
is a linear combination of
\partial/\partial x^{V}_{1},\ldots,\partial/\partial x^{V}_{k}
.

The latter implies that
u^{V}
is tangent to all
D_{i}
. Using a partition of unity, we can glue the
u^{V}
to a smooth vector field
w
on
Y
. This construction provides a smooth lift
w
of
u
that is tangent to each
D_{i}
. The flow
\Phi^{w}_{\tau}
along such a vector field preserves the submanifolds:
\Phi^{w}_{\tau}(D_{i})=D_{i}

Now pick any point
p\in T
and a chart
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
t(p)=0
. Construct lifts
w_{\alpha}
of the vector fields
u_{\alpha}=\partial/\partial t_{\alpha}
on
U
, then we have

\Phi^{u_{\alpha}}_{\tau}\circ\pi=\pi\circ\Phi^{w_{\alpha}}_{\tau}

whenever the corresponding flows are defined. Since
\pi
is proper, we can shrink
U
and find
\epsilon>0
so that
\Phi^{w_{\alpha}}_{\tau}(y)
is defined for all
y\in\pi^{-1}(U)
and
\lvert\tau\rvert<\epsilon
. We obtain the desired trivialization
\phi\colon{\pi}^{-1}(U)\to{\pi}^{-1}(p)\times U
by

y\longmapsto\Big(\left(\Phi^{w_{k}}_{-t_{k}}\circ\cdots\circ\Phi^{w_{1}}_{-t_{1}}\right)(y),\pi(y)\Big)

where
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})=\pi(y)
.
∎

2.3.
Landau varieties

Let
\pi\colon Y\to T
be smooth and surjective, and
\mathfrak{S}
a Whitney regular stratification of a subset
D\subset Y
.
The
critical set

cS\subseteq S
of a stratum
S\in\mathfrak{S}
is the subset where the restriction
\pi|_{S}
fails to be a submersion:
\mathrm{rank}({\mathrm{d}\pi|_{S}})<\dim T
. Points in
cS
and
\pi(cS)\subset T
are called
critical points
and
critical values
, respectively.

Definition 2.7
.

The
Landau variety
is the set of all critical values,
8
8

8

In
[
12
]
, the Landau variety is defined to consist only of the codimension one part.

L=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\pi(cS)\subset T.

If
\pi
is proper, then
2.5
guarantees that the restriction of
\pi
to
{\pi}^{-1}(T\setminus L)
is a stratified fibre bundle over
T\setminus L
.

Now let
Y
and
T
be complex manifolds,
\pi\colon Y\to T
proper and analytic, and
D\subset Y
stratified such that the
C_{i}
in (
2.1
) are complex analytic varieties. Then the Landau variety is a complex analytic variety
[
51
]
. We care only for its irreducible components with complex codimension one, which we denote

\mathfrak{L}=\{\ell\subseteq L\colon\ \text{$\ell$ is irreducible and $\codim\ell=1$}\}.

The restriction to codimension one arises as follows: An integral in fibres of
\pi
, see
section

2.4
, defines a multivalued analytic function
\mathcal{I}\colon T\setminus L\to\mathbb{C}
. The Landau variety provides an upper bound on the singularities of
\mathcal{I}
. However, we also have Riemann’s Extension Theorem
[
25
, §7.1]
:

Theorem 2.8
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be open and
A\subset U
a closed analytic subset of complex codimension greater than one. Then every analytic function
f\colon U\setminus A\to\mathbb{C}
can be extended to an analytic function on
U
.

Any irreducible component
\ell\subseteq L
with codimension greater than one can therefore not arise as a singularity of
\mathcal{I}
.
Therefore, the variation of
\mathcal{I}
around such
\ell
is zero. In fact, this follows already from a merely topological fact, without any reference to a function: The fundamental groups

\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)\cong\pi_{1}(T\setminus\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}\ell)

of the complements of the Landau variety
L
, and its codimension one part, are canonically isomorphic. This is reviewed in
appendix

D
.

As a consequence, if
D
has simple normal crossings, we only need to consider critical sets of strata that arise from at most
(n+1)
-fold intersections
D^{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}
,
\lvert I\rvert\leq n+1
. All lower dimensional strata are entirely critical,
cS=S
, but their critical values
\pi(cS)
have codimension greater than one and therefore they do not contribute to
\mathfrak{L}
.

Remark 2.9
.

As defined, the Landau variety
L=L(\mathfrak{S})
depends on the choice of stratification. But local triviality of
D\rightarrow T
is an open condition in
T
, hence it holds outside the intersection
L_{\text{min}}=\bigcap_{\mathfrak{S}}L(\mathfrak{S})
over all possible Whitney stratifications
\mathfrak{S}
of
D
. In our setup, where
D\subset Y
are analytic, all such
\mathfrak{S}
are refinements of a unique minimal Whitney stratification
\mathfrak{S}_{\text{min}}
of
D

[
57
, Proposition 3.2 on p. 479]
; hence
L_{\text{min}}=L(\mathfrak{S}_{\text{min}})
gives a canonical definition of
the
Landau variety of
D
. Whenever
D
is transverse, the canonical stratification (
2.2
) is Whitney regular and minimal, hence it computes
L_{\text{min}}
.

2.4.
Integrals in fibres

Assume now that
D
is a union of complex hypersurfaces in
Y
, and furthermore that the irreducible components
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}
of
D
are partitioned into two finite sets
\mathfrak{A}=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r}\}
and
\mathfrak{B}=\{B_{1},\ldots,B_{s}\}
. In all our applications,
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
and
D\subset Y
will be
algebraic
, but this additional structure will play no role. In
section

1.2
, we considered integrals

\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\sigma_{t}}\omega_{t}

with
\omega
a holomorphic form on
Y\setminus A
of degree
n
, the complex dimension of the fibres
Y_{t}
. Then
\omega_{t}=\omega|_{Y_{t}\setminus A_{t}}
is a closed form, and furthermore zero when restricted to the subvariety
B_{t}
. Thus
[\omega_{t}]\in H^{n}_{\mathsf{dR}}(Y_{t}\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
defines a class in de Rham cohomology.
9
9

9

More generally, we could allow
\omega
that are neither holomorphic, nor of degree
n
. What matters is that
\omega|_{B}=0
,
\omega
is closed fibrewise (
\mathrm{d}(\omega_{t})=0
), and further that in a holomorphic chart
(x,t)\colon Y\supset U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+q}
with
t=\pi(x,t)
,
\omega
is smooth in
x
and holomorphic in
t
.

Our assumption of global holomorphicity of
\omega
imposes that the family of integrands
\omega_{t}
is single-valued; hence the analytic continuation of
\mathcal{I}(t)
is determined completely by the monodromy of the integration chain
\sigma_{t}
.

Remark 2.10
.

In every Whitney stratification
\mathfrak{S}
of
D=A\cup B
, the irreducible components
\mathfrak{D}
are in bijection with the strata
S\in\mathfrak{S}
of codimension one. Therefore, each
D_{i}=\overline{S}\in\mathfrak{D}
is a union of strata by
2.1
(2). It follows that the subvarieties
A\subseteq D
and
B\subseteq D
are also unions of strata.

Take a Whitney stratification
\mathfrak{S}
of
D
, with its Landau variety
L=L(\pi,\mathfrak{S})
. A continuous trivialization
\phi\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\cong Y_{t_{0}}\times U
over
t_{0}\in U\subseteq T\setminus L
from
2.5
preserves all strata. Since
A
and
B
are unions of strata,
\phi
restricts to trivializations of the subvariety
\pi^{-1}(U)\cap A\cong A_{t_{0}}\times U
and similarly for
B
. Hence the homeomorphism
\phi
restricts to a compatible pair

(2.5)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.63724pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-40.60773pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(Y\setminus A)\cap\pi^{-1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.01106pt\raise 6.53224pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71777pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi|_{Y\setminus A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.66675pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern A)\cap\pi^{-1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.99998pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 51.05707pt\raise-26.10777pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.71777pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi|_{B\setminus A}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 94.63724pt\raise-32.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(B_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}})\times U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 131.8133pt\raise-24.64001pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 131.8133pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

of trivializations of the two fibre bundles
(Y\setminus A)|_{\pi^{-1}(T\setminus L)}
and
(B\setminus A)|_{\pi^{-1}(T\setminus L)}
. This is what we mean by a
pair of fibre bundles
under
(L)
in the introduction
section

1.1
. Taking homology of this pair, it follows that also the groups
H_{\bullet}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
are locally trivial (a local system) over
T\setminus L
. Therefore, a relative cycle
\sigma_{t_{0}}
extends to a multivalued section
\sigma_{t}
and hence determines the integral
\mathcal{I}(t)
as a multivalued function on
T\setminus L
. The analyticity of
\mathcal{I}(t)
is shown in
[
12
, Theorem 56]
.

3.
Picard-Lefschetz theory

Throughout this section, we consider a proper holomorphic submersion
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
and a subset
D=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}\subset Y
that is the union of transverse smooth complex hypersurfaces
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}=\{A_{1},\ldots,A_{r},B_{1},\ldots,B_{s}\}
. We denote by
L\subset T
the Landau variety of the canonical stratification
\mathfrak{S}
of
D
.

Definition 3.1
.

A
small loop
is a closed curve
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow T\setminus L
such that there exists a holomorphic chart
t\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{k}
of
T
with
U\cap L=\{t_{1}=0\}
and
\gamma(\tau)=(e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau},0,\ldots,0)
is the counter-clockwise loop in the
t_{1}
-plane.
A
simple loop
is a loop which is homotopic to the conjugate
{\xi}^{-1}\star\eta\star\xi
of a small loop
\eta
by a path
\xi
(
fig.

3
).

Figure 3.
Simple loops around Landau components.

Note that simple loops become contractible under the inclusion
T\setminus L\rightarrow T
. In fact, simple loops generate the entire kernel of the corresponding surjection

(3.1)

\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(T,t_{0})

of fundamental groups of
T\setminus L
, for any base point
t_{0}\in T\setminus L
(see
appendix

D
).
Picard-Lefschetz theory determines the variation along small loops, but says nothing about loops that remain non-trivial in
\pi_{1}(T,t_{0})
.
10
10

10

For this reason,
[
5
]
and
[
51
]
assume that
T
is simply connected.

The latter emerge from the global topology of
T
, whereas the former arise from the degeneration of the fibres of
\pi
over a critical point
t_{c}\in L
, and are in this sense local in
T
.

Example 3.2
.

In
fig.

3
the loops
\xi_{1}^{-1}\star\eta\star\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}^{-1}\star\eta\star\xi_{2}
are two non-homotopic simple loops. The loop
\xi_{1}^{-1}\star\xi_{2}
is not simple, because it is non-trivial in the fundamental group of the torus
T\cong\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
denote the set of irreducible components
\ell\subseteq L
of complex codimension one in
T
.
Since we are only concerned with small loops, we can replace
T
with a disk
\mathbb{D}=\{\lvert t_{1}\rvert<2\}\subset\mathbb{C}
of complex dimension one in a suitable coordinate
t_{1}
. This disk embeds transverse to some component
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
such that
\mathbb{D}\cap L=\{t_{c}\}
is a single point of
\ell
. By
3.1
,
t_{c}
is a smooth point of
\ell
, so that, in particular,
t_{c}\notin\ell\cap\ell^{\prime}
for any other component
\ell\neq\ell^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
.

Since
\pi
is a proper submersion and hence
Y\rightarrow T
a fibre bundle, this localization in
T
also implies that we can assume that
Y\cong X\times T
is trivial as a smooth manifold, with
X
a smooth compact manifold and
\pi(x,t)=t
.

In fact, in this section we will only consider variations around Landau singularities
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
that emerge from fibrewise isolated critical points: We assume that over the point
t_{c}\in\ell
, the critical sets
cS\cap\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
consist of isolated points, for each stratum
S\in\mathfrak{S}
. In
section

3.2
we show that this leads to a further localization, this time in
X
, to neighbourhoods of precisely those isolated points in the critical fibre. After this second localization, we may assume that
Y\cong X\times T
, with
\pi(x,t)=t
, also as a complex manifold.

3.1.
Monodromy and variation

Let
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow T\setminus L
be a closed path based at
\gamma(0)=\gamma(1)=t_{0}
. Since
\pi|_{{\pi}^{-1}(T\setminus L)}
is a pair of fibre bundles (
2.5
), the pullback bundle
\gamma^{*}\pi
over the contractible interval
[0,1]
is trivial.
11
11

11

Glue together local trivializations over a sufficiently small subdivision of
\gamma
.
Hence one can find a continuous family
g_{\tau}\colon Y_{t_{0}}\rightarrow Y_{\gamma(\tau)}
of homeomorphisms, which furthermore restrict to homeomorphisms
g_{\tau}|_{S}\colon S_{t_{0}}\rightarrow S_{\gamma(\tau)}
of the fibres of every stratum
S\in\mathfrak{S}
of
A\cup B
. The induced homeomorphisms

(Y_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}},B_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}})\longrightarrow(Y_{\gamma(\tau)}\setminus A_{\gamma(\tau)},B_{\gamma(\tau)}\setminus A_{\gamma(\tau)})

of pairs are well-defined up to homotopy, and depend (up to homotopy) only on the homotopy class of
\gamma
.
12
12

12

For a homotopy
h\colon[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow T\setminus L
between paths
\gamma(t)=h(0,t)
and
\eta(t)=h(1,t)
, the pullback bundle
h^{*}\pi
on
[0,1]^{2}
is also trivial (
[0,1]^{2}
is simply connected). Any global trivialization of
h^{*}\pi
provides a homotopy between
\gamma^{*}\pi=h^{*}\pi|_{\{0\}\times[0,1]}
and
\eta^{*}\pi
.

Hence on the level of the homology groups

H_{\bullet}(t)=H_{\bullet}(Y_{t}\setminus A_{t},B_{t}),

the pushforward of
g_{\tau}
(restricted to
Y_{t_{0}}\setminus A_{t_{0}}
) is independent of the choice of the trivialization
g
of
\gamma^{*}\pi
. We denote this pushforward of
g_{1}
by

\gamma_{*}\colon H_{\bullet}(t_{0})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(t_{0}).

This map depends only on the homotopy class of
\gamma
. The assignment
\gamma\mapsto\gamma_{*}
defines an action of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})
on the homology group
H_{\bullet}(t_{0})
, called the
monodromy representation
.

Definition 3.3
.

The
variation
operators on the homology group of a fibre measure the difference between the monodromy and the identity:

(3.2)

\Var_{\gamma}\colon H_{\bullet}(t_{0})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(t_{0}),\quad h\longmapsto\gamma_{*}h-h.

Remark 3.4
.

If the fibres
Y_{t}=X
are constant (
Y=X\times T
), then a trivialization
g
of
\gamma^{*}\pi
provides an
ambient isotopy

g_{1}
of
(A\cup B)_{t_{0}}
in
X
that preserves each stratum individually.
An ambient isotopy of a subspace
M\subseteq X
is a homeomorphism
\phi\colon M\rightarrow M
that can be extended to a continuous family
(g_{\tau})_{0\leq\tau\leq 1}
of homeomorphisms
g_{\tau}\colon X\to X
, such that
g_{0}=\mathrm{id}
and
g_{1}|_{M}=\phi
.

Since we assume that
A\cup B
has at worst simple normal crossing singularities, we can in fact find an ambient isotopy in the
smooth
category (provided that the path
\gamma
is smooth). This follows from
2.6
, and means that the trivialization
g\colon X\times[0,1]\rightarrow X
can be chosen to be smooth and such that each
g_{\tau}\colon X\rightarrow X
is a diffeomorphism. Such an isotopy is also called a
diffeotopy

[
30
]
.

Example 3.5
.

Consider projective space
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
over the base
T=\mathbb{C}
, with
B=\varnothing
and remove
A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}
where
A_{1}=\{x=\infty\}
and
A_{2}=\{x^{2}=t\}
. All fibres
X\setminus A_{t}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\sqrt{t},-\sqrt{t}\}
over
t\neq 0
are isomorphic via
x\mapsto x/\sqrt{t}
to
X\setminus A_{t=1}=\mathbb{C}\setminus\{1,-1\}
. This trivialization lifts the loop
t=\gamma(\tau)=e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
around the Landau variety
L=\{0\}
to the diffeotopy
g_{\tau}(x)=x\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
. The latter ends in the antipode
g_{1}(x)=-x
, which swaps the punctures
\pm 1
in the fibre over
t_{0}=1
. Let
\sigma_{\pm}
be two loops around
\pm 1
. They generate
H_{1}(t_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and the monodromy
\gamma_{*}
swaps them,
\gamma_{*}(\sigma_{\pm})=\sigma_{\mp}
, see
fig.

4
. Hence, the variation is

\Var_{\gamma}(\sigma_{+})=-\Var_{\gamma}(\sigma_{-})=\sigma_{-}-\sigma_{+}.

\tau=0
\scriptstyle{-1}
\scriptstyle{+1}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma_{+}}
0<\tau<1
\scriptstyle{e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}}
\scriptstyle{e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\tau}^{*}\sigma_{+}}
\scriptstyle{g_{\tau}^{*}\sigma_{-}}
\tau=1
\scriptstyle{e^{-\mathrm{i}\pi}}
\scriptstyle{e^{\mathrm{i}\pi}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}^{*}\sigma_{+}}
\scriptstyle{g_{1}^{*}\sigma_{-}}

Figure 4.
Isotopy for
A=\{x^{2}=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}
by rotation swaps the loops
\sigma_{\pm}
(
3.5
).

Example 3.6
.

Consider
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}
,
A=\{x=t,\infty\}
and
B=\{x=0,2\}
over the loop
t=\gamma(\tau)=e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
(see
fig.

9
). The trivialization
(X\setminus A,B)_{\gamma(\tau)}\cong(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{1\},\{0,2\})
given by
g_{\tau}(x)=xe^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
yields the isotopy
g_{\gamma}(x)=x
. Let
t_{0}=1
. Choose a path
\sigma
in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{1\}
from
0
to
2
, and let
\delta\{1\}
as above. Then
H(t_{0})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is generated by (the classes of)
\sigma
and
\delta\{1\}
, and the monodromy
\gamma_{*}
maps
\delta\{1\}
to itself and
\sigma
to
\sigma-\delta\{1\}
. Thus,

\Var_{\gamma}(\delta\{1\})=0,\qquad\Var_{\gamma}(\sigma)=-\delta\{1\}.

3.2.
Localization

A crucial property of the variation operators is that both their domain and codomain can be localized near the critical points over
\ell
.

In order to define a local variation operation, we need to show that if we are able to localize the critical sets
cS
of all strata inside an open submanifold
W\subseteq X
, then the isotopy
g_{\gamma}
can be chosen to be the identity outside
W
. Note that for fibrewise isolated critical points such a
W
always exists by Milnor’s theorem
[
43
]
—take for instance the union of small open balls around each critical point. If
\cup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}cS
is not isolated (fibrewise), but still smooth, then we can construct
W
by taking small tubular neighbourhoods of the critical sets in a fibre.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
W
be an open submanifold of
X
such that
\partial W
is smooth and transverse to
A\cup B
, and such that
W
contains the critical sets of all strata of
A_{t}\cup B_{t}
, for all
t
in a small disk near
t_{c}\in L
. Then the ambient isotopy
g_{\gamma}\colon(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})\to(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
is homotopic to a map that is the identity outside of
W
.

Proof.

This is a special instance of the
isotopy extension theorem

[
30
, §8]
.

Let
C
denote the union of all critical sets
cS
in the fiber over
t_{c}\in L
. Using a collar neighbourhood of
\partial W
we can construct a closed submanifold
W^{\prime}\subset W
that contains
C
and whose boundary
\partial W^{\prime}
is also transverse to all strata of
A\cup B
. This transversality implies that adding
\partial W
and
\partial W^{\prime}
to the arrangement
A\cup B
does not alter the associated Landau variety. From
2.5
and
2.6
we obtain thus a smooth stratum preserving trivialization
h
that gives rise to (see
3.4
) a smooth ambient isotopy
g_{\gamma}\colon(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})\to(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
. The crucial point here is that this map fixes
\partial W^{\prime}
(globally).

Let
g_{\tau}
be a realization of
g_{\gamma}
, that is, a family of (stratum preserving) diffeomorphisms
g_{\tau}\colon X\to X
and subsets
S_{\tau}\subseteq X
with
S_{0}=S_{1}=A_{t}\cup B_{t}\cup\partial W\cup\partial W^{\prime}
, such that
g_{0}=\id_{X}
,
g_{1}=g_{\gamma}
and
g_{\tau}|_{S_{0}}\colon S_{0}\cong S_{\tau}
. The
track
of
g
is the diffeomorphism

G\colon I\times X\longrightarrow I\times X,\quad(\tau,x)\longmapsto(\tau,g_{\tau}(x)).

Let
V
be the
velocity field
of
G
, the vector field on
I\times X
, tangential to the curves
\tau\mapsto(\tau,g_{\tau}(x))
. Identifying
T(\mathbb{R}\times X)
with
T(\mathbb{R})\oplus T(X)
it has the form
V(\tau,x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+v(\tau,x)
where
v(\tau,x)
is a vector field on
X
.
In this way every ambient isotopy gives rise to a time-dependent vector field on
X
. The reverse statement is also true if the time-dependent vector field is compactly supported. In our case
X
is compact, so this condition is automatically satisfied.
Moreover, observe that
V
is tangential to the set
S=\{(\tau,x)\mid x\in S_{\tau}\}\subseteq I\times X
, hence its flow (which is
G
) leaves
S
invariant.

Since
C
is contained in
W^{\prime}
, the ambient isotopy
g_{\gamma}
will be homotopic to the identity map on its complement
X\setminus W^{\prime}
. Let
V_{\lambda}(\tau,x)
be the associated homotopy of vector fields on
I\times(X\setminus W^{\prime})
(between
V_{0}(\tau,x)=V(\tau,x)|_{x\in X\setminus W^{\prime}}
and the constant vector field
V_{1}(\tau,x)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}
, whose flow induces the identity map).
Choose a partition of unity
\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}
, subordinate to the open cover
U_{1}\cup U_{2}=X
with
U_{1}=W
and
U_{2}=X\setminus W^{\prime}
, and define a vector field
F_{\lambda}
on
I\times X
by
F_{\lambda}(\tau,x)=\rho_{1}(x)V(\tau,x)+\rho_{2}(x)V_{\lambda}(\tau,x)
. Let
f_{\lambda}
be the ambient isotopy induced by
F_{\lambda}
. Then

•

f_{\lambda}|_{S_{0}}\colon S_{0}\cong S_{1}
for each
\lambda\in[0,1]
,

•

f_{\lambda}
is a homotopy between
f_{1}
and
f_{0}=g_{\gamma}
,

•

f_{1}
is equal to
g_{\gamma}
on
W^{\prime}
,

•

f_{1}
is the identity outside of
W
.

∎

Example 3.8
.

The isotopy
g_{\gamma}(x)=-x
from
3.5
is not the identity outside of
W=\{\lvert x\rvert<1\}\subset\mathbb{C}
. Pick any smooth function
\rho\colon\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
with
\rho(r)=1
for
r\geq 1
and
\rho(r)=0
for
r\leq 1/2
. Then the diffeomorphisms
\phi_{\tau}(x)=x\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau\rho(\lvert x\rvert)}
restrict to
x\mapsto x\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
outside
W
, and to the identity inside the smaller ball
W^{\prime}=\{\lvert x\rvert<1/2\}
. In particular,
\phi_{\tau}
fixes the stratum
S=\{x^{2}=t\}\subseteq W^{\prime}
and thereby defines a diffeotopy of the pair
(\mathbb{C},S_{t})
. This family
\phi_{\tau}
is smooth in
\tau
, so
\phi_{1}
is homotopic to
\phi_{0}=\id
. It follows that
g_{\gamma}\simeq f
is homotopic the composition
f(x)=\phi_{1}g_{\gamma}(x)=x\cdot e^{\mathrm{i}\pi(1-\rho(\lvert x\rvert))}
which has the desired property that
f|_{W^{\mathsf{c}}}=\id
(
fig.

5
).

\scriptstyle{f=-\id}
\scriptstyle{x_{1}}
\scriptstyle{x_{2}}
\scriptstyle{x_{3}}
\scriptstyle{W^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{W}
\scriptstyle{f=\id}
\scriptstyle{f(x_{1})}
\scriptstyle{f(x_{2})}
\scriptstyle{f(x_{3})}

Figure 5.
3.8
of an ambient isotopy
f=\phi_{1}g_{\gamma}
that is the identity outside of
W
and homotopic to
g_{\gamma}(x)=-x
. Here
S=\{x^{2}=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}
and
\phi_{1}|_{W^{\prime}}=\id
,
\phi_{1}|_{W^{\mathsf{c}}}=-\id
.

By
3.7
, the variation of a class
h\in H_{\bullet}(t_{0})
can be represented by a cycle contained in
W\setminus A_{t_{0}}
with boundary in
W\cap B_{t_{0}}
, and furthermore this variation
\Var_{\gamma}h
depends only on how
h
intersects the closed ball
\overline{W}
.
To make this precise, let
W^{\mathsf{c}}=X\setminus W
denote the complement of the ball and consider the following map, induced by an inclusion of pairs:

(3.3)

H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\setminus A_{t},\partial W\cup B_{t})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},W^{\mathsf{c}}\cup B_{t}).

Lemma 3.9
.

The map (
3.3
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Extend the inclusion
\partial W\hookrightarrow W^{\mathsf{c}}
of the boundary to some collar neighbourhood
u\colon\partial W\times[0,1)\cong U\subset W^{\mathsf{c}}
.

Figure 6.
Collar neighbourhood
U\cong[0,1)\times\partial W\subset W^{\mathsf{c}}
used for the excision argument in the proof of
3.9
.

Adding the collar
U
, we can thicken
W
into
W^{\prime}=W\cup U
and excise
W^{\mathsf{c}}\setminus(W^{\prime}\cup A_{t})
from the pair
(X\setminus A_{t},W^{\mathsf{c}}\cup B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
, to replace it with the pair
(W^{\prime}\setminus A_{t},U\cup B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
; see
fig.

6
. Since
\partial W\cup A_{t}\cup B_{t}
intersect transversely, we can choose
u
in such a way that it preserves
A
and
B
(see
appendix

B
). For the corresponding projection
r\colon U\rightarrow\partial W
, this means
r(U\cap A_{t})=\partial W\cap A_{t}
and
r(U\cap B_{t})=\partial W\cap B_{t}
. Note that
r
fixes
\partial W
pointwise, so we can extend
r
to a map
r\colon W^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{W}
by setting
r|_{W}=\id_{W}
and this still preserves
A_{t}
and
B_{t}
. This
r
descends to deformation retractions
(U\cup B_{t})\setminus A_{t}\rightarrow(\partial W\cup B_{t})\setminus A_{t}
and
W^{\prime}\setminus A_{t}\rightarrow\overline{W}\setminus A_{t}
.
∎

Definition 3.10
.

The
W
-trace
or
trace-in-the-ball

[
21
]
is the homomorphism

(3.4)

W_{*}\colon H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\setminus A_{t},\partial W\cup B_{t})

given by the map of pairs
H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t})\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},W^{\mathsf{c}}\cup B_{t})
and the isomorphism (
3.3
). We further write

\iota\colon(W\setminus A_{t},W\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t})\rightarrow(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
for the natural inclusion of pairs, and denote its push-forward
\iota_{*}
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose that
g_{\gamma}
is the identity map outside of
W
. Then
g_{\gamma}
canonically determines a homomorphism
13
13

13

Unlike
\Var_{\gamma}
, the map (
3.5
) may depend on the choice of
g_{\gamma}
(not just
\gamma
alone): if two realizations
g_{\gamma}
and
g^{\prime}_{\gamma}
with
g_{\gamma}|_{W^{\mathsf{c}}}=g_{\gamma}^{\prime}|_{W^{\mathsf{c}}}=\id
are homotopic, but the homotopy does not fix
W^{\mathsf{c}}
, then the corresponding variations
\var_{\gamma}
could differ by elements in the kernel of
\iota_{*}
.

(3.5)

\var_{\gamma}\colon H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\setminus A_{t},\partial W\cup B_{t})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(W\setminus A_{t},B_{t})

such that the following diagram for

commutes:

Proof.

The triple

B_{t}\setminus A_{t}\subset(B_{t}\cup W^{\mathsf{c}})\setminus A_{t}\subset X\setminus A_{t}

gives a long exact sequence that identifies the kernel of
W_{*}
with the image of

j_{*}\colon H_{\bullet}((B_{t}\cup W^{\mathsf{c}})\setminus A_{t},B_{t})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t}).

Analogously to the proof of
3.9
, we can excise all of
\overline{W}\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t}
from the domain of
j_{*}
, except for a layer
U\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t}\cong[0,1)\times(\partial W\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t})
in a collar neighbourhood of
\partial W
inside
W
(
fig.

7
).

Figure 7.
Collar neighbourhood
U\cong[0,1)\times\partial W\subset\overline{W}
used for the excision argument in the proof of
3.11
.

The deformation retraction

U\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t}\longrightarrow\partial W\cap B_{t}\setminus A_{t}

then identifies the domain of
j_{*}
with
H_{\bullet}(W^{\mathsf{c}}\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
. Every
h\in\ker W_{*}
can thus be written as
h=j_{*}\sigma
where
\sigma\in H_{\bullet}(W^{\mathsf{c}}\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
has support inside
W^{\mathsf{c}}
. This has variation

\Var_{\gamma}h=(g_{\gamma*}-\id)j_{*}\sigma=j_{*}(g_{\gamma}|_{W^{\mathsf{c}}\setminus A_{t}}-\id)_{*}\sigma=j_{*}0\sigma=0,

and we conclude that indeed,
\Var_{\gamma}
factors through
W_{*}
. Now consider again the thicker
W^{\prime}\supset W
from
3.9
. Since
X=W^{\prime}\cup(X\setminus\overline{W})
is an open cover, we can represent every
h\in H_{\bullet}(X\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
by a chain
h=x+y
with
x
and
y
supported in
W^{\prime}\setminus A_{t}
and
X\setminus(\overline{W}\cup A_{t})
, respectively. Since
g_{\gamma*}y=y
, we see that
\Var_{\gamma}h=g_{\gamma*}x-x
takes values in
H_{\bullet}(W^{\prime}\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
. But
H_{\bullet}(W\setminus A_{t},B_{t})\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(W^{\prime}\setminus A_{t},B_{t})
is an isomorphism, because the deformation retracts
W^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{W}\rightarrow\overline{W}\setminus U\subseteq W
(from
3.9
and the above collar
U
inside
W
) are compatible with
A_{t}
and
B_{t}
. This proves the factorization of
\Var_{\gamma}
through
\iota_{*}
.
∎

Here it is important to note that
\var_{\gamma}
is not simply a localized version of
\Var_{\gamma}
; it does not carry the same information. The crucial point is that the latter
factorizes
by means of
W_{*}
and
\var_{\gamma}
, not that local and global variations are necessarily equal. See
3.5
/
fig.

8
and
3.6
/
fig.

9
for examples where the two are indeed different. These highlight a general fact that holds for the
simple pinch singularities
that we consider in the next section: For linear pinches the variation always vanishes in
H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\setminus A,\partial W\cup B)
, for quadratic pinches the variation vanishes if
n-m
is odd.
