Remark 3.12
.

All of the above remains valid in the special cases
A=\varnothing
or
B=\varnothing
. Furthermore, it holds also if we replace
X\setminus A_{t_{0}}
by other subsets of
X
such as intersections, unions and complements of elements of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
.

3.3.
Simple pinch singularities

The Picard-Lefschetz formulas from
[
21
,
51
]
apply only where the degeneration of
A\cup B
over
L
is sufficiently benign. They require that the neighbourhoods of the critical points can be modelled by hypersurface arrangements of the following types (we comment on generalizations in
section

3.9
).

Definition 3.13
.

Consider a critical point
p\in cS
of the stratified map
\pi
, on some stratum
S\subseteq D_{1}\cap\ldots\cap D_{m}
with complex codimension
m\leq n+1
. Then
p
is called a
linear
(
m=n+1
) or
quadratic
(
m\leq n
)
simple pinch
14
14

14

This is the terminology from
[
51
]
. In
[
21
]
, a simple pinch is called
zero pinch
.
if there exist holomorphic local coordinates
t=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{q})
at
\pi(p)\in T
and
y=(x,t)\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+q}
at
p\in U\subseteq Y
, trivializing
\pi(x,t)=t
, such that the hypersurfaces
D_{i}\cap U\cong S_{i}\cap y(U)
take the form

(3.7)

\begin{split}S_{i}&=\{x_{i}=0\},\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,m-1,\\
S_{m}&=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1}+x_{m}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2}=t_{1}\}.\end{split}

These hypersurfaces
\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\}
are smooth and intersect transversely.
There are no critical points
c(S^{I})=\varnothing
on the intersections
S^{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}S_{i}
of a proper subset
I\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
.
The only critical stratum is the intersection of all
m
hypersurfaces. Its critical set

(
\sharp
)

c(S^{1,\ldots,m})=\{x=0\}\cap\{t_{1}=0\}=\{(0,\ldots,0,t_{2},\ldots,t_{q})\}

is smooth and projects isomorphically onto the Landau variety
\{t_{1}=0\}
; each fibre with
t_{1}=0
has a unique critical point with coordinates
(0,t)
.

For a linear simple pinch, the fibres of
S^{1,\ldots,n+1}=cS^{1,\ldots,n+1}
are empty over
t_{1}\neq 0
, and consist of a single point (the critical point) over
t_{1}=0
.

For a quadratic simple pinch, each fibre of the stratum
S^{1,\ldots,m}
, over
t
with
t_{1}\neq 0
, contains a real sphere

\mathbb{S}^{n-m}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{n-m+1}\colon\lVert u\rVert=1\},

embedded as
u\mapsto(0,\ldots,0,u\cdot\sqrt{t_{1}},t)
. As
t_{1}\rightarrow 0
, these spheres shrink and eventually collapse, over
t_{1}=0
, to a point (the critical point). They are therefore called
vanishing spheres
.

Definition 3.14
.

A
simple critical value
is a point
t\in L
such that all critical points in the fibre
\pi^{-1}(t)
are linear or quadratic simple pinches.

A
simple pinch component
of
L
is a component
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
such that a generic point
t\in\ell
is simple.

Suppose
t_{c}\in L
is a simple critical value, and denote
P=\pi^{-1}(t_{c})\cap\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}cS
the corresponding simple pinches. Every
p\in P
is an isolated critical point in the compact fibre
Y_{t_{c}}
, hence
P
is finite. Pick a chart
y_{p}\colon U_{p}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{n+q}
of the form (
3.7
) for each
p
, centred at
y_{p}(p)=(0,0)
. By rescaling these coordinates, and shrinking the neighbourhoods
U_{p}
, we can ensure that (
fig.

10
):

•

U_{p}
and
U_{p^{\prime}}
are disjoint when
p\neq p^{\prime}
,

•

the chart
y_{p}(U_{p})
contains the closure
\overline{W}\times\{0\}
of the open ball

W=\{\lVert x\rVert^{2}=\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{2}+\ldots+\lvert x_{n}\rvert^{2}<1\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}.

•

the only hypersurfaces
D_{i}\in\mathfrak{D}
that intersect
U_{p}
are those that contain
p
(that is, those that correspond to the
S_{i}
).

Figure 10.
Global and fibrewise localization.

Consider now the corresponding open balls
W_{p}=y_{p}^{-1}(W\times\{0\})\subset Y_{t_{c}}
in the fibre over
t_{c}
. Observe that the unit sphere
\partial W=\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
lies transverse to the arrangement (
3.7
). It follows that the closure of the disjoint union

W_{P}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigsqcup_{p\in P}W_{p}\;\subset\;Y_{t_{c}}

embeds as a compact submanifold, whose boundary intersects
\mathfrak{D}
transversely. Furthermore,
W_{P}\supset P
contains all critical points over
t_{c}
. In fact, since
P
is finite and the simple pinches (

♯

‣
3.3
) depend continuously on
t
, we conclude that every local trivialization
\pi^{-1}(V)\cong X\times V
of
Y
at
t_{c}\in V\subseteq T
can be restricted to an open
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
, still containing
t_{c}
, such that
W_{P}\times V^{\prime}
contains all critical points over
V^{\prime}
(
fig.

10
).

Corollary 3.15
.

Let
t_{c}\in L
a simple critical value, and
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})
a class that can be represented by a small loop near
t_{c}
. Then the variation along
\gamma
decomposes into a sum

(3.8)

\Var_{\gamma}=\sum_{p\in P}\Var_{p}\quad\text{where}\quad\Var_{p}=\iota_{p*}\var_{p}W_{p*}.

Proof.

Apply
3.7
to
W_{P}
to find an ambient isotopy
g_{\gamma}
of
D_{t_{0}}
inside
X
, so that
g_{\gamma}
is the identity outside
W_{P}
. By continuity,
g_{\gamma}(W_{p})=W_{p}
maps each ball to itself. Therefore, in the splitting

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{p\in P}H_{\bullet}(\overline{W_{p}}\setminus A,\partial W_{p}\cup B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(\overline{W_{P}}\setminus A,\partial W_{P}\cup B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\var_{\gamma}=\id-g_{\gamma*}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{p\in P}H_{\bullet}(W_{p}\setminus A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(W_{P}\setminus A,B)}

from the disjoint union
\overline{W_{P}}=\bigsqcup_{p}\overline{W_{p}}
, the map
\var_{\gamma}
from
3.11
, viewed as a matrix (arrow on the left), is diagonal:
\var_{\gamma}=\bigoplus_{p}\var_{p}
. The entry
\var_{p}
is the restriction of
\id-g_{\gamma*}
to
W_{p}
and thus equal to the factorization of the variation
\Var_{p}=\id-g_{p*}
induced by the homeomorphism
g_{p}\colon X\rightarrow X
defined by
g_{p}(x)=g_{\gamma}(x)
for
x\in W_{p}
and
g_{p}(x)=x
otherwise.
∎

For a simple pinch
p\in cS
on a stratum
S
of codimension
m
, let
J\subseteq\mathfrak{A}
and
K\subseteq\mathfrak{B}
denote the
m=\lvert J\rvert+\lvert K\rvert
hypersurfaces in
A
and
B
, respectively, that contain the pinch:
p\in D^{J\sqcup K}
. We chose
W_{p}
so small that the pairs

\displaystyle(W_{p}\setminus A,B)

\displaystyle=(W_{p}\setminus A_{J},B_{K})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle(\overline{W_{p}}\setminus A,\partial W_{p}\cup B)

\displaystyle=(\overline{W_{p}}\setminus A_{J},\partial W_{p}\cup B_{K})

only meet precisely those hypersurfaces.
15
15

15

Here we write, as for the homology groups, simply
(W\setminus A,B)
for the pair
(W\setminus A,W\cap B\setminus A)
etc. (the second element of a pair is always to be viewed as a subspace of the first).
We have thus reduced the computation of the variation
\Var_{\gamma}
, at least for small loops around Landau singularities
\ell\subseteq L
with a simple critical value
t_{c}\in\ell
, to a calculation of
\var_{p}
for the arrangement (
3.7
) within the unit ball
W\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The coordinates
y_{p}
translate
J\sqcup K
into a bipartition of
\{S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\}
.

3.4.
Leray’s residue and (co)boundary maps

To state the relative Picard-Lefschetz theorem, we employ two operations on relative homology groups: the
partial boundary

\partial_{i}
and the
relative coboundary

\delta_{i}
. These were constructed by Leray in
[
39
]
and a review is provided in
appendix

B
.

In summary, we assume that
\mathfrak{D}=(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots)
is a finite transverse family of smooth complex hypersurfaces
D_{i}\subset X
inside a complex manifold
X
. For any index set
I
, we denote the corresponding union and intersection by

D_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}D_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad D^{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}D_{i}.

Then for disjoint index sets
I\cap J=\varnothing
, and any
i\in J
, Leray’s partial boundary comes from the usual boundary map in relative homology, but only keeps the piece that lies in
D_{i}\subseteq D_{J}
. This partial boundary is denoted

\partial_{i}\colon H_{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(D_{i}\setminus D_{I},D_{J-i})

and it fits into a long exact sequence of the form

(3.9)

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J-i})\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})\xrightarrow{\partial_{i}}H_{\bullet-1}(D_{i}\setminus D_{I},D_{J-i})\rightarrow\cdots

where the other two maps are the natural inclusions of pairs.

Leray’s relative coboundary takes a chain in
D_{i}
(for some
i\in I
) and fibres it in circles through a tubular neighbourhood around
D_{i}
. This yields a homomorphism

\delta_{i}\colon H_{\bullet}(D_{i}\setminus D_{I-i},D_{J})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet+1}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})

which also fits into a long exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I-i},D_{J})\xrightarrow{\varpi_{i}}H_{\bullet-2}(D_{i}\setminus D_{I-i},D_{J})\xrightarrow{\delta_{i}}H_{\bullet-1}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})\rightarrow\cdots

where the map
\varpi_{i}
intersects a transverse chain with
D_{i}
.
For
multiple
indices
K=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{r}\}\subseteq J
, we denote the corresponding
iterated
boundaries and coboundaries as

\displaystyle\partial_{K}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i_{1}}\!\cdots\partial_{i_{r}}\colon H_{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet-r}(D^{K}\setminus D_{I},D_{J-K})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\delta_{K}

\displaystyle=\delta_{i_{1}}\!\cdots\delta_{i_{r}}\colon H_{\bullet}(D^{K}\setminus D_{I},D_{J-K})\longrightarrow H_{\bullet+r}(X\setminus D_{I+K},D_{J-K}).

The ordering of these compositions only affects the overall sign, since for
i\neq j
, the maps
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}=-\partial_{j}\partial_{i}
and
\delta_{i}\delta_{j}=-\delta_{j}\delta_{i}
and
\partial_{i}\delta_{j}=-\delta_{j}\partial_{i}
anticommute.

Remark 3.16
.

On the level of differential forms (de Rham cohomology), the Leray coboundary is dual to Leray’s residue map
16
16

16

In the category of mixed Hodge structures, a Tate twist factor
(-1)=H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{\times})=\mathbb{C}
appears on the right, to account for the weight of the factor
2\mathrm{i}\pi
in (
3.10
).

\Res_{i}\colon H^{\bullet}_{\mathsf{dR}}(X\setminus D_{I},D_{J})\longrightarrow H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{\bullet-1}(D_{i}\setminus D_{I-i},D_{J}).

The latter generalizes the Cauchy residue of a function: for any chain
\sigma
in
D_{i}\setminus D_{I-i}
with boundary in
D_{J}
, and any closed smooth form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X\setminus D_{I})
that vanishes on
D_{J}
, their (co)homology classes
[\sigma]
and
[\omega]
integrate to

(3.10)

\int_{\delta_{i}[\sigma]}[\omega]=2\mathrm{i}\pi\cdot\int_{[\sigma]}\Res_{i}[\omega].

3.5.
Vanishing chains

The Picard-Lefschetz theorem describes the variation
\Var_{p}
at a linear or quadratic simple pinch, in terms of certain homology classes of the arrangement (
3.7
). The example in
section

1.8.1
illustrates these classes in dimension
n=1
; illuminating figures for higher dimensions can be found in
[
51
, Fig. V.2]
and
[
61
, Figs. I.14–16]
.

From now on we employ the localization discussed in the context of small and simple loops (
3.1
), that is, we assume
T=\mathbb{C}
and
\ell=\{0\}
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
p\in cS
denote a linear or quadratic simple pinch on a stratum
S\subseteq A^{J}\cap B^{K}
of codimension
m
. Let
A_{j}\in\mathfrak{A}
(
j\in J
) and
B_{k}\in\mathfrak{B}
(
k\in K
) denote the hypersurfaces containing
S
. Recall that, in coordinates of the form (
3.7
), these define a bipartition
J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Let
0<t<1
.

(1)

The
vanishing cell

\zeta\in H_{n}(W_{p},A\cup B)_{t}\cong H_{n}(W,S_{1,\ldots,m})_{t}
is the class of the compact region in
real
coordinates
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, bounded by all
m
hypersurfaces
S_{i}
, in the fibre over
t
:

\quad\quad\quad\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1}\geq 0\}\cap\{x_{m}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2}\leq t-x_{1}-\ldots-x_{m-1}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(2)

The
vanishing cycle

\nu\in H_{n}(W_{p}\setminus A,B)_{t}\cong H_{n}(W\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}
is obtained from the vanishing cell by taking first the boundary in, and then the Leray coboundary around, each hypersurface
A_{j}
:

(3.11)

\nu=\Big(\prod_{j\in J}\delta_{A_{j}}\partial_{A_{j}}\Big)\>\zeta.

(3)

The
dual vanishing cycle

\widetilde{\nu}\in H_{n}(W_{p}\setminus B,A)_{t}\cong H_{n}(W\setminus S_{K},S_{J})_{t}
is obtained in the same way, but swapping the roles of
A
and
B
:
17
17

17

The sign
(-1)^{\lvert K\rvert}
is included here to simplify the signs in the Picard-Lefschetz theorem.

\widetilde{\nu}=(-1)^{\lvert K\rvert}\cdot\Big(\prod_{k\in K}\delta_{B_{k}}\partial_{B_{k}}\Big)\>\zeta.

(4)

Their images under the inclusion
\iota_{p}\colon W_{p}\hookrightarrow Y_{t}
are denoted

\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\nu_{p}=\iota_{p*}\nu\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\nu}_{p}=\iota_{p*}\widetilde{\nu}\in H_{n}(Y\setminus B,A)_{t}.

Remark 3.18
.

The operators
\delta_{j}\partial_{j}
in (
3.11
) are even, hence
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}
depend only on the orientation of
\zeta
, but not on any implicit ordering of
J
or
K
. In fact, for
any
ordering
J=\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{r}\}
, we have
\nu=(-1)^{\lvert J\rvert(\lvert J\rvert-1)/2}\cdot\delta_{J}\partial_{J}\zeta
in terms of
\delta_{J}=\delta_{j_{1}}\!\cdots\delta_{j_{r}}
and
\partial_{J}=\partial_{j_{1}}\!\cdots\partial_{j_{r}}
.

The vanishing cell
\zeta
varies continuously with
t\in(0,1)
and it literally vanishes as
t\rightarrow 0
; hence the name.
For a linear pinch (
m=n+1
),
\zeta
is the simplex bounded by
x_{i}\geq 0
(from
S_{i}
for
i\leq n
) and
x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}\leq t
from

S_{n+1}=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n}=t\}.

For a quadratic pinch (
m\leq n
),
\zeta
is a family of
n-m+1
dimensional disks (coordinates
x_{m},\ldots,x_{n}
) over an
m-1
dimensional simplex
\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1}\leq t,x_{i}\geq 0\}
, with the disks collapsing to a point over
x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1}=t
. The maximal boundary of this cell,

\partial_{1,\ldots,m}\zeta=\pm[\mathbb{S}^{n-m}]\in H_{n-m}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m}_{t}),

is the fundamental class of the
vanishing sphere
. This name refers to the embedded spheres
\mathbb{S}^{n-m}\hookrightarrow S^{1,\ldots,m}_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
u\mapsto(0,\ldots,0,u\cdot\sqrt{t})
.
See
[
51
, Fig. V.2]
and
[
61
, Figs. I.14–16]
for figures of these vanishing chains for
n\leq 3
and
m\leq 4
.

Remark 3.19
.

We treat the zero-dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{1,-1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
as an oriented manifold with the orientation induced from the interval
(-1,1)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. So its fundamental class is
[\mathbb{S}^{0}]=[\{1\}]-[\{-1\}]\in H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
.

The vanishing cell
\zeta
is defined only up to a sign (orientation). There is no canonical choice, because the natural orientations on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
are not compatible between different parametrizations (
3.7
); e.g. swap
x_{1}\leftrightarrow x_{2}
. This sign is the only ambiguity, because the vanishing cell is a generator of the cyclic group

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle\in H_{n}(W_{p},A\cup B)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}.

This isomorphism is reviewed in
3.29
. The (dual) vanishing cycles
\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}
also depend on the orientation of
\zeta
, but nothing more (
3.18
).
Hence the sign ambiguity cancels in the quadratic expression
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\nu
in the Picard-Lefschetz formula.

Lemma 3.20
.

The monodromy of the vanishing cell of a simple pinch, along a simple loop
\gamma(\tau)=t\cdot e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
based at
t\in(0,1)
, is

\gamma_{*}\zeta=(-1)^{n-m+1}\cdot\zeta.

Proof.

The trivialization
\phi_{\tau}(x)=(x_{1}e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau},\ldots,x_{m-1}e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau},x_{m}e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau},\ldots,x_{n}e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau})
identifies the fibres over
\gamma
. It lifts the vanishing cell to a section
\phi_{\tau*}(\zeta)\in H_{n}(W,S_{1,\ldots,m})_{\gamma(\tau)}
.
After one revolution,
\phi_{1*}
acts by
(-1)^{n-m+1}
on the orientation
\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{n}
, because
\phi_{1}(x)=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{m-1},-x_{m},\ldots,-x_{n})
.
∎

This simple computation determines the variation of the vanishing cycles, because variation commutes with (co)boundary maps:
18
18

18

This follows from (
B.6
) in
section

B.3
, because
\Var_{\gamma}=g_{\gamma*}-\id
is determined by a push-forward under a (stratified) diffeomorphism
g_{\gamma}
.

(3.12)

\Var_{\gamma}\nu_{p}=\left[(-1)^{n-m+1}-1\right]\cdot\nu_{p}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $n-m$ odd, and}\\
-2\nu_{p}&\text{if $n-m$ even.}\\
\end{cases}

Beware this does not imply that
\Var_{\gamma}=0
for
n-m
odd. Consider the map of pairs
(W_{p}\setminus A,B)\rightarrow(\overline{W}_{p}\setminus A,\partial W_{p}\cup B)
, which induces

(3.13)

W_{*}\iota_{*}\colon H_{n}(W_{p}\setminus A,B)\longrightarrow H_{n}(\overline{W}_{p}\setminus A,\partial W_{p}\cup B).

We will see later that, for odd
n-m\geq 1
, this map is zero. Hence
\Var_{\gamma}\nu_{p}=0
simply reflects the fact that the
W
-trace of
\nu_{p}
is zero; we learn nothing about
\var_{p}
in (
3.8
). The linear pinch
n-m=-1
is more subtle.

3.6.
Picard-Lefschetz theorem

Several variants of the Picard-Lefschetz theorem can be found for example in
[
21
,
35
,
34
]
.
The following relative version for hypersurface arrangements
D=A\cup B
is stated in
[
51
]
, albeit without a complete proof, and with an inaccurate interpretation for linear pinches.

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
p\in D
be a linear or quadratic simple pinch over a smooth point
\pi(p)\in L
of the Landau variety.
Then the local variation

\var_{p}\colon H_{d}(\overline{W}_{p}\setminus A,\partial W_{p}\cup B)_{t}\longrightarrow H_{d}(W_{p}\setminus A,B)_{t}

can be expressed in terms of the vanishing cycles
\nu\in H_{n}(W_{p}\setminus A,B)_{t}
and
\widetilde{\nu}\in H_{n}(W_{p}\setminus B,A)_{t}
from
3.17
:

•

In degree
d\neq n
,
\var_{p}=0
.

•

In degree
d=n
, there exist integers
N(h)\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

(3.14)

\var_{p}(h)=N(h)\cdot\nu.

•

This identity holds for the intersection numbers

(3.15)

N(h)=(-1)^{(n+1)(n+2)/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle.

Remark 3.22
.

The theorem covers linear and quadratic pinches simultaneously, but they behave slightly differently. At a quadratic pinch, the variation map
\var_{p}
is always non-zero. At a linear pinch
p
, we distinguish three cases:

•

If
p\in A\setminus B
(“
A
type”), then
\nu_{p}=0
and thus
\var_{p}=0
.

•

If
p\in B\setminus A
(“
B
type”), then
\widetilde{\nu}_{p}=0
and thus
\var_{p}=0
.

•

If
p\in A\cap B
(“mixed type”), then
\var_{p}\neq 0
.

At an
A
-type linear pinch, (
3.14
) holds for
any
choice of
N(h)
. For all other simple pinches, the integer
N(h)
in (
3.15
) is the
unique
solution to (
3.14
).

For a trace
h=W_{p*}\,\sigma
of some cycle
\sigma\in H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
, the intersection (
3.15
) inside the ball
W_{p}
can also be thought of as taking place in the fibre
Y_{t}
,

\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{W_{p*}\,\sigma}\rangle=\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle,

because
\partial W_{p}
is transverse to
D
. The local formulas globalize with (
3.8
):

Corollary 3.23
.

For a small loop
\gamma
around a simple pinch component
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, the variation
\Var_{\gamma}
on
H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
can be written as:

(3.16)

\Var_{\gamma}(\sigma)=(-1)^{(n+1)(n+2)/2}\cdot\sum_{p}\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle\cdot\nu_{p}.

Remark 3.24
.

Let
A_{j}
(
j\in J
) and
B_{k}
(
k\in K
) denote the hypersurfaces that contain the pinch
p
. Iterating
\langle{\delta\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle=(-1)^{d}\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{\partial h}\rangle
from (
C.4
), with
d
the degree of
h
, the intersection number reduces to

\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle=(-1)^{n\lvert K\rvert+\lvert K\rvert(\lvert K\rvert+1)/2}\cdot\langle{\partial_{K}\zeta}\,|\,{\partial_{K}h}\rangle.

The coefficient
N(h)
can thus be computed on the manifold
\overline{M}=\overline{W_{p}}\cap B^{K}
with its intersection pairing

H_{n-\lvert K\rvert}(M,A^{\prime})\times H_{n-\lvert K\rvert}(\overline{M}\setminus A^{\prime},\partial M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}

for
A^{\prime}=B^{K}\cap A_{J}
, after taking all
B_{k}
-boundaries of
\zeta
and
h
.

For the proof of
3.21
, we fix a pinch
p
(henceforth suppressing the subscript
p
) and consider every disjoint triple
I\sqcup J\sqcup K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
of the hypersurfaces
S_{1},\ldots,S_{m}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
that contain
p
, as in (
3.7
). The corresponding divisors
S_{J}\cap S^{I}\cap W
and
S_{K}\cap S^{I}\cap W
inside the manifold
W\cap S^{I}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
define pairs of fibre bundles with associated variations

(3.17)

\var\colon H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}.

Remark 3.25
.

The configuration of a strict subset
I\sqcup J\sqcup K\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
has no critical points and thus
\var=0
.
Henceforth, we consider only the case of tripartitions
I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
of all
m
hypersurfaces.

More precisely, we define the variations (
3.17
) by choosing a
single
isotopy
g_{\gamma}
as in
3.7
. We then obtain
\var=g^{\prime}_{*}-\id
via
3.11
, using the corresponding restrictions
g^{\prime}=g_{\gamma}|_{S^{I}\setminus S_{J}}
of
g_{\gamma}
.
Thus all variations arise from the same isotopy, and no confusion should arise from denoting all of them with the same symbol
\var
.

Furthermore, since (co)boundaries commute with push-forward, see (
B.6
), the following diagram commutes for all
i\in I
:
19
19

19

This commutativity from
section

B.3
requires that
\var
is computed using a
smooth
isotopy
g_{\gamma}
, hence our insistence on the smooth isotopy theorem (
2.6
).

Following
[
21
]
, the strategy to prove
3.21
is to exploit these diagrams to reduce the calculation to a few special cases. For a quadratic pinch, we can always reduce to
J=K=\varnothing
(
section

3.8
). For a linear pinch (
section

3.7
), we have to distinguish three different types, as in
3.22
.

To spell out these reductions, we extend
3.17
and denote, for all
I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
, the respective (dual) vanishing cycles as

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\Big(\prod_{j\in J}\delta_{j}\partial_{j}\Big)\partial_{I}\zeta

\displaystyle\in H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nu}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\lvert K\rvert}\cdot\Big(\prod_{k\in K}\delta_{k}\partial_{k}\Big)\partial_{I}\zeta

\displaystyle\in H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{K},S_{J}).

Then
3.21
arises as the special case
I=\varnothing
of

Theorem 3.26
.

For every tripartition
(I,J,K)
, the variation (
3.17
) is zero in all degrees other than
n-\lvert I\rvert
. For a class
h
in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
,

(3.19)

\var h=(-1)^{(n-\lvert I\rvert+1)(n-\lvert I\rvert+2)/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\nu.

Note that, for this formula to make any sense, it has to be compatible with the commutative diagram (
3.18
). This can be verified easily:

Lemma 3.27
.

Let
\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}\colon H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}
denote the map defined by the right-hand side of (
3.19
). Then the diagram (
3.18
), with
\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}
in place of
\var
, is commutative.

Proof.

Consider the bottom square of (
3.18
), relating the variations of the triples
(I,J,K)
and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})=(I-i,J+i,K)
. Let
\nu^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
denote the vanishing cycles for
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
. Note that
\nu^{\prime}=\epsilon\delta_{i}\nu
and
\widetilde{\nu}=\epsilon\partial_{i}\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}
, where the sign
\epsilon=\pm 1
is determined by the order of
I
such that
\partial_{I}=\epsilon\partial_{i}\partial_{I-i}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\delta_{i}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}h)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(d+1)(d+2)/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\delta_{i}\nu

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(d+2)(d+3)/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}}\,|\,{\delta_{i}h}\rangle\cdot\nu^{\prime}=\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}(\delta_{i}h)

follows from
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle=\epsilon\langle{\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime}}\,|\,{\delta_{i}}\rangle\cdot(-1)^{d}
by (
C.4
), where
d=n-\lvert I\rvert
denotes the degree of
h
and
d+1=n-\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert
is the degree of
\delta_{i}h
. In the same way, one checks the upper square, where the vanishing cycles
\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime\prime}
of the triple
(I-i,J,K+i)
are related by
\nu=\epsilon\partial_{i}\nu^{\prime\prime}
and
\widetilde{\nu}^{\prime\prime}=-\epsilon\delta_{i}\widetilde{\nu}
.
∎

The proof of
3.26
simplifies due to the following observation: if a vertical map (
\partial_{i}
or
\delta_{i}
) of the diagram (
3.18
) is surjective in the domain (left column), or injective in the codomain (right column), then the validity of (
3.19
) for some partition implies that (
3.19
) also holds for a related partition.

Corollary 3.28
.

Subject to injectivity or surjectivity, each of the four vertical maps
\partial_{i},\delta_{i}
in the diagram (
3.18
) gives rise to an implication:

(
3.19
) holds for

(I-i,J+i,K)

\delta_{i}
injective

in codomain

(
3.19
) holds for
(I,J,K)

\partial_{i}
injective

in codomain

\delta_{i}
surjective

in domain

(
3.19
) holds for

(I-i,J,K+i)

\partial_{i}
surjective

in domain

Proof.

By the previous lemma, we can put the difference
\var-\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}
on the horizontal arrows in (
3.18
) and obtain a commutative diagram. The validity of (
3.19
) is equivalent to
\var-\operatorname{\widetilde{\var}}=0
.
∎

In most cases, the (co)boundary maps are isomorphisms, and so the proof of
3.26
reduces to a few boundary cases.
For small
t\neq 0
, the groups

H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}

and

H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}

in the domain and codomain of the local variations (
3.17
), together with the (co)boundary maps between them, are computed in
appendix

A
. In particular, we find:

Proposition 3.29
.

For
K\neq\varnothing
, the codomain
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is concentrated in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
. The iterated (co)boundaries give isomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{n}(W,S_{1,\ldots,m})_{t}\xrightarrow[\delta_{J}\partial_{I\sqcup J}]{\cong}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}.

For
J\neq\varnothing
, the domain
H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is concentrated in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
. The iterated (co)boundaries give isomorphisms

H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}\xrightarrow[\delta_{I\sqcup K}\partial_{K}]{\cong}H_{n}(\overline{W}\setminus S_{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

See
A.4
and
section

A.1
, in particular item (2).
∎

This result applies to linear and quadratic simple pinches. Note that in any degree other than
n-\lvert I\rvert
, the domain or codomain, and hence the map
\var
, is zero (the first claim of
3.26
).
Furthermore, with
3.28
we learn that in order to prove
3.26
, it is sufficient to confirm (
3.19
) for partitions with
\lvert J\rvert,\lvert K\rvert\leq 1
.

3.7.
Linear pinch

For
J=K=\varnothing
, the identity (
3.19
) holds trivially, because the intersection
S^{I}=S^{1,\ldots,n+1}=\varnothing
is empty. The factorization through the trivial group
0=H_{-1}(\varnothing)\ni\partial_{1,\ldots,n+1}\zeta
also shows that:

•

If
J=\varnothing
, then
\widetilde{\nu}=\pm\delta_{K}\partial_{1,\ldots,n+1}\zeta=0
.

•

If
K=\varnothing
, then
\nu=\pm\delta_{J}\partial_{1,\ldots,n+1}\zeta=0
.

So in these cases,
3.26
amounts to
\var=0
. Let us first confirm this vanishing when
J\sqcup K=\{k\}
is a singleton. Since
S^{I}=\bigcap_{i\neq k}S_{i}=\{O_{k}\}
is then a single point—a corner of the vanishing cell—the groups

\displaystyle H_{0}(\{O_{k}\})_{t}

\displaystyle=H_{0}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W\cup S_{k})_{t}=H_{0}(W\cap S^{I},S_{k})_{t}

\displaystyle=H_{0}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{k},\partial W)_{t}\,=H_{0}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{k})_{t}

are all
canonically
identified
H_{0}(\{O_{k}\})_{t}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}
, for all
t\neq 0
, by the augmentation map. Thus the class of this corner has no variation, and it also generates the domain of
\var
. Therefore we see that indeed
\var=0
.

For
J=\varnothing
, this vanishing extends to all
K
via the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W\cup S_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\var}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{K-k}}
\textstyle{H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I},S_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{K-k}}
\textstyle{H_{0}(O_{k})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\var=0}
\textstyle{H_{0}(O_{k})}

for any
k\in K
, because
\partial_{K-k}
is an isomorphism in the codomain (
3.29
). Similarly, for
K=\varnothing
and
j\in J
, the isomorphism
\delta_{J-j}
in the domain shows that
\var=0
follows from the previous singleton case
\lvert J\rvert=1
.

So far, we have proved
\var=0
for all linear pinches of “
A
type” (
K=\varnothing
) or “
B
type” (
J=\varnothing
), as referred to in
3.22
.

The remaining possibility is “mixed type”, where both sets,
J
and
K
, are non-empty. In this region, all boundaries and coboundaries are isomorphisms (
3.29
). Hence it suffices to verify the variation formula (
3.19
) for a single partition with
\lvert J\rvert=\lvert K\rvert=1
.
20
20

20

The only exception is at
n=1
, where there are no (co)boundary maps in the mixed region. Hence the two partitions
(I,J,K)=(\varnothing,\{1\},\{2\})
and
(\varnothing,\{2\},\{1\})
have to be considered separately. The calculations are the same, only
0
and
t
swap roles in
fig.

11
.

We pick
I=\{2,\ldots,n\}
,
J=\{1\}
and
K=\{n+1\}
.
Inside the unit disk
W\cap S^{I}\cong\mathbb{D}=\{\lvert x\rvert<1\}\subset\mathbb{C}
, the edge
\partial_{I}\zeta=[O_{1},O_{n+1}]
of the vanishing cell is represented by a path from the corner
O_{1}=\{x=t\}
to
O_{n+1}=\{x=0\}
. The groups

\displaystyle H_{1}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}

\displaystyle=H_{1}(\mathbb{D}\setminus\{0\},\{t\})\cong\mathbb{Z}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle H_{1}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{K},S_{J})_{t}

\displaystyle=H_{1}(\mathbb{D}\setminus\{t\},\{0\})\cong\mathbb{Z}

are generated by the counter-clockwise vanishing cycles
\nu=\delta_{1}\partial_{1}\partial_{I}\zeta
and
\widetilde{\nu}=-\delta_{n+1}\partial_{n+1}\partial_{I}\zeta
, respectively (see
fig.

11
).
The domain of the variation,

H_{1}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}=H_{1}(\overline{\mathbb{D}}\setminus\{0\},\partial\mathbb{D}\cup\{t\})\cong\mathbb{Z},

is generated by any path
h
from the boundary circle
\partial\mathbb{D}
to
t
.
At the intersection of
h
and
\widetilde{\nu}
, their tangent spaces
Th\wedge T\widetilde{\nu}
are negatively oriented compared to the standard orientation
\mathrm{d}\Re x\wedge\mathrm{d}\Im x
of
\mathbb{C}
. The intersection number is therefore
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle=-1
, and
3.26
amounts to

\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{I}\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{T\widetilde{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{Th}
\scriptstyle{\Re x}
\scriptstyle{\Im x}

Figure 11.
The homology classes of a mixed linear pinch in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert=1
.

\var h=(-1)^{2\cdot 3/2}\cdot\langle{\widetilde{\nu}}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\nu=\nu.

This finishes the proof of
3.26
for linear pinches, since this is indeed the correct variation as computed by an isotopy (illustrated in
fig.

12
).

\scriptstyle{\gamma_{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{t}

\cong

\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\nu}
\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{t}

Figure 12.
Variation in the proof of
3.26
for linear pinches.

3.8.
Quadratic pinch

For a quadratic pinch (
m\leq n
), even more of the (co)boundary maps are isomorphisms, at least in the degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
of interest. In addition to
3.29
, we show in
appendix

A
:

•

A.7
: For
K=\varnothing
, the coboundary maps are isomorphisms in the codomain,

\delta_{J}\colon H_{n-m}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J}).

•

Section

A.1
, (3): For
J=\varnothing
, boundaries are isomorphisms in the domain,

\partial_{K}\colon H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W\cup S_{K})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-m}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m},\partial W).

Only two kinds of (co)boundary maps remain, that are not necessarily isomorphisms by the results above: the first coboundary (
J=\varnothing
,
i\in I
)

(3.20)

\delta_{i}\colon H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W\cup S_{K})\longrightarrow H_{n-\lvert I\rvert+1}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I-i}\setminus S_{i},\partial W\cup S_{K})

in the domain, and the last boundary (
K=\{k\}
) in the codomain:

(3.21)

\partial_{k}\colon H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{k})\longrightarrow H_{n-\lvert I\rvert-1}(W\cap S^{I+k}\setminus S_{J}).

Lemma 3.30
.

For a quadratic pinch, the first coboundaries (
3.20
) are surjective, and the last boundaries (
3.21
) are injective.

Proof.

Since all boundaries
\partial_{K}
are isomorphisms in the domain and
\delta_{i}\partial_{K}=(-1)^{\lvert K\rvert}\partial_{K}\delta_{i}
, it suffices to prove surjectivity of (
3.20
) for
K=\varnothing
. Then the term after (
3.20
) in the long exact residue sequence is zero,

H_{n-\lvert I\rvert+1}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I-i},\partial W)\cong H^{n-\lvert I\rvert+1}(W\cap S^{I-i})=0,

by Poincaré-Lefschetz duality and contractibility of
W\cap S^{I-i}
(
A.1
). Similarly, for the injectivity of (
3.21
), it suffices to consider
J=\varnothing
, since the coboundaries
\delta_{J}
are isomorphisms in the codomain and
\partial_{k}\delta_{J}=(-1)^{\lvert J\rvert}\delta_{J}\partial_{k}
.
Then the group to the left of (
3.21
) in the boundary sequence of
S_{k}
is
H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I})=0
, because
W\cap S^{I}
is contractible and
\lvert I\rvert<m\leq n
.
∎

Remark 3.31
.

For linear pinch, one finds instead that (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) are zero, because
\partial_{k}=(-1)^{\lvert J\rvert}\delta_{J}\partial_{k}\delta_{J}^{-1}=0
and
\delta_{i}=(-1)^{\lvert K\rvert}\partial_{K}^{-1}\delta_{i}\partial_{K}=0
factor through the trivial homology group of
S^{1,\ldots,n+1}=\varnothing
.

We conclude that, for a quadratic pinch, all coboundary maps are surjective in the domain, and all boundary maps are injective in the codomain.
Using
3.28
, the proof of
3.26
thus reduces to the single case
J=K=\varnothing
of the complete intersection
I=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. The first
m-1
coordinates are zero on this intersection
S^{I}
and can be forgotten, so that

S^{I}\cong\{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{r+1}^{2}=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+1}

where
r=n-m
. The vanishing cycles
\nu=\widetilde{\nu}=[\mathbb{S}^{r}]
are equal and given by the fundamental class of the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{r}=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\colon\lVert u\rVert=1\}
, embedded as

\mathbb{S}^{r}\hookrightarrow W\cap S^{I},\quad u\mapsto x=u\cdot\sqrt{t}.

This vanishing sphere parametrizes the real part of
S^{I}
, and it is a deformation retract (
A.2
) such that
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I})\cong H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S}^{r})
. The claim (
3.19
),

\var h=(-1)^{(r+1)(r+2)/2}\cdot\langle{\nu}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\nu,

is the classical Picard-Lefschetz formula. A proof can be found e.g. in
[
35
,
34
]
; we include a proof in
section

A.2
for convenience of the reader.

Remark 3.32
.

As a corollary of the Picard-Lefschetz theorem and (
3.12
), we can read off the well-known self-intersection number
[
19
]
of the real sphere
\mathbb{S}^{r}
inside the quadric
\{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{r+1}^{2}=1\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+1}
:

\langle{[\mathbb{S}^{r}]}\,|\,{[\mathbb{S}^{r}]}\rangle=(-1)^{(r+1)(r+2)/2}\cdot\left[(-1)^{r+1}-1\right]=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $r$ odd, and}\\
2\cdot(-1)^{r/2}&\text{if $r$ even.}\\
\end{cases}

3.9.
Arbitrary simple pinch

The observations in
section

3.8
generalize beyond quadratic pinches. Extending
3.13
, we say that a critical point
p\in cS
on a stratum
S\subseteq D_{1}\cap\ldots\cap D_{m}
of codimension
m
is a
simple pinch
if there exist local coordinates
(x,t)
near
p
such that
\pi(x,t)=t
and

(3.22)

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1}=0\},\quad\ldots,\quad D_{m-1}=\{x_{m-1}=0\},

\displaystyle D_{m}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1}+f(x_{m},\ldots,x_{n})=t\}

for some polynomial
f\colon\mathbb{C}^{r+1}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
with an isolated critical point at the origin (
r=n-m
). Milnor
[
43
,
16
]
showed that the boundary
\partial W\cong\mathbb{S}^{2n-1}
of the ball
W=\{\lVert x\rVert<\epsilon\}
, with
\epsilon
small enough, intersects the arrangement
D_{1,\ldots,m}
transversely (hence the variation localizes) and moreover, the local fibres

(3.23)

S_{t}\cap W\simeq\mathbb{S}^{r}\vee\ldots\vee\mathbb{S}^{r}

over sufficiently small
t\neq 0
are homotopy equivalent to a bouquet (wedge sum) of finitely many spheres. The number of these spheres is called the
Milnor number

\mu
. For a quadratic pinch,
\mu=1
. For any proper subset
I\subsetneq\{1,\ldots,m\}
, the arrangement
D_{I}
has no critical point,
\mu=0
, and thus

D^{I}_{t}\cap W\simeq\{*\}

is contractible, with the homology of a point. With this generalization of
A.1
, and (
3.23
) standing in for
A.2
, the proofs in
appendix

A
still apply—we only need to replace
\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}
. In particular, for
any
non-linear simple pinch (
m\leq n
), it remains true that all boundary maps in the domain, and all coboundary maps in the codomain, are isomorphisms (in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
). Moreover, for any tripartition
I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
, the maps

\displaystyle H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap D^{I}\setminus D_{J},\partial W\cup D_{K})_{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{I\sqcup K}\partial_{K}}}{{\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow}}H_{n}(\overline{W}\setminus D_{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}\cong H_{n}(W,D_{1,\ldots,m})_{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta_{J}\partial_{I\sqcup J}}}{{\lhook\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\relbar\joinrel\rightarrow}}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap D^{I}\setminus D_{J},D_{K})_{t}

stay surjective in the domain, and injective in the codomain, of
\var_{p}
.

Corollary 3.33
.

The local variation of any simple pinch
p\in A^{J}\cap B^{K}
of codimension
m=\lvert J\sqcup K\rvert
factorizes through the boundary
\partial_{K}
in the domain, and the coboundary
\delta_{J}
in the codomain:
21
21

21

For a linear pinch, these maps can vanish:
\delta_{J}=0
if
K=\varnothing
(
A
type) and
\partial_{K}=0
if
J=\varnothing
(
B
type). Nevertheless, the factorization still holds, because
\var_{p}=0
in these cases.

(3.24)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 50.64366pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\cr&\cr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-47.90909pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-56.2778pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 99.01334pt\raise-31.94446pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 137.83585pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 114.25003pt\raise-15.97223pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.92778pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta_{J}\partial_{J}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 137.83585pt\raise-8.05554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.09914pt\raise-63.55557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 129.09914pt\raise-63.55557pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.58585pt\raise-47.75002pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 137.83585pt\raise-43.05556pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

The fundamental classes
\nu_{i}=[\mathbb{S}^{r}]\in\widetilde{H}_{n-r}(W\cap D^{1,\ldots,m})_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}
define a basis of the reduced homology of the bouquet (
3.23
), to which corresponds a basis
\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{\mu}\in H_{n}(W,D)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}
of vanishing cells such that
\nu_{i}=\partial_{1,\ldots,m}\zeta_{i}
. By Poincaré-Lefschetz duality, the intersections
h\mapsto\langle{\zeta_{k}}\,|\,{h}\rangle
yield a basis of the dual of
H_{n}(\overline{W}\setminus D_{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{\mu}
, and we can write

\var_{p}(h)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\mu}M_{ij}\cdot\langle{\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\delta_{K}\partial_{K}h}\rangle\cdot\delta_{J}\partial_{J}\zeta_{j}

for some matrix
M\in M_{\mu\times\mu}(\mathbb{Z})
. Hence, if we define
\widetilde{\nu}_{j}=\pm\delta_{K}\partial_{K}\sum_{i}M_{ij}\zeta_{i}
and
\nu_{i}=\pm\delta_{J}\partial_{J}\zeta_{i}
, with suitable signs as in
3.17
, then we can write a Picard-Lefschetz formula for an arbitrary simple pinch in the form

(3.25)

\var_{p}(h)=(-1)^{(n+1)(n+2)/2}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\mu}\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{i}}\,|\,{h}\rangle\cdot\nu_{i}.

Note that the same formula (
3.16
) describes
\mu
quadratic pinches, and it can indeed be derived by considering a deformation of the singularity
[
34
, §6]
.

As in the quadratic case, the precise formula (the matrix
M
) is fully determined by the variation of the complete intersection
D^{1,\ldots,m}_{t}=\{f=t\}
. The monodromy of such isolated hypersurface singularities is a vast subject; as an example let us only note Pham’s contribution
[
48
]
of the highly structured case
f=x_{m}^{a_{m}}+\ldots+x_{n}^{a_{n}}
with arbitrary integer exponents
a_{i}\geq 2
.

4.
The hierarchy principle

In the Picard-Lefschetz theorem, the partition
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}
of the components of the divisor
D=A\cup B
is important: vanishing cycles are coboundaries (tubes) around
A
with boundary in
B
, whereas the dual vanishing cycles are coboundaries around
B
with boundary in
A
. The
hierarchy principle
refers to the vanishing of iterated variations for simple set-theoretic reasons, due to merely keeping track of which elements of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
support a simple pinch.

Definition 4.1
.

We say that a stratum
S
of the canonical stratification of
D
has
type

(J,K)
if
S
is a connected component of

(
\sharp
)

A^{J}\cap B^{K}\setminus\bigcup_{i\notin J\sqcup K}D_{i}.

The type of a stratum
S
lists the components of
A
and
B
that contain
S
:
J=\{j\colon S\subseteq A_{j}\}
and
K=\{k\colon S\subseteq B_{k}\}
. Since every point lies on a unique stratum, we also say that “
p
has type
(J,K)
” if the stratum containing
p
has this type, that is, if
p
is an element of the set (

♯

‣
4.1
).

4.1.
A preorder on simple pinches

For every simple pinch
p
, we defined a local variation endomorphism
\Var_{p}=\iota_{p*}\var_{p}W_{p*}
of
H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
in
3.15
, for
t\in T\setminus L
sufficiently close to the critical value
\pi(p)
. For a second simple pinch
p^{\prime}
, the local variation
\Var_{p^{\prime}}
is defined canonically for
t^{\prime}
near
\pi(p^{\prime})
. To make sense of the iterated variation
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}
, we need to identify

\eta_{*}\colon H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t^{\prime}}

by parallel transport. Hence the iterated variation should more precisely be written as
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=\eta_{*}^{-1}\Var_{p^{\prime}}\eta_{*}\Var_{p}
and it depends on the choice of a path
\eta
in
T\setminus L
from
t
to
t^{\prime}
. However, the vanishing results derived below apply for
any
choice of
\eta
, hence we write just
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}
.

Figure 13.
Iterated variation around two critical values.

By the Picard-Lefschetz theorem,
\Var_{p}
maps any class in
H_{n}(X\setminus A,B)_{t}
to a multiple of the vanishing cycle
\nu_{p}
. So if
\Var_{p^{\prime}}(\eta_{*}\nu_{p})=0
, it follows that the iterated variation
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}
is zero.
Rewriting the intersection number

\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\nu_{p}}\rangle=\pm\langle{\partial_{K^{\prime}}\zeta_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\partial_{K^{\prime}}\nu_{p}}\rangle=\pm\langle{\partial_{J}\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\partial_{J}\zeta_{p}}\rangle

as in
3.24
,
22
22

22

More precisely,
\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\eta_{*}\nu_{p}}\rangle=\pm\langle{\iota_{p^{\prime}*}\partial_{K^{\prime}}\zeta_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\eta_{*}\partial_{K^{\prime}}\nu_{p}}\rangle=\pm\langle{\partial_{J}\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}}\,|\,{\eta_{*}\iota_{p*}\partial_{J}\zeta_{p}}\rangle
.
we see that such vanishing is implied by
\partial_{K^{\prime}}\nu_{p}=0
or
\partial_{J}\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}=0
. This observation leads directly to the hierarchy in (
4.3
) below. However, intersection theory is not required to explain this phenomenon; what matters more fundamentally is the factorization of
\Var_{p}
—through
\partial_{K}
in the domain, and through
\delta_{J}
in the codomain (
3.11
and
3.33
).

The cycle
\nu_{p}
is supported in a small ball
W_{p}
that intersects only those
A_{j}
and
B_{k}
where
j\in J
and
k\in K
are in the type
(J,K)
of
p
. For any
other
boundary component (
k\notin K
), we have
W_{p}\cap B_{k}=\varnothing
and
\partial_{B_{k}}\nu_{p}=0
. After continuation to
t^{\prime}
, the cycle
\eta_{*}\nu_{p}
need not be contained in any small ball anymore—but it does stay away from
B_{k}
and so
\partial_{B_{k}}\eta_{*}\nu_{p}=0
, for
k\notin K
. This follows from the diagram

\textstyle{H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{B_{k}}}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{n-1}(B_{k}\setminus A,B^{\prime})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{B_{k}}}
\textstyle{H_{n-1}(B_{k}\setminus A,B^{\prime})_{t^{\prime}}}

where
B^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\neq k}B_{i}
. The diagram commutes (see
section

B.3
), because an isotopy of the stratified set
D
descends to compatible local trivializations of the fibre bundles
(Y\setminus A,B)
and
(B_{k}\setminus A,B^{\prime})
over
T\setminus L
, and furthermore the isotopy can be chosen to be smooth.

As pointed out in
3.24
and
3.33
, the variation
\Var_{p^{\prime}}
factors through the iterated boundary
\partial_{K^{\prime}}
of the second simple pinch
p^{\prime}
with type
(J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
. So if
K^{\prime}\setminus K
is non-empty, and hence
\partial_{K^{\prime}}\eta_{*}\nu_{p}=0
as explained above, it follows that
\Var_{p^{\prime}}(\eta_{*}\nu_{p})=0
.
We conclude that

(4.1)

\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{if $K^{\prime}\nsubseteq K$}.

Now consider some
j\in J
and let
A^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\neq j}A_{i}
. As a coboundary around
A_{j}
, the vanishing cycle
\nu_{p}
becomes zero under the inclusion
\kappa\colon Y\setminus A\hookrightarrow Y\setminus A^{\prime}
, because the composition
\kappa_{*}\delta_{j}=0
vanishes as part of the residue sequence

H_{n-1}(A_{j}\setminus A^{\prime},B)_{t}\xrightarrow{\delta_{j}}H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\xrightarrow{\kappa_{*}}H_{n}(Y\setminus A^{\prime},B)_{t}.

As discussed above, such vanishing
\kappa_{*}\nu_{p}=0
persists by parallel transport also at
t^{\prime}
, whence
\kappa_{*}(\eta_{*}\nu_{p})=0
. If
A_{j}
does not contain
p^{\prime}
, then
A_{j}
does not intersect the ball
W_{p^{\prime}}
and so
W_{p^{\prime}}\setminus A=W_{p^{\prime}}\setminus A^{\prime}
. Then the trace map
W_{p^{\prime}*}
factors through
\kappa_{*}
, and thus annihilates the vanishing cycle. It follows that

(4.2)

\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{if $J^{\prime}\nsupseteq J$}.

Definition 4.2
.

We define a relation on the set of simple pinches as follows:
For simple pinches
p
and
p^{\prime}
with type
(J,K)
and
(J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
, we set

p^{\prime}\leqslant p\quad\text{if}\quad\text{$J^{\prime}\supseteq J$ and $K^{\prime}\subseteq K$}.

This relation is transitive and reflexive (a preorder). We can then summarize the observations made above as

(4.3)

\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{whenever}\quad p^{\prime}\nleqslant p.

Remark 4.3
.

These conclusions about variations at simple pinches hold also in the presence of further, non-simple critical points.

4.2.
Linear and quadratic refinements

For a linear pinch
p
, the boundary
\partial_{K}\nu_{p}=\pm\iota_{p*}\delta_{J}\partial_{1,\ldots,n+1}\zeta=0
of the vanishing cell (simplex) is empty.
In addition to the vanishing (
4.1
) for
K^{\prime}\setminus K\neq\varnothing
, we therefore also have

(4.4)

\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\big(\text{$K^{\prime}=K$ and $p$ linear}\big).

Similarly, if
p^{\prime}
is a linear pinch, then
\partial_{J^{\prime}}\widetilde{\nu}_{p^{\prime}}=0
extends the vanishing (
4.2
) by the additional constraint

(4.5)

\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\big(\text{$J^{\prime}=J$ and $p^{\prime}$ linear}\big).

Corollary 4.4
.

If all critical points are linear pinches, then
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}
can only be non-zero if
J^{\prime}\supset J
and
K^{\prime}\subset K
are strict inclusions. Hence in this situation, the monodromy representation is unipotent: every sequence of
n
or more variations is zero. We discuss this further in
section

5.1
.

From (
3.12
), we also know that for a linear or quadratic simple pinch
p
on a stratum with codimension
m=\lvert J\rvert+\lvert K\rvert
, the repeated variation vanishes,

(4.6)

\Var_{p}\circ\Var_{p}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\text{$n-m$ is odd.}

This applies in particular to linear pinches (
m=n+1
).

Remark 4.5
.

We can incorporate the additional constraints (
4.4
)–(
4.6
) into (
4.3
) by removing the relations
p^{\prime}\leqslant p
among those particular pairs of pinches. The resulting relation is still transitive, but not necessarily reflexive anymore.

4.3.
A hierarchy on simple pinch components

Over every irreducible component
\ell\subseteq L
of the Landau variety, we may have
several
critical strata. Suppose that
\ell
is a simple pinch component (
3.14
), that is, all critical points over a generic point
t_{c}\in\ell
are simple pinches.
23
23

23

We can allow here the more general notion (
3.22
) of simple pinch, not just the linear and quadratic ones from
3.13
.
Let
P_{\ell}\subset\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
denote these pinches.
Recall from
3.15
that

\Var_{\ell}=\sum_{p\in P_{\ell}}\Var_{p},

where we write
\Var_{\ell}
for the variation
\Var_{\gamma}
along a simple loop around
t_{c}\in\ell
. The operator
\Var_{\ell}
does depend on the choice of
\gamma
, but this choice is irrelevant for the following vanishing results. Furthermore, we write
\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\ell}
to abbreviate
\eta_{*}^{-1}\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\eta_{*}\Var_{\ell}
, as above.

Definition 4.6
.

Define a binary relation
\leqslant
on the set of simple pinch components of the Landau variety as follows:

\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell\quad\text{if there exist $p\in P_{\ell}$ and $p^{\prime}\in P_{\ell^{\prime}}$ such that $p^{\prime}\leqslant p$.}

Corollary 4.7
.

For any pair
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\subseteq L
of simple pinch components, we have

\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell^{\prime}\nleqslant\ell.

Due to the arbitrariness of the intermediate path
\eta
in
section

4.1
, we have actually shown that for arbitrary paths
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})
,

\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\gamma}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell^{\prime}\nleqslant\ell.

4.4.
Generalization

Above, we derived the hierarchy principle as a consequence of the explicit Picard-Lefschetz formula for (arbitrary)
simple pinches
, as characterized by the local description (
3.22
). What is “simple” about these critical points
p\in cS
is not just that they are (fibrewise) isolated, but furthermore that the exclusion of any of the supporting hypersurfaces from the arrangement removes the critical point. We will demonstrate below that the latter property is sufficient to explain the hierarchy principle—without any recourse to the Picard-Lefschetz formula. This discussion will lead to a generalization of the hierarchy principle to “non-simple” critical points.

Consider some generic critical value
t_{c}\in\ell\subseteq L
. The corresponding critical points
P=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}cS\cap\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
need not be isolated; they form some closed subvariety
P\subset X
. In any case, we expect some form of the localization from
section

3.2
: For every connected component
p\in\pi_{0}(P)
, suppose we can find a smooth manifold
W_{p}\subset X
containing
p\subset W_{p}
, not intersecting any other components of
P
, and such that its boundary
\partial W_{p}
is smooth and transverse to
D
. Then the proof of
3.15
still applies, so that

\Var_{\ell}=\sum_{p\in\pi_{0}(P)}\Var_{p}\quad\text{with}\quad\Var_{p}=\iota_{p*}\var_{p}W_{p*}

where
\var_{p}
is defined just as in
3.11
. For example, if
p\in\pi_{0}(P)
is a smooth component, we can construct
W_{p}
as a tubular neighbourhood of
p
. For an isolated point
p
, this reproduces the balls used in
section

3.3
.

Definition 4.8
.

The
type

(J,K)
of a critical component
p\in\pi_{0}(P)
is the set of those hypersurfaces
A_{j}
(
j\in J
) and
B_{k}
(
k\in K
) which intersect
p
.

Note that if we choose
W_{p}
small enough, then it will intersect only the hypersurfaces in the type of
p
:
W_{p}\cap A=W_{p}\cap A_{J}
and
W_{p}\cap B=W_{p}\cap B_{K}
. Then the trace
W_{p*}
factors through the inclusion
X\setminus A\hookrightarrow X\setminus A_{J}
, and
\iota_{p*}
factors through the map of pairs
(X\setminus A,B_{K})\rightarrow(X\setminus A,B)
:

(4.7)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern The hierarchy constraints arise from hypersurfaces in the type that are indispensable to make
p
critical. We call those hypersurfaces
simple
.

Definition 4.9
.

Given a critical component
p\in\pi_{0}(P)
of type
(J,K)
, we call an element
i\in J\sqcup K
and the corresponding hypersurface
D_{i}

simple
if the union
\bigcup_{k\neq i}D_{k}
of all other components of
D
has no critical points in
p
. We denote the simple elements of the type as
J_{\mathsf{s}}\subseteq J
and
K_{\mathsf{s}}\subseteq K
.

Remark 4.10
.

For a simple pinch, omitting
any
hypersurface
D_{1},\ldots,D_{m}
in (
3.22
) results in a non-critical arrangement. Therefore, the entire type is simple:
(J_{\mathsf{s}},K_{\mathsf{s}})=(J,K)
. In fact, this property characterizes simple pinches topologically: Every isolated critical point with
(J_{\mathsf{s}},K_{\mathsf{s}})=(J,K)
admits local coordinates of the form (
3.22
).

Non-simple situations can arise even for isolated critical points, for example if one starts with a simple pinch
p
and then enlarges the arrangement by an additional hyperplane that contains
p
.

Example 4.11
.

Consider
Y=X\times T=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{C}
with the transverse arrangement
D=D_{1}\cup D_{2}
formed by the smooth hypersurfaces
D_{1}=\{x^{2}=t\}
and
D_{2}=\{x=0\}
in coordinates
[x:1]\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The pair
(Y,D)
has a single critical point,
c(D_{1}\cap D_{2})=\{(p,t_{c})\}
over
t_{c}=0
, namely
p=[0:1]
.

•

D_{1}
is simple: The remaining pair
(Y,D_{2})\cong(\mathbb{P}^{1},\{p\})\times\mathbb{C}
is a (trivial) fibre bundle, without any critical points.

•

D_{2}
is not simple: Even after removing
D_{2}
, the remaining pair
(Y,D_{1})
still has
c(D_{1})=\{(p,t_{c})\}
as a critical point.

For simple pinches, the hierarchy follows from the factorization (
3.24
) of
\var_{p}
through each
\partial_{k}
in the domain, and each
\delta_{j}
in the codomain. For general critical sets, such a factorization applies only to the simple indices. Given an index
k\in K
or
j\in J
, consider the natural map of pairs

\displaystyle\rho_{k}\colon

\displaystyle\textstyle(X\setminus A,\bigcup_{i\neq k}B_{i})

\displaystyle\rightarrow(X\setminus A,B),

\displaystyle\kappa_{j}\colon

\displaystyle(X\setminus A,B)

\displaystyle\textstyle\rightarrow(X\setminus\bigcup_{i\neq j}A_{i},B).

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
p\in\pi_{0}(P)
with type
(J,K)
and simple part
(J_{\mathsf{s}},K_{\mathsf{s}})
. Then for every
k\in K_{\mathsf{s}}
, and for every
j\in J_{\mathsf{s}}
, we have

(4.8)

\kappa_{j*}\Var_{p}=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Var_{p}\rho_{k*}=0.

Equivalently, the local variation at
p
factors through the boundary map
\partial_{k}
in the domain, and through the Leray coboundary
\delta_{j}
in the codomain.

Proof.

Let
A^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\neq j}A_{i}
and
B^{\prime}=\bigcup_{i\neq k}B_{i}
denote the arrangements
A
and
B
, with one of the simple indices omitted. By
4.9
, the corresponding pairs

(W_{p}\setminus A^{\prime},B)
and
(W_{p}\setminus A,B^{\prime})
are locally trivial even over the critical value
\{t_{c}\}=\pi(P)
. Hence they have trivial monodromy and zero variation.

In the domain, this implies that the local variation
\Var_{p}
vanishes on the image of the push-forward of the map
\rho\colon(X\setminus A,B^{\prime})\rightarrow(X\setminus A,B)
. By the long exact boundary sequence (
3.9
), this image equals the kernel of
\partial_{k}
, thus
\Var_{p}
factors through
\partial_{k}
:

\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A,B^{\prime})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\Var_{p}}
\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A,B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Var_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{k}}
\textstyle{H_{n-1}(B_{k}\setminus A,B^{\prime})_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A,B)_{t}}

In the codomain, the zero variation of
(W_{p}\setminus A^{\prime},B)
implies that
\Var_{p}
takes values in the kernel of
\kappa_{*}
, where
\kappa\colon(X\setminus A,B)\rightarrow(X\setminus A^{\prime},B)
:

\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A,B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Var_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{*}}
\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A^{\prime},B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Var_{p}}
\textstyle{H_{n-1}(A_{j}\setminus A^{\prime},B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{j}}
\textstyle{H_{n}(X\setminus A,B)_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\kappa_{*}}
\textstyle{0}

By the long exact residue sequence, the kernel of
\kappa_{*}
is the image of
\delta_{j}
.
∎

\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\sqrt{t}}
\scriptstyle{-\sqrt{t}}

\overset{g_{*}}{\longmapsto}\

\scriptstyle{g_{*}h}
\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}

\Rightarrow\Var_{p}h=

\scriptstyle{D_{2}}

\scriptstyle{\sqrt{t}}

\scriptstyle{\partial W}
\scriptstyle{0}

\Rightarrow\Var_{p}h=
