Non-vanishing variations at a non-simple isolated singularity (
4.13
).

Example 4.13
.

Consider
4.11
with the partition
(A,B)=(D_{1},D_{2})
. As illustrated in
fig.

14
, the variation
\Var_{p}h
is a coboundary around
D_{1}
as expected (
1\in J_{\mathsf{s}}
). But it is non-zero, despite
\partial_{2}h=0
, so
\Var_{p}
does not factor through
\partial_{2}
in the domain. The case
(A,B)=(D_{2},D_{1})
demonstrates that the variation
\Var_{p}h
need not be a coboundary around the non-simple hypersurface
D_{2}
.

Remark 4.14
.

If there are two simple types
j,j^{\prime}\in J_{\mathsf{s}}
, then
4.12
states that
\nu=\Var_{p}h
can be written as a coboundary
\nu=\delta_{j}\alpha
around
A_{j}
, and also as a coboundary
\nu=\delta_{j^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime}
around
A_{j^{\prime}}
. This does
not
necessarily imply that
\nu=\delta_{j}\delta_{j^{\prime}}\alpha^{\prime\prime}
is an iterated coboundary
[
65
]
. Similarly, for two simple types
k,k^{\prime}\in K_{\mathsf{s}}
, it is not necessarily the case that
\Var_{p}h
depends only on the iterated boundary
\partial_{k}\partial_{k^{\prime}}h
.

Definition 4.15
.

Suppose that
p\subset\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
and
p^{\prime}\subset\pi^{-1}(t_{c}^{\prime})
are connected components of the critical sets in two fibres. Let
(J,K),(J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
denote the types of
p,p^{\prime}
and
(J_{\mathsf{s}},K_{\mathsf{s}}),(J^{\prime}_{\mathsf{s}},K_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime})
their simple parts. We then declare
p^{\prime}
and
p
to be
compatible
,

p^{\prime}\leqslant p,\qquad\text{if}\qquad K^{\prime}_{\mathsf{s}}\subseteq K\quad\text{and}\quad J_{\mathsf{s}}\subseteq J^{\prime}.

For simple pinches, this relation is just
4.2
, due to
4.10
.

Corollary 4.16
.

If
p^{\prime}\nleqslant p
, then
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0
.

Proof.

Combine the factorization (
4.7
) with the vanishing (
4.8
). For example, if
k\in K_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}\setminus K
, then
\iota_{p*}
factors through
\rho_{k*}
, which is annihilated by
\Var_{p^{\prime}}
.
∎

We can now formulate the general hierarchy principle. It enforces the vanishing of certain iterated variations, and it follows from the previous constraints on
\Var_{p^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{p}=0
, together with the localization
\Var_{\ell}=\sum_{p}\Var_{p}
discussed at the beginning of
section

4.4
.

Definition 4.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}
denote the set of Landau singularities, that is, the set of irreducible components of the Landau variety that have complex codimension one. Define a binary relation
\leqslant
on
\mathfrak{L}
as follows: For
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, let
P_{\ell}\subset\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
denote the critical set in a fibre over a generic point
t_{c}\in\ell
. Then set

\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
if and only if there exist
p\in P_{\ell}
and
p^{\prime}\in P_{\ell^{\prime}}
such that
p^{\prime}\leqslant p
.

Corollary 4.18
.

For any pair
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{L}
of Landau singularities, we have

\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0\quad\text{if}\quad\ell^{\prime}\nleqslant\ell.

5.
Polylogarithms

In this section, we illustrate the hierarchy principle with polylogarithms. The most generic situation, Aomoto polylogarithms, is the simplest—all critical points are linear simple pinches. In
section

5.2
, we also discuss the hierarchy for the dilogarithm, which involves non-isolated critical sets.

5.1.
Aomoto polylogarithms

A collection
Q=Q_{0}\cup\ldots\cup Q_{n}
of
n+1
hyperplanes in complex projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is called a simplex. We say that it is non-degenerate if the intersection
Q_{0}\cap\ldots\cap Q_{n}
is empty.

To a pair of non-degenerate simplices
Q
and
R
, we associate:

•

a differential form
\omega_{Q}
with simple poles on each
Q_{i}
that generates
H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{n}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\setminus Q)\cong\mathbb{C}
. In terms of linear equations
f_{i}=0
for
Q_{i}
,

(5.1)

\omega_{Q}=\mathrm{d}\log\left(\frac{f_{1}}{f_{0}}\right)\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}\log\left(\frac{f_{n}}{f_{0}}\right).

•

a generator
\sigma_{R}\in H_{n}(\mathbb{P}^{n},R)\cong\mathbb{Z}
corresponding to the real standard simplex
\{\sum_{i}u_{i}=1\}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{n+1}
, embedded as
\{[u_{0}:\cdots:u_{n}]\colon u_{i}\geq 0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
with boundary in
R
, in coordinates
u
where
R_{i}=\{u_{i}=0\}
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{R}
can be deformed to avoid
Q
, so that
\sigma_{R}
has a preimage
\sigma_{R}^{\prime}\in H_{n}(\mathbb{P}^{n}\setminus Q,R)
under the inclusion of pairs
(\mathbb{P}^{n}\setminus Q,R\setminus Q)\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{n},R)
.

The
Aomoto polylogarithm

[
2
, §2]
of weight
n
is the integral

\Lambda_{n}(Q,R)=\int_{\sigma_{R}^{\prime}}\omega_{Q}.

It depends on the choice of
\sigma_{R}^{\prime}
, hence
\Lambda_{n}
is a multivalued function on the space
T=(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{2n+2}
of pairs
t=(Q_{0},\ldots,Q_{n},R_{0},\ldots,R_{n})
of simplices. We identify hyperplanes
Q_{i}\subset X=\mathbb{P}^{n}
with points
[Q_{0i}:\cdots:Q_{ni}]
in the dual
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, and we can represent points
t\in T
as
(n+1)\times(2n+2)
matrices with column vectors
Q_{i}=(Q_{0i},\ldots,Q_{ni})^{\intercal},R_{j}=(R_{0j},\ldots,R_{nj})^{\intercal}
.
We obtain an arrangement
A\cup B
in the total space
Y=X\times T
, with the components

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}

\displaystyle=\{A_{0},\ldots,A_{n}\},\quad A_{i}=\big\{x_{0}Q_{0i}+\ldots+x_{n}Q_{ni}=0\big\}\subset Y,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}

\displaystyle=\{B_{0},\ldots,B_{n}\},\quad B_{j}=\big\{x_{0}R_{0j}+\ldots+x_{n}R_{nj}=0\big\}\subset Y.

Lemma 5.1
.

The divisor
A\cup B
has simple normal crossings.

Proof.

At every point
[p_{0}:\cdots:p_{n}]\in X
we can find
k
such that
p_{k}\neq 0
. If
(p,t)\in A_{i}
, there must be an index
r\neq k
such that
Q_{ri}\neq 0
, for otherwise, the equation of
A_{i}
would collapse at
p
to
p_{k}Q_{ki}=0
and imply
Q_{i}=0\notin\mathbb{P}^{n}
. So after relabelling, we may assume that
p_{0}=Q_{1i}=1
.
It follows that at
p
, and hence in a small neighbourhood, the functions

z^{(i)}=(z_{1}^{(i)},\ldots,z_{n}^{(i)})=(Q_{0i}+p_{1}+p_{2}Q_{2i}+\ldots+p_{n}Q_{ni},Q_{2i},\ldots,Q_{ni})

form a coordinate chart of the
\mathbb{P}^{n}
that parametrizes the hyperplane
Q_{i}
. This way, we can locally choose coordinates
(z^{(0)},\ldots,z^{(2n+1)})
on the product
T=(\mathbb{P}^{n})^{2n+2}
such that
A_{i}=\{z_{1}^{(i)}=0\}
and
B_{j}=\{z_{1}^{(n+1+j)}=0\}
.
∎

The strata
S
of the canonical stratification of
A\cup B
are indexed by pairs
I,J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,n\}
so that
\overline{S}=A^{I}\cap B^{J}=\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}\cap\bigcap_{j\in J}B_{j}
.
24
24

24

The actual strata are the subsets
S=A^{I}\cap B^{J}\setminus(\bigcup_{i\notin I}A_{i}\cup\bigcup_{j\notin J}B_{j})
.
It follows from considerations similar to
[
51
, §IV.4.4]
that the Landau variety can be computed from the closures of the strata:

L=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\pi(cS)=\bigcup_{S\in\mathfrak{S}}\pi(c\overline{S})=\bigcup_{I,J}\pi(c(A^{I}\cap B^{J})).

Let
t_{IJ}=(Q_{i\in I},R_{j\in J})
denote the submatrix of
t
, consisting of the columns in
I
and
J
. Write
V_{IJ}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
for the vector space spanned by these columns.

Lemma 5.2
.

A point
(x,t)
of the submanifold
A^{I}\cap B^{J}\subset Y
is critical precisely when the columns
t_{IJ}=(Q_{i\in I},R_{j\in J})
of
t
are linearly independent.

Proof.

The submanifold
S=A^{I}\cap B^{J}
has complex dimension
\dim T+n-k
with
k=\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert
. The fibre at
t
is the projectivization
S_{t}=\mathbb{P}(V_{IJ}^{\perp})\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
of

V_{IJ}^{\perp}=\bigcap_{v\in V_{IJ}}\{y\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}\colon x_{0}v_{0}+\ldots+x_{n}v_{n}=0\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n+1},

the annihilator of
V_{IJ}
. Therefore, at any
t\in T
, the fibre
S_{t}=A^{I}\cap B^{J}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)
is a complex manifold with complex dimension

(5.2)

\dim S_{t}=\dim V_{IJ}^{\perp}-1=n-\dim V_{IJ}.

The kernel of the differential
\mathrm{d}(\pi|_{S})
is the intersection (in
TY=TX\oplus TT
) of the vectors that are tangent to
S
(
TS
) and vertical (
TX
). In other words,
\ker\mathrm{d}(\pi|_{S})=T(S_{t})
is the tangent bundle of the fibre. Hence the image of
\mathrm{d}(\pi|_{S})
has dimension
\dim S-(n-\dim V_{IJ})=\dim T-(k-\dim V_{IJ})
and we have a submersion precisely when
\dim V_{IJ}=k
.
∎

The fibre of
A^{I}\cap B^{J}
over
t
has dimension
n-\dim V_{IJ}
, see (
5.2
). In particular, the fibre is empty, unless
\dim V_{IJ}\leq n
.

Corollary 5.3
.

The Landau variety of
\pi\colon(Y,A\cup B)\rightarrow T
is

L=\bigcup_{I,J}\big\{\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})<\min\{\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert,n+1\}\big\}.

For
k=\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert\leq n+1
, the subvariety
\{\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})<k\}\subset T
has codimension
n+1-(k-1)
: instead of
n+1
arbitrary entries, some column is a linear combination of the other
k-1
columns. Similarly, for
k\geq n+1
the codimension of the subvariety
\{\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})<n+1\}\subset T
is
k-n
.

Corollary 5.4
.

The components of
L
with codimension one in
T
are in bijection with pairs
I,J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,n\}
so that
\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert=n+1
. Their form is

\displaystyle\ell_{IJ}=\big\{\det(t_{IJ})=0\big\}\subset T.

In fact,
L
has no irreducible components of higher codimension. For if
k=\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert\leq n
, then
\{\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})<k\}\subset\ell_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}}
for any
I^{\prime}\supseteq I
,
J^{\prime}\supseteq J
such that
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert=n+1
. Similarly, if
k>n+1
, then any
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
,
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
with
\lvert I^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert J^{\prime}\rvert=n+1
provide
\{\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})<n+1\}\subset\ell_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}}
. It follows that

(5.3)

L=\bigcup_{I,J\colon\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert=n+1}\ell_{IJ}.

At a generic point of
\ell_{IJ}
, we have
\mathrm{rank}({t_{IJ}})=n
and the one-dimensional kernel of
t_{IJ}
defines the unique critical point
x
in the fibre (
5.2
). It is a linear pinch of type
(I,J)
.
The vanishing cell
\zeta_{IJ}
is a real
n
-simplex bounded by the hyperplanes
Q_{i}
(
i\in I
) and
R_{j}
(
j\in J
).

The cases where
I=\varnothing
or
J=\varnothing
have zero variation (
section

3.7
) and the corresponding loci
\ell_{\varnothing,0\ldots n}
and
\ell_{0\ldots n,\varnothing}
in
T
parametrize where
R
, respectively
Q
, become degenerate.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\varnothing\neq I,J\subseteq\{0,\ldots,n\}
with
\lvert I\rvert+\lvert J\rvert=n+1
. Then with the intersection number
N=\langle{\partial_{J}\zeta_{IJ}}\,|\,{\partial_{J}\sigma_{R}^{\prime}}\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(5.4)

\Var_{\ell_{IJ}}\Lambda_{n}(Q,R)=\pm N(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{|I|}\Lambda_{n-|I|}(\partial_{I}Q,R_{J}\cap Q^{I}).

Proof.

The iterated boundary
\partial_{I}\zeta_{IJ}
is a simplex in
Q^{I}
bounded by
R_{J}=\bigcup_{j\in J}R_{j}
. By the Picard-Lefschetz and residue formulas
eqs.

3.14
and
3.10
,

\Var_{\ell_{IJ}}\Lambda_{n}(Q,R)=\pm N\int_{\delta_{I}\partial_{I}\zeta_{IJ}}\omega_{Q}=\pm N\cdot(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{|I|}\int_{\partial_{I}\zeta_{IJ}}\Res_{I}\omega_{Q}

is an Aomoto polylogarithm of lower weight. Namely,
\Res_{I}
denotes the iteration of all residues
\Res_{A_{i}}
with
i\in I
, and we see from (
5.1
) that
\Res_{A_{i}}\omega_{Q}=(-1)^{i-1}\omega_{\partial_{i}Q}
.
∎

4.4
states that iterated variations
\Var_{\ell_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{IJ}}
are zero, unless
I^{\prime}\supset I
and
J^{\prime}\subset J
are strict inclusions. Together with the vanishing of
\Var_{\ell_{IJ}}
when
I=\varnothing
or
J=\varnothing
, it follows that any iteration
\Var_{\ell_{I_{k}J_{k}}}\circ\cdots\circ\Var_{\ell_{I_{1}J_{1}}}
of more than
n
variations of
\Lambda_{n}
is zero. This is also clear from (
5.4
).

Thus, the maximal iterated variations are
n
-fold, and correspond to an increasing sequence
I_{k}=\{\sigma(0),\ldots,\sigma(k-1)\}
and a decreasing sequence
J_{k}=\{\tau(k),\ldots,\tau(n)\}
. Such sequences encode permutations
\sigma,\tau
of
\{0,\ldots,n\}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

The
n
-fold iterated variations for all permutations
\sigma,\tau
are

(5.5)

\Var_{\ell_{\sigma(0)\ldots\sigma(n-1),\tau(n)}}\circ\cdots\circ\Var_{\ell_{\sigma(0),\tau(1)\ldots\tau(n)}}\Lambda_{n}=\pm(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{n}.

Proof.

Iterating
5.5
shows that the iterated variation is an integral multiple of
(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{n}
. It remains to show that all intersection numbers are
\pm 1
.

For the first intersection, note that the
n
-fold boundaries
\partial_{J_{1}}
of
\sigma_{R}^{\prime}
and the first vanishing cell
\zeta_{I_{1}J_{1}}
are, up to sign, the fundamental class of the (0-dimensional) corner
R^{J_{1}}
that both simplices share. In particular, in this case where
\lvert J\rvert=n
, the number
N=\pm 1
in (
5.4
) only depends on the orientation of
\sigma_{R}
, but not on the choice of lift
\sigma_{R}^{\prime}
.

The subsequent intersections are between the vanishing cycles
\pm\delta_{I_{k}}\partial_{I_{k}}\zeta_{I_{k}J_{k}}
of the
k
’th variation, and the
(k+1)
’th dual vanishing cycle. Again,

\langle{\partial_{J_{k+1}}\zeta_{I_{k+1}J_{k+1}}}\,|\,{\partial_{J_{k+1}}\delta_{I_{k}}\partial_{I_{k}}\zeta_{I_{k}J_{k}}}\rangle=\pm\langle{\partial_{J_{k+1}\sqcup I_{K}}\zeta_{I_{k+1}J_{k+1}}}\,|\,{\partial_{J_{k+1}\sqcup I_{K}}\zeta_{I_{k}J_{k}}}\rangle

is the self-intersection of the shared corner
Q^{I_{k}}\cap R^{J_{k+1}}
of both simplices.
∎

From their differential equations, obtained in
[
2
, §2]
, it follows that Aomoto polylogarithms are iterated integrals of the differential forms

\alpha_{IJ}=\mathrm{d}\log\big(\det(t_{IJ})\big).

This means that for any base point
t_{0}\in T\setminus L
, there exists a linear combination
W
of words (tensors) in
\alpha_{IJ}
’s, such that

(5.6)

\Lambda_{n}(t)=\Lambda_{n}(t_{0})+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}W.

Here the integral of a word is defined in terms of a path
\gamma
from
t_{0}
to
t
, as

\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\alpha_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}\gamma^{*}\alpha_{k}(\lambda_{k})\int_{0}^{\lambda_{k}}\gamma^{*}\alpha_{k-1}(\lambda_{k-1})\,\cdots\int_{0}^{\lambda_{2}}\gamma^{*}\alpha_{1}(\lambda_{1}).

In the representation (
5.6
), the variations around
\ell_{IJ}
can be computed using path concatenation with simple loops. The vanishing of any iteration of more than
n
variations, dictated by the hierarchy principle as explained above, shows that the words in
W
must have lengths
k\leq n
.

Corollary 5.7
.

The part of
W
including all words with length
n
is

(5.7)

\pm\sum_{\sigma,\tau}(\sgn\sigma)(\sgn\tau)\,\alpha_{\sigma(0)\ldots\sigma(n-1),\tau(n)}\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha_{\sigma(0),\tau(1)\ldots\tau(n)}.

Proof.

By (
5.5
), the words for all pairs of permutations of
\{0,\ldots,n\}
must have the coefficient
+1
or
-1
. Since
\Lambda_{n}(Q,R)
is odd under swapping two columns in
Q
or
R
, the relative signs are determined by the signs of the permutations. The overall sign depends on the orientation of
R
.
∎

The expression (
5.7
) is is called the
symbol
of
\Lambda_{n}
in
[
4
, (B.13)]
and was computed, in a slightly different form, already in
[
2
, Proposition §2.2]
.

5.2.
Dilogarithm

The dilogarithm is defined by
\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}t^{n}/n^{2}
for
\lvert t\rvert<1
. It extends to a multivalued holomorphic function on
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\}
, for example via the integral representation

(5.8)

\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=\int_{\sigma}\omega_{t}=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\frac{t\mathrm{d}x_{1}\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{1-tx_{1}x_{2}}.

To interpret this integral as in
section

2.4
, we can compactify the fibres to
X=(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
and set
Y=X\times T
over the parameter space
T=\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In coordinates
([x_{1}:1],[x_{2}:1],[t:1])\in Y
, the divisor
D=A\cup B
consists of:
25
25

25

In homogeneous coordinates
([x_{1}:y_{1}],[x_{2}:y_{2}],[t:s])
, the equations for
A
are
A_{i}=\{y_{i}=0\}
and
A_{3}=\{tx_{1}x_{2}=sy_{1}y_{2}\}
.

•

two hyperplanes
A_{1,2}
and a quadric
A_{3}
where the integrand has poles:

A_{i}=\{x_{i}=\infty\}\quad\text{and}\quad A_{3}=\{tx_{1}x_{2}=1\}

•

four hyperplanes that bound the integration domain
[0,1]^{2}\subset X
:

B_{i}=\{x_{i}=0\}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{2+i}=\{x_{i}=1\}

Figure 15.
Blowup
X\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
and divisors
A
(red) and
B
(blue) for the dilogarithm (
5.8
).

When
\lvert t\rvert<1
, the square
[0,1]^{2}
does not intersect
A
, hence it defines a homology class
\sigma\in H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
in those fibres. However, the projection

\pi\colon Y=(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{3}\longrightarrow T=\mathbb{P}^{1}

onto the third factor and the subset
D\subset Y
are not immediately amenable to Picard-Lefschetz theory, due to two problems:

(1)

The divisor
A\cup B
is not normal crossing, e.g.
B_{1}\cap A_{2}\subset A_{3}
.

(2)

The critical points are not always isolated, e.g. the entire stratum
B_{1}\cap A_{3}\setminus(A_{2}\cup B_{2}\cup B_{4})=\{x_{1}=0\}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{x_{2}=0,1,\infty\}\times\{t=\infty\}
is contained in a single fibre, so each point on this stratum is critical.

To solve the first problem, we separate the triple intersections by replacing
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
with the blow up
X\rightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
of the points
(0,\infty),(\infty,0)\in(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}
, see
fig.

15
. We add the exceptional divisors
A_{4,5}
to the arrangement
\mathfrak{A}
, so that

X\setminus\widetilde{A}_{t}\cong(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{2}\setminus A_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{B}\setminus\widetilde{A}\cong B\setminus A

where
\widetilde{A}
is the total transform of
A
and
\widetilde{B}
the strict transform of
B
. Having replaced the pair
((\mathbb{P}^{1})^{3}\setminus A,B\setminus A)
with
(Y\setminus\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B}\setminus\widetilde{A})
, we have achieved that
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B}
has normal crossings. Therefore, we can use the canonical stratification of
\widetilde{D}
to compute the Landau variety. We find
L=\{0,1,\infty\}
:

•

Over
t=0
, the quadric
A_{3}
(we omit
\sim
in the following) degenerates into a union of two lines,
\{x_{1}=\infty\}\cup\{x_{2}=\infty\}
. Their intersection point
p_{0}=(\infty,\infty,0)\in Y
would define a quadratic simple pinch,
26
26

26

Write
x_{1}=u+\mathrm{i}v
and
x_{2}=u-\mathrm{i}v
to bring
A_{3}=\{x_{1}x_{2}=1/t\}
into the form (
3.7
)
were it not for the fact that these two lines are precisely
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. Therefore, the critical set over
t=0
is not just the point
p_{0}
, but rather the entire union

P_{0}\cong(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\cap A_{3}=(A_{1}\cup A_{2})\cap\pi^{-1}(0).

•

Similarly, the critical set over
t=\infty
is the union

P_{\infty}\cong(B_{1}\cup B_{2})\cap A_{3}=(B_{1}\cup B_{2})\cap\pi^{-1}(\infty).

•

Over
t=1
, we have linear simple pinch
p_{1}=(1,1,1)
at the triple intersection
B_{3}\cap B_{4}\cap A_{3}=\{p_{1}\}
, with vanishing cell
\zeta_{p_{1}}
(see
fig.

15
).

Figure 16.
As
t
encircles the critical value 1, the relative cycle
\sigma=[0,1]^{2}
is deformed to avoid the quadric
\{x_{1}x_{2}={t}^{-1}\}
. After a full circle, the quadric has “cut out a strip” from
\sigma
, the resulting cycle is now homologous to
\sigma+\nu_{p_{1}}
(cf.
fig.

2
).

The Picard-Lefschetz theorem applies only to the linear pinch at
t=1
(the vanishing cycle is a cylinder, see
fig.

16
), but not to the non-isolated critical sets over
t=0,\infty
. However, we can still conclude constraints, e.g.

Lemma 5.8
.

\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{0}=\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{1}=\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{\infty}=0
.

Proof.

Consider the terminology from
section

4.4
. The critical set
P_{0}
over
t=0
has type
(\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},A_{4},A_{5}\},\{B_{3},B_{4}\})
, because it intersects all divisors except
B_{1,2}
. However, the simple type is just
(\{A_{3}\},\varnothing)
: removing
A_{3}
from the arrangement makes all critical points disappear, whereas removing any other divisor still leaves critical points behind (especially the quadratic pinch point
p_{0}\in P_{0}
of
A_{3}
on its own). So even without an explicit description of
\Var_{0}
, we conclude from
4.12
that it is a coboundary
\delta_{A_{3}}\mu
of some

(5.9)

\mu\in H_{1}(A_{3}\setminus(A_{4}\cup A_{5}),B_{3}\cup B_{4})_{t}.

Since
p_{1}
is a linear pinch, the variation at
t=1
factors through the iterated boundary in
B_{3}
and
B_{4}
(
3.24
). But
A_{3}\cap B_{3}\cap B_{4}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)=\varnothing
for all
t\neq 1
, so
\partial_{B_{3}}\partial_{B_{4}}\mu\in H_{-1}(A_{3}\cap B_{3}\cap B_{4})_{t}=0
and thus
\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{0}=0
.
27
27

27

It is trivial that
H_{-1}=0
(negative degree), but the argument as given here shows
H_{\bullet}(A_{3}\cap B_{3}\cap B_{4})_{t}=0
in all degrees and hence could generalize to other applications.
A very similar argument shows that
\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{\infty}=0
. Furthermore,
\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{1}=0
holds for every linear pinch (
section

4.2
).
∎

Of course, the monodromy of the dilogarithm is well known: with respect to the loops
\gamma_{0}(\tau)=e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}/2
and
\gamma_{1}(\tau)=1-\gamma_{0}(\tau)
based at
t_{0}=1/2
that generate
\pi_{1}(T\setminus L,t_{0})=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,1\},t_{0})
, one has

\Var_{0}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=0,\quad\Var_{1}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=-2\mathrm{i}\pi\log(t),\quad\Var_{0}\log(t)=2\mathrm{i}\pi.

From this, it follows easily that
\Var_{1}\circ\Var_{\ell}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=0
for
\ell=0,1,\infty
. The point of
5.8
is that such constraints can be derived from purely topological considerations.

Remark 5.9
.

Consider the cells
\sigma
and
\zeta_{p_{1}}
with the orientation
\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{2}
, then their intersection in the corner
B_{3}\cap B_{4}=\{(1,1)\}
gives

\Var_{1}\sigma=\langle{\widetilde{\nu}_{p_{1}}}\,|\,{\sigma}\rangle\nu_{p_{1}}=-\langle{\partial_{B_{3}B_{4}}\zeta_{p_{1}}}\,|\,{\partial_{B_{3}B_{4}}\sigma}\rangle\nu_{p_{1}}=-\nu_{p_{1}}

by
3.24
.
So with the additional input
\omega_{t}=\mathrm{d}\log(1-tx_{1}x_{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}\log(x_{1})
of the actual integrand, Picard-Lefschetz reproduces
\Var_{1}\operatorname{Li}_{2}(t)=-2\mathrm{i}\pi\log(t)
through the residue theorem (
3.10
):

\int_{\nu_{p_{1}}}\omega_{t}=2\mathrm{i}\pi\int_{\partial_{A_{3}}\zeta_{p_{1}}}\Res_{A_{3}}\omega_{t}=2\mathrm{i}\pi\int_{(1/t,1)}^{(1,1/t)}\mathrm{d}\log(x_{1})=2\mathrm{i}\pi\log(t).

Despite the non-isolated critical set
P_{0}
over
t=0
, the variation
\Var_{0}
behaves like the variation of the quadratic simple pinch
p_{0}\in A_{3}
, as if the hyperplanes
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
would not be part of the divisor
D
. Namely, for the quadratic pinch, we would expect that
\Var_{0}h=N(h)\nu_{p_{0}}
is an integer multiple of the vanishing cycle
\nu_{p_{0}}=\delta_{A_{3}}\eta
, where
\eta\in H_{1}(A_{3}\setminus(A_{4}\cup A_{5}),B_{3}\cup B_{4})_{t}
is the class of the vanishing sphere
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, embedded as the closed loop

\eta(\tau)=(x_{1}(\tau),x_{2}(\tau))=\big(e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}/\sqrt{t},e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}/\sqrt{t}\big).

The vanishing cycle
\nu_{p_{0}}\in H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
is the fundamental class of an embedded torus
\mathbb{S}^{1}\!\times\mathbb{S}^{1}\hookrightarrow(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{3}\setminus A
, which we can parametrize explicitly as

(x_{1}(\tau,\rho),x_{2}(\tau,\rho))=\big((1+e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\rho}/2)e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}/\sqrt{t},\,e^{-2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}/\sqrt{t}\big).

For an isolated quadratic pinch, we also would have
\Var_{0}\circ\Var_{0}=0
by (
4.6
).

Lemma 5.10
.

For the dilogarithm homology
H_{\bullet}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
, the variation
\Var_{0}
is an integer multiple of
\nu_{p_{0}}
, and
\Var_{0}\circ\Var_{0}=0
.

Proof.

As a quadric in
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we have
A_{3}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so the homology group in (
5.9
) is the group of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
punctured at two points, relative to two other points. This group has rank 2, generated by a path (in
A_{3}
) from
B_{4}
to
B_{3}
(
\partial_{A_{3}}\zeta_{p_{1}}
) and a closed loop (
\eta
). Therefore,

\Var_{0}h=N(h)\nu_{p_{0}}+M(h)\nu_{p_{1}}.

To see that
M(h)=0
, note that
H_{1}(B_{3}\setminus(A_{2}\cup A_{3}),B_{2}\cup B_{4})_{t}
has zero variation at
t=0
(linear pinch of type
(\{A_{2},A_{3}\},\varnothing)
). So
0=\Var_{0}\partial_{B_{3}}h=\partial_{B_{3}}\Var_{0}h=M(h)\alpha
, where the circle
\alpha
at the
B_{3}
-end of the cylinder
\nu_{p_{1}}
is non-zero in
H_{1}(B_{3}\setminus(A_{2}\cup A_{3}),B_{2}\cup B_{4})_{t}
. One way to see that
\Var_{0}\nu_{p_{0}}=0
is to compute
\int_{\nu_{p_{0}}}\omega_{t}=(2\mathrm{i}\pi)^{2}
with the residue theorem (see
5.9
).
∎

Remark 5.11
.

The homology classes
\nu_{p_{0}}
,
\nu_{p_{1}}
, and
\sigma
constitute a basis of
H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{3}
at every
t\neq 0,1,\infty
.

6.
Feynman integrals

We apply the results of
sections

2
,
3
and
4
to the study of Feynman integrals in the parametric representation
[
13
, §1]
. Such integrals are associated to a graph
G
(assumed to be connected) with
n+1
edges
E(G)
, and each edge
e\in E(G)
labels a coordinate
x_{e}
(called Schwinger/Feynman parameter) of
X=\mathbb{P}^{n}=\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{C}^{E_{G}})
. The real simplex

\sigma=\{[x_{0}:\cdots:x_{n}]\colon x_{e}>0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}

defines a relative cycle
\sigma\in H_{n}(X,B)
with boundary in the divisor

B=B_{0}\cup\ldots\cup B_{n},\quad\text{where}\quad B_{e}=\{x_{e}=0\}.

The first and second
Symanzik polynomials

[
7
]
of a graph,
\mathcal{U}\in\mathbb{Z}[x]
and
\mathcal{F}\in\mathbb{Z}[x,t]
, are homogeneous in
x
of degrees
h_{1}(G)
and
h_{1}(G)+1
, respectively.
28
28

28

In the notation of
[
13
]
,
\mathcal{U}=\Psi_{G}
and
\mathcal{F}=\Xi_{G}
.
Here
h_{1}(G)=\mathrm{rank}({H_{1}(G)})
is called the
loop number
of
G
, and it equals
|E(G)|-|V(G)|+1
in terms of the number
|V(G)|
of vertices. We denote the hypersurfaces defined by the Symanzik polynomials as

A=A_{1}\cup A_{2},\quad\text{where}\quad A_{1}=\{\mathcal{U}=0\}\quad\text{and}\quad A_{2}=\{\mathcal{F}=0\}.

The parameters
t
in
\mathcal{F}
have interpretations in physics as masses and scalar products of momenta of particles. These parameters range over a complex variety
T
. We set
Y=X\times T
and denote
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
the canonical projection.

Definition 6.1
.

The Landau variety
L_{G}
of a Feynman graph
G
is the minimal (
2.9
) Landau variety (
2.7
) of
D=A\cup B\subset Y
.

Our definition follows
[
12
]
, but other authors
[
45
,
29
]
use the phrase “Landau variety of
G
” for the subset
L^{\prime}\subseteq L_{G}
that arises as the Landau variety of the arrangement
A_{2}\cup B
without
A_{1}
(so-called
first type singularities
).
29
29

29

In dimensions
D<2d
, the singularities of
\mathcal{I}(t)
reduce from
L
to just
L^{\prime}
, because the form (
6.1
) has then no pole on
A_{1}
.

As explained in
section

2.4
, the Landau variety
L_{G}
is an upper bound on the singularities of integrals
\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\sigma}\omega
, where

(6.1)

\omega=\frac{P(x,t)\,\Omega}{\mathcal{U}(x)^{D/2-d}\mathcal{F}(x,t)^{d}}\in H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{n}(Y\setminus A,B)

is a rational differential
n
-form with poles contained in
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
. The exponents in the denominator are called
superficial degree of convergence

d
and
dimension of space-time

D
. We assume that
D/2
and
d
are integers, so that
\omega
is rational and in particular single-valued. The
n
-form

\Omega=\sum_{e}(-1)^{e}x_{e}\bigwedge_{k\neq e}\mathrm{d}x_{k}

is the projectivized Lebesgue measure, and
P(x,t)
is a polynomial, homogeneous in
x
of degree
d-E(G)+h_{1}(G)D/2
, so that
\omega
is homogeneous of degree zero. The precise form of
P
is irrelevant for us; it depends on the nature of the particles and interaction vertices encoded by the graph.

To analyse the singularities of an integral
\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\sigma}\omega_{t}
with the topological method (
section

2.4
), we must in the first step deform the simplex
\sigma
away from
A
, in order to define a class in
H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
. The resulting integral is then necessarily convergent, and it makes sense to discuss its singularities. We do not consider divergent Feynman integrals; those need to be renormalized first
[
14
]
.

However, convergence is not sufficient—indeed, it often happens that the boundary of
\sigma
intersects
A
, so that
\sigma
cannot define a class in
H_{n}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
.
This problem is solved by replacing the fibre
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with an iterated blowup
\rho\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{n}
. We will illustrate this construction explicitly in several examples (
sections

6.3
and
6.2
) and refer to
[
13
]
for details.
30
30

30

These blowups were first described by Boyling
[
10
, §3]
, but it appears that this work was largely ignored or unknown. They were rediscovered in
[
6
]
and generalized in
[
13
]
.
In summary:

•

The centres of the blowups are linear subspaces
\bigcap_{e\in\gamma}B_{e}
indexed by certain subgraphs
\gamma\subset E(G)
, called the
motic subgraphs
.

•

The total transform
\widetilde{B}=\rho^{-1}(B)
is a simple normal crossings divisor. Its components are (strict transforms of) the original
B_{e}
, plus the exceptional divisors (denoted
B_{\gamma}
, one for each motic subgraph).

•

In
X
, the closure
\widetilde{\sigma}=\overline{\rho^{-1}(\sigma\setminus B)}
of the preimage of the interior of
\sigma
, is a manifold with corners, called the
Feynman polytope
.
31
31

31

This is usually not the same as the “Feynman polytope”
\mathbf{S}_{G}
from
[
3
]
: the face
\widetilde{\sigma}\cap B_{\gamma}
of
\widetilde{\sigma}
has codimension one for
every
motic
\gamma
, but the face of
\mathbf{S}_{G}
corresponding to
\gamma
can have higher codimension, e.g. if
\gamma
is not biconnected.

This construction achieves that, for generic parameters
t
and convergent integrands (
6.1
), the Feynman polytope and the pullback of
\omega
define (co)homology classes

[\widetilde{\sigma}]\in H_{n}(Y\setminus\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B})_{t}\qquad\text{and}\qquad[\rho^{\ast}\omega]\in H^{n}_{\mathsf{dR}}(Y\setminus\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B})_{t}.

Here
\widetilde{A}\subset Y=X\times T
denotes the strict transform of
A
. We can now indeed apply
section

2.4
to study the monodromy of
\mathcal{I}(t)=\int_{\widetilde{\sigma}}\rho^{\ast}\omega
, purely in terms of the homology groups (called the
Feynman motive
)

(6.2)

H_{n}(Y\setminus\widetilde{A},\widetilde{B})_{t}=H_{n}(X\setminus\widetilde{A}_{t},\widetilde{B}).

To apply our machinery from
sections

2
and
3
straightforwardly, we require that
\widetilde{D}=\widetilde{A}\cup\widetilde{B}
is transverse. Although
\widetilde{B}
is transverse on its own, this property is easily lost once we add
\widetilde{A}
to the arrangement. Hence, further blowups become necessary, like for the dilogarithm (
section

5.2
). There is no general recipe for this step. We will discuss examples where
\widetilde{A}_{2}
is smooth but not transverse to
\widetilde{B}
(
sections

6.3
and
6.4
).

We begin this section with a discussion of the simplest case, massive one loop Feynman integrals. Here, no blowups whatsoever are needed, and all critical points are simple pinches. Hence we can apply the results of
sections

3.6
,
3.7
and
3.8
and find strong hierarchies according to
sections

4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
.

After that, we consider two rather degenerate examples: the massive sunrise and the massless triangle (
fig.

18
). We discuss the construction of their Feynman motives and demonstrate that several Landau singularities arise from
non-isolated critical points
(
section

4.4
), and that often the simple type is only
(\{A_{2}\},\varnothing)
. As a consequence, a more subtle analysis is needed to derive hierarchy constraints, and we give examples of such.

In
section

6.4
we study the ice cream cone graph and we prove hierarchy constraints in this notoriously subtle case. Note that these subtleties are a general phenomenon rather than an exception, as was already noted in
[
10
]
. We suggest a modification of the Feynman motive by additional blowups, to resolve non-transverse intersections (
6.14
).

6.1.
Massive one loop integrals

We consider the geometry associated to generic (fully massive) 1-loop integrals with
n+1
external and internal edges. The case
n=1
was discussed in
section

1.8.2
.

The parameters
t=(m,s)\in T=\mathbb{C}^{(n+1)(n+2)/2}
consist of
n+1
masses
m_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
of internal edges, and
n(n+1)/2
invariants
s_{ij}=s_{ji}=-(p_{i}+\ldots+p_{j-1})^{2}\in\mathbb{C}
formed from the incoming momenta
p_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{D}
at each vertex.
32
32

32

We treat the invariants
s_{ij}
as unconstrained parameters, that is, we assume that
D\geq n
so that there are no relations among the
s_{ij}
other than
s_{ii}=0
and
s_{ij}=s_{ji}
.
We can identify
T
with the space of all symmetric
(n+1)\times(n+1)
matrices
M
, with entries

M_{ij}=\begin{cases}m_{i}^{2}&\text{ if }i=j,\\
\frac{m_{i}^{2}+m_{j}^{2}+s_{ij}}{2}&\text{ if }i\neq j.\end{cases}

The two Symanzik polynomials are
\mathcal{U}=x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n+1}
and
\mathcal{F}=x^{\intercal}Mx
. They define a hyperplane
A_{1}=\{\mathcal{U}=0\}
and a quadric
A_{2}=\{\mathcal{F}=0\}
. Together with the
(n+1)
coordinate hyperplanes
B_{i}=\{x_{i}=0\}
, we obtain a simple normal crossings divisor
A\cup B\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}\times T
.

The quadric
A_{2t}=A_{1}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}
is smooth precisely when
\det M\neq 0
. The critical values of
\pi|_{A_{2}}
form the
leading Landau singularity of the first type
,

\ell_{G}^{\mathcal{F}}=\{\det M=0\}\subset T.

Over a generic point
t
of
\ell_{G}^{\mathcal{F}}
, the rank of
M
is
n
, and the one-dimensional kernel
\mathbb{C}p
of
M
defines the unique singular point
[p]\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
of
A_{2t}
. This point is a quadratic simple pinch, and the corresponding vanishing cycle is an
\mathbb{S}^{1}
-bundle over the vanishing sphere
\cong\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset A_{2t}
(cf.
section

3.5
).

The
leading singularity of the second type
refers to the critical values of the intersection
A_{12}=A_{1}\cap A_{2}
. Its fibre over
t
is

A_{12}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{n+1}=0\}\cap\{x^{\intercal}Sx=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}

and depends only on the momentum part
S=M|_{m=0}=(s_{ij}/2)
of
M
, but not the masses. A point
[x]\in A_{12}
is critical if either, the normal vectors
\mathbf{1}=(1,\ldots,1)^{\intercal}
and
Sx
of
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
become collinear, or if
\{x^{\intercal}Sx=0\}
is singular. So either
\det S=0
, or
S
is invertible and
x\propto S^{-1}\mathbf{1}
. In the latter case, for
x
to lie on
A_{12}
, we need
\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}S^{-1}\mathbf{1}=0
.
Thus, using the
Cayley matrix

S^{\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}\\
\mathbf{1}&S\\
\end{pmatrix}

with determinant
(\mathbf{1}^{\intercal}S^{-1}\mathbf{1})\cdot\det S
, the leading second type singularity is
33
33

33

\det S^{\prime}
is proportional to the Gram determinant
[
42
, Appendix A.1]
.

\ell_{G}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}=\left\{\det S^{\prime}=0\right\}\subset T.

Over a generic point of this component, there is a unique critical point. It is a quadratic simple pinch of type
(\{A_{1},A_{2}\},\varnothing)
.
The corresponding vanishing cycle is an
\mathbb{S}^{1}\!\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
-bundle over the vanishing sphere
\cong\mathbb{S}^{n-2}\subset A_{12}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)
.

All other strata lie in
B
. Since
\mathcal{U}|_{x_{e}=0}
and
\mathcal{F}|_{x_{e}=0}
are the Symanzik polynomials of the graph
G/e
where the edge
e
is contracted, the critical values of
A_{2}\cap B^{I}
and
A_{12}\cap B^{I}
are the leading singularities of the quotient graph
G/I
, where
B^{I}=\bigcap_{e\in I}B_{e}
for any subset
I
of edges. To summarize:

Proposition 6.2
.

The full Landau variety of a generic one-loop graph
G
is

(6.3)

L_{G}=\bigcup_{I\subsetneq E(G)}\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}}\cup\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}.

All components of
L_{G}
are simple pinch components (
3.14
).

If we denote by
M_{I}
and
S^{\prime}_{I}
the submatrices without the rows and columns indexed by
I
, then
\ell^{\mathcal{F}}_{G/I}=\{\det M_{I}=0\}
and
\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/I}=\{\det S_{I}^{\prime}=0\}
. We exclude
\lvert I\rvert=n+1
in (
6.3
) because
B_{1}\cap\ldots\cap B_{n+1}=\varnothing
. In the case
\lvert I\rvert=n
, the graph
G/I
consists of a single edge, say
i
, and we have no 2nd type singularity:
\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/I}=\varnothing
because
A_{1}\cap B^{I}
is empty. The first type singularity is

(6.4)

\ell^{\mathcal{F}}_{G/I}=\{m_{i}^{2}=0\}\quad\text{for}\quad I=E(G)\setminus\{i\}.

For
\lvert I\rvert=n-1
, the quotient
G/I
is a graph with two edges (c.f.
section

1.8.2
). In particular, the second type singularity is

(6.5)

\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}=\{s_{ij}=0\}\quad\text{for}\quad I=E(G)\setminus\{i,j\}.

The components (
6.4
) and (
6.5
) come from strata that are
(n+1)
-fold intersections, hence linear simple pinches. All other components of
L_{G}
have quadratic simple pinches.

\textstyle{\ell_{G/12}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G/13}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G/23}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G/1}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G/2}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G/3}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell_{G}^{\mathcal{F}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Figure 17.
The hierarchy of the massive triangle integral. An arrow
\ell\rightarrow\ell^{\prime}
indicates the compatibility
\ell^{\prime}\leqslant\ell
of the corresponding variations. If there is no directed path from
\ell
to
\ell^{\prime}
, then
\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0
(
section

4.3
).

The hierarchy relation defined in
section

4
is antisymmetric in this case, and illustrated in
eq.

1.5
for
n=1
and in
fig.

17
for
n=2
. The reflexive elements (indicated by a self-loop) are
\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}}
with
n-\lvert I\rvert
odd, and
\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}
with
n-\lvert I\rvert
even, according to (
4.6
).
This hierarchy implies:

(1)

The variation around a singularity of
G/I
, followed by a variation of a graph
G/J
that contracts an edge that is not in
I
, must vanish:

\left(\Var_{\ell_{G/J}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}}\ \text{or}\ \Var_{\ell_{G/J}^{\mathcal{F}}}\right)\circ\left(\Var_{\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}}\ \text{or}\ \Var_{\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}}}\right)=0\quad\text{whenever}\quad J\not\subseteq I.

(2)

The variation of any second type singularity, followed by the variation of any first type singularity, must vanish:

\Var_{\ell_{G/J}^{\mathcal{F}}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{G/I}^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}}=0\quad\text{for all}\quad I,J\subsetneq E(G).

(3)

If
n-\lvert I\rvert
is even, then
\Var_{\ell^{\mathcal{F}}_{G/I}}\circ\Var_{\ell^{\mathcal{F}}_{G/I}}=0
.
If
n-\lvert I\rvert
is odd, then
\Var_{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/I}}\circ\Var_{\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G/I}}=0
.

We hence recover the results of
[
10
, eq.’s (2.7), (2.12–14)]
. For an analysis of one-loop integrals in (compactified)
momentum space
, see
[
9
,
47
]
. The fundamental group of
T\setminus L
is studied in
[
53
]
.

Remark 6.3
.

The integrals
\int_{\sigma}\Omega/\mathcal{F}^{D/2}
with
D=n+1
compute the volume of a spherical simplex and have been studied in great detail. Since
\mathcal{U}
is absent, the singularities of 2nd type disappear. It was shown in
[
1
]
that these integrals can be written as iterated integrals of logarithmic one-forms. As mentioned in
section

5.1
, the corresponding symbol encodes the maximal iterated variations. We verified that the symbol
[
1
, eq. (3.15)]
is compatible with the hierarchy. In fact, as for the Aomoto polylogarithm, the variations can be computed explicitly using Picard-Lefschetz theory, see
[
4
, §6]
.

6.2.
Massless triangle

Setting a mass to zero is a singularity of the generic one-loop integral. The corresponding linear pinch (
6.4
) indicates that the integration simplex
\sigma
now intersects the quadric
A_{2}=\{\mathcal{F}=0\}
in a corner.

p_{2}
p_{3}
p_{1}
3
2
1
p_{1}

Figure 18.
The triangle graph (left) and the sunrise graph (right).

For the massless triangle graph (
fig.

18
) with parameters
t=(p_{1}^{1},p_{2}^{2},p_{3}^{2})\in T=\mathbb{C}^{3}
and the Symanzik polynomials

\mathcal{U}=x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{F}=-p_{1}^{2}x_{2}x_{3}-p_{2}^{2}x_{1}x_{3}-p_{3}^{2}x_{1}x_{2},

this situation is illustrated in
fig.

19
. The Feynman motive is obtained from the blowup
X\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
of the three intersection points
34
34

34

The three subgraphs consisting of two edges are the “motic graphs” from
[
13
]
.

A_{2}\cap B=(B_{2}\cap B_{3})\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{3})\cup(B_{1}\cap B_{2})=\{[1:0:0],[0:1:0],[0:0:1]\}

of
B=B_{1}\cup B_{2}\cup B_{3}
. The total transform
\widetilde{B}
of
B
includes the exceptional divisors
B_{ij}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
above
x_{i}=x_{j}=0
and thus has 6 components in total. It bounds the Feynman polytope
\widetilde{\sigma}
, which is a hexagon, see
fig.

19
.

Figure 19.
The geometry of the Feynman motive of the massless triangle, in a generic fibre.

The blowups do not affect the existing divisor components, and we denote their strict transforms in
Y=X\times T
still as
B_{i}
and
A_{j}
. All divisor components are smooth,
A_{i}\cong B_{j}\cong B_{jk}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and intersect transversely.

An inspection of the projection
\pi\colon Y\rightarrow T
and the canonical stratification of
D=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup\widetilde{B}
reveals a Landau variety with 4 components:

(6.6)

L_{G}=\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{3}\cup\ell_{\delta}.

The first three components
\ell_{i}=\{p_{i}^{2}=0\}
constitute a singularity of the 1st type. Namely, when
p_{i}^{2}=0
, then
A_{2}\cap Y_{t}=B_{i}\cup\{p_{k}^{2}x_{j}+p_{j}^{2}x_{k}\}
degenerates into a union of two lines, where
\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}
. Since one of these lines is
B_{i}
, the critical set over
\ell_{i}
is not just the simple quadratic pinch point of
A_{2}
(e.g.
[0:p_{2}^{2}:-p_{3}^{2}]
for
i=1
), but the entire line
B_{i}
. Note that
A_{2}
and
B_{i}
do not intersect for generic
t
, only when
p_{i}^{2}=0
. The critical set over
\ell_{i}
is therefore not isolated and can be denoted as

P_{i}=c(A_{2}\cap B_{i})=B_{i}\cap\ell_{i}=\{x_{i}=0\}\times\{p_{i}^{2}=0\}\subset X\times T.

The 2nd type singularity
\ell_{\delta}=\{\delta=0\}
arises from the coincidence of the two intersection points

(A_{1}\cap A_{2})_{t}=\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}
.
Explicitly, it is defined by

\delta=p_{1}^{4}+p_{2}^{4}+p_{3}^{4}-2p_{1}^{2}p_{2}^{2}-2p_{1}^{2}p_{3}^{2}-2p_{2}^{2}p_{3}^{2},

because
Q_{1,2}=[-p_{1}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}\pm\sqrt{\delta}:p_{1}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}\mp\sqrt{\delta}:2p_{3}^{2}]
degenerate to the same point when
\delta=0
.
35
35

35

In terms of the notation from
section

6.1
,
\ell_{\delta}=\ell^{\mathcal{F}\mathcal{U}}_{G}
and
\delta=\det S^{\prime}
.
This is a quadratic simple pinch, with the vanishing sphere
\{Q_{1},Q_{2}\}\cong\mathbb{S}^{0}
.

The Picard-Lefschetz formula does not apply due to the non-isolated critical set
P_{i}
, but the variation still localizes in the sense of
3.7
into some tubular neighbourhood
W
of
B_{i}
. The type of
P_{i}
is
(\{A_{1},A_{2}\},\{B_{i},B_{ij},B_{ik}\})
, but only
A_{2}
is simple in the sense of
4.9
(c.f.
4.11
). So without further analysis, all components of (
6.6
) have the same simple type, and we do not get any hierarchy constraints from
4.17
.

However, with a more detailed analysis, we can (as in
5.8
and
5.10
) nevertheless still derive non-trivial constraints:

Lemma 6.4
.

\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{i}}=\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{\delta}}=0
for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Proof.

The type of
P_{i}
shows that the localization in
W
factors
\Var_{\ell_{i}}
through
H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B_{i}\cup B_{ij}\cup B_{ik})_{t}
. Since
A_{2}
is simple, and
A_{2t}=A_{2}\cap Y_{t}
does not intersect
B_{i}
, we know that
\Var_{\ell_{i}}
must be a coboundary
\delta_{A_{2}}
of some class in

H_{1}(A_{2}\setminus A_{1},B_{ij}\cup B_{ik})_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

This group is generated by a path
\gamma_{i}
in
A_{2t}\setminus A_{1}
between the points
B_{ij}\cap A_{2t}
and
B_{ik}\cap A_{2t}
, and a closed loop
\eta=\delta_{A_{1}}Q_{1}=-\delta_{A_{1}}Q_{2}
. Note that

\nu_{\delta}=\delta_{A_{2},A_{1}}\partial_{A_{2},A_{1}}\zeta_{\delta}=2\delta_{A_{2}}\delta_{A_{1}}Q_{1}=2\delta_{A_{2}}\eta\in H_{2}(Y\setminus A)_{t}

is the vanishing cycle of the quadratic pinch over
\ell_{\delta}
. The image of
\Var_{\ell_{\delta}}
and
\Var_{\ell_{i}}
is thus contained in the span of
\nu_{\delta}/2
and the cylinder

\nu_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\delta_{A_{2}}\gamma_{i}\in H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B_{ij}\cup B_{ik}\cup B_{jk})_{t}.

Since
B_{i}
is now absent, the critical point over
\ell_{i}
is an isolated, quadratic simple pinch
q_{i}
(e.g.
q_{1}=[0:p_{2}^{2}:-p_{3}^{2}]
). The claim,
\Var_{\ell_{i}}\nu_{\delta}=\Var_{\ell_{i}}\nu_{i}=0
, thus follows from the vanishing of the intersection numbers

\langle{\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\nu_{\delta}}\rangle=-2\langle{\partial_{A_{2}}\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\eta}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\langle{\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\nu_{i}}\rangle=-\langle{\partial_{A_{2}}\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\gamma_{i}}\rangle

with the vanishing cell
\zeta_{i}
of the quadratic pinch. This cell is localized in a neighbourhood of the critical point
q_{i}
. The intersection with
\nu_{\delta}
vanishes, because the latter can be localized near
Q_{1}
or
Q_{2}
, and even over a critical value
t_{c}\in\ell_{i}
(and hence, by continuity, for nearby
t\notin L
), neither point is equal to
q_{i}
. For example,
q_{1}=[0:p_{2}^{2}:-p_{3}^{2}]
is not equal to

Q_{1}|_{t_{c}}=[0:1:-1]\quad\text{or}\quad Q_{2}|_{t_{c}}=[p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}:-p_{2}^{2}:p_{3}^{2}],

unless
t_{c}
lies in the intersection
\ell_{1}\cap\ell_{\delta}=\{p_{1}^{2}=0\}\cap\{p_{2}^{2}=p_{3}^{2}\}
.

(A\cup B_{12}\cup B_{13}\cup B_{23})_{t\in\ell_{1}}

A_{2}\cap(A_{1}\cap\widetilde{B})_{t\in\ell_{1}}

A_{2}\cap(A_{1}\cap\widetilde{B})_{t\notin\ell_{1}}

Figure 20.
The reduced divisor (without
B
) of the massless triangle in a critical fibre (left), the degenerate quadric
A_{2t}=B_{1}\cup C_{1}
(middle), the quadric
A_{2t}
in a nearby fibre (right).

The embedded vanishing sphere
\mathbb{S}^{1}\subset A_{2t}
with class
[\mathbb{S}^{1}]=\partial_{A_{2}}\zeta_{i}
cuts the quadric
A_{2t}\cong\mathbb{S}^{2}
over
t\notin L
into two hemispheres. Over
\ell_{i}
, these hemispheres degenerate into the two components
B_{i}
and
C_{i}=\{p_{k}^{2}x_{j}+p_{j}^{2}x_{k}=0\}
of
A_{2t_{c}}
. Since both endpoints of
\gamma_{i}
lie on the same component (
B_{i}
) in the critical fibre, they must lie on the same hemisphere for
t
near, but not on,
\ell_{i}
. Hence the path
\gamma_{i}
can be homotoped to lie within that hemisphere and thus does not intersect the vanishing sphere. This is illustrated in
fig.

20
.
∎

In summary, for the massless triangle, the double variations can be computed using Picard-Lefschetz theory: after the first variation, no cycle has boundary in
B
, hence we can drop
B
from the arrangement such that for the second variation, the critical points are quadratic simple pinches.

6.4
covers all constraints of the form
\Var_{\ell^{\prime}}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0
. In particular,

(6.7)

\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{j}}\neq 0

for distinct
i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Namely,
\langle{\zeta_{i}}\,|\,{\nu_{j}}\rangle=\pm 1
and so
\Var_{\ell_{i}}\nu_{j}=\pm\nu_{\delta}/2
.
36
36

36

The path
\gamma_{j}
must cross the vanishing sphere, because the endpoints of
\gamma_{j}
lie in different hemispheres, see
fig.

20
and the last part of the proof of
6.4
.

Furthermore, although this does not follow directly from Picard-Lefschetz theory, we can choose
\gamma_{i}
so that
\Var_{\ell_{i}}\widetilde{\sigma}=\nu_{i}
, and therefore

\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{j}}\widetilde{\sigma}=\pm\nu_{\delta}/2.

Remark 6.5
.

If one interprets
\Var_{\ell_{i}}
as only the singularity of the massless bubble quotient graph
G/i
, then (
6.7
) would violate the hierarchy principle of absorption integrals
[
49
,
29
]
. However, there is no contradiction here, because the latter does not apply to Feynman integrals to begin with (the integration domain
\widetilde{\sigma}
has boundary), and also because the masses would be required to be non-zero. In addition, the Landau components
\ell_{i}
also parametrize the leading singularity of the full triangle graph, hence
\Var_{\ell_{i}}
cannot be attributed to
G/i
alone (in our terminology,
B_{i}
is not simple).

Remark 6.6
.

The relations from
6.4
and (
6.7
) agree with explicit computations of the triangle integral in terms of polylogarithms
[
8
, §6.2.2]
.

6.3.
Massive sunrise

The sunrise graph with unequal masses depends on four parameters
t=(p^{2},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3})\in T=\mathbb{C}^{4}
. Its Symanzik polynomials

\mathcal{U}=x_{2}x_{3}+x_{1}x_{3}+x_{1}x_{2},\quad\mathcal{F}=(m_{1}^{2}x_{1}+m_{2}^{2}x_{2}+m_{3}^{2}x_{3})\mathcal{U}-p^{2}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}

define a smooth quadric and cubic, respectively, in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Both of them intersect in the corners
x_{i}=x_{j}=0
of the simplex
\{x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}=0\}
. The Feynman motive is defined by the same blowup
X^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
as in
section

6.2
. It achieves the separation of the hexagon
\widetilde{\sigma}
(Feynman polytope), which has boundary in

B^{\prime}=B^{\prime}_{1}\cup B^{\prime}_{2}\cup B^{\prime}_{3}\cup B^{\prime}_{12}\cup B^{\prime}_{13}\cup B^{\prime}_{23},

from the strict transforms
A_{1}^{\prime},A_{2}^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}\times T
of
\{\mathcal{U}=0\}
and
\{\mathcal{F}=0\}
. Here we denote
B_{i}^{\prime}
the strict transform of
\{x_{i}=0\}
and
B^{\prime}_{ij}
the exceptional divisors over the points
\{x_{i}=x_{j}=0\}\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

\subset X\quad\longrightarrow\quad X^{\prime}\supset

Figure 21.
Divisors
A\cup B\subset X
and
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}
of the sunrise graph.

However, the divisor
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}\subset X^{\prime}\times T
is still not normal crossing, because on each
B^{\prime}_{ij}
, the intersections with
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime}
coincide (see
fig.

21
). For example, in local coordinates
x=[u:uv:1]
where
B^{\prime}_{12}=\{u=0\}
, one finds

A_{1}^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}_{12}=A_{2}^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}_{12}=\{v=-1\}\subset B^{\prime}_{12}.

This is resolved by an additional blowup
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
of the fibre in the three points
A_{1}^{\prime}\cap B^{\prime}_{ij}
. Let
A_{ij}
denote the corresponding exceptional divisors, and write
A_{i}
,
B_{i}
,
B_{ij}
for the strict transforms of
A_{i}^{\prime}
,
B_{i}^{\prime}
, and
B_{ij}^{\prime}
. We set

A=A_{1}\cup A_{2}\cup A_{12}\cup A_{13}\cup A_{23}

and analyse the normal crossings divisor
A\cup B\subset Y=X\times T
with respect to the projection
\pi\colon Y\to T
. This is illustrated in
fig.

21
. Note in particular that, in a generic fibre,
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
do not intersect.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
\ell_{p}=\{p^{2}=0\}
,
\ell_{i}=\{m_{i}^{2}=0\}
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
, and for any pair of signs
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,-1\}
set
\ell_{\alpha\beta}=\{p^{2}=(m_{1}+\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{3})^{2}\}
.
37
37

37

\ell_{++}
is the
leading threshold
, whereas
\ell_{+-}
,
\ell_{-+}
,
\ell_{--}
are called
pseudo-thresholds
.

The Landau variety of the sunrise graph is

L_{G}=\ell_{p}\cup\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\cup\ell_{3}\cup\ell_{++}\cup\ell_{+-}\cup\ell_{-+}\cup\ell_{--}.

Proof.

The Landau variety contains in particular the critical values of the cubic
A_{2}
. Those are determined by the parameters
t
such that the fibre
A_{2t}=A_{2}\cap\pi^{-1}(t)
is singular. This discriminant of
\mathcal{F}
is

p^{2}m_{1}^{2}m_{2}^{2}m_{3}^{2}\prod_{\alpha,\beta=\pm}\big(p^{2}-(m_{1}+\alpha m_{2}+\beta m_{3})^{2}\big)

and produces
L_{G}
. It remains to verify that the intersections of
A_{2}
with (one or several) of the other components
A_{1},A_{ij},B_{i},B_{ij}
do not generate additional components. This is a routine calculation.
∎

In order to describe the variations, we need to determine the critical sets
P\subset\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
over a generic critical value
t_{c}\in L
. These are as follows:

•

Over
\ell_{\alpha\beta}
,
P
is a quadratic simple pinch of type
(\{A_{2}\},\varnothing)
. This point is the unique node (double point) of the cubic
A_{2t}
. In coordinates:

P=\{[\alpha\beta m_{2}m_{3}:\beta m_{1}m_{3}:\alpha m_{1}m_{2}]\}.

•

Over
\ell_{i}
, the cubic
A_{2t}
acquires two nodes and splits into two
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. For example, in the chart
x=[u:uv:1]
with
B_{12}=\{u=0\}
,

A_{2t}\cap B_{12}=\{(1+v)m_{3}^{2}=0\}

is generically a point; but for
t_{c}\in\ell_{3}
, it contains the entire hyperplane
B_{12}\subset A_{2t_{c}}
. The other factor of
A_{2t_{c}}
(solve
\mathcal{F}
for
x_{3}
) is the image of

\mathbb{P}^{1}\ni[y_{1}:y_{2}]\mapsto\left[y_{1}:y_{2}:\tfrac{y_{1}y_{2}(m_{1}^{2}y_{1}+m_{2}^{2}y_{2})}{p^{2}y_{1}y_{2}-(y_{1}+y_{2})(m_{1}^{2}y_{1}+m_{2}^{2}y_{2})}\right].

The two components of
A_{2t_{c}}
meet in the two roots
v=y_{2}/y_{1}
of
p^{2}v=(1+v)(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}v)
. These double points are quadratic simple pinches of
A_{2}
; however, since the factor
B_{12}
is in the arrangement, the critical set is not isolated:

P=B_{jk}\cap\pi^{-1}(t_{c})\quad\text{with type}\quad(\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{jk}\},\{B_{j},B_{k},B_{jk}\}).

•

Over
t_{c}\in\ell_{p}
, the cubic also degenerates into two components,
A_{2t_{c}}=(A_{1}\cup\{x_{1}m_{1}^{2}+x_{2}m_{2}^{2}+x_{3}m_{3}^{2}=0\})_{t_{c}}
. These components intersect in two quadratic simple pinches of
A_{2}
, and the critical set is

P=A_{1}\cap\pi^{-1}(t_{c})\quad\text{with type}\quad(\{A_{1},A_{2},A_{12},A_{13},A_{23}\},\varnothing).

The simple type of every critical set is just
(\{A_{2}\},\varnothing)
, because the divisor
A_{2}\subset Y
alone already produces the entire Landau variety. As for the massless triangle, we thus get no hierarchy constraints from
4.17
, at least not between different variations. For the simple pinches, we do get that

(6.8)

\Var_{\ell_{\alpha\beta}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{\alpha\beta}}=0

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,-1\}
, from (
4.6
).
Furthermore, with a very similar argument as in the proof of
6.4
, one can show that

(6.9)

\Var_{\ell_{i}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{i}}=0

for all
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Note in particular that, by the localization near the critical set, the variations of any class
h\in H_{2}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}
take values in

\displaystyle\Var_{\ell_{\alpha\beta}}h,\Var_{\ell_{p}}h

\displaystyle\in\delta_{A_{2}}H_{1}(A_{2}\setminus(A_{12}\cup A_{13}\cup A_{23}),\varnothing)_{t}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\Var_{\ell_{i}}h

\displaystyle\in\delta_{A_{2}}H_{1}(A_{2}\setminus(A_{12}\cup A_{13}\cup A_{23}),B_{j}\cup B_{k})_{t}.

So after any first variation, no cycle has boundary in
B_{12}\cup B_{13}\cup B_{23}
, so we can drop those from the arrangement. Thus the critical points over
\ell_{i}
for the second variation are just the two nodes, hence subject to the Picard-Lefschetz theorem, and we can proceed as in
6.4
to confirm (
6.9
).

Although

The Symanzik polynomials vanish to first order on

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}\mathcal{U}_{G/\gamma}\equiv(x_{1}+x_{2})(x_{3}+x_{4})\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\prime}\cap B_{12}^{\prime}

are unions of lines (

. Note also that

intersect not in a point but in a line—another witness of non-transversality.

Similarly, we identify two further non-transverse intersections. Instead of a point,

.

Remark 6.9
.

The component
B_{3}^{\prime}
is the blowup of
\mathbb{P}^{2}\cong\{x_{3}=0\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{3}
in the 3 corners of

in

.
Each of the 3 lines (

in 3 further points, that is,
