with our additional blowups.

Lemma 6.10
.

The divisor
D=A\cup B\subset Y
has simple normal crossings.

Proof.

In coordinates
x=[u:uv:x_{3}:1]
where
B_{12}^{\prime}=\{u=0\}
, we are blowing up
\{u=0\}\cap\{v=-1\}
. Hence in the chart
x=[\lambda w:\lambda w(\lambda-1):x_{3}:1]
, we see
B_{12}=\{w=0\}
and
A_{12}=\{\lambda=0\}
. The strict transform

A_{1}=\{1+x_{3}-w+w\lambda=0\}

intersects
B_{12}
in the horizontal line
\{x_{3}=-1\}
, whereas the previously vertical line has moved onto
A_{12}\cap A_{1}=\{w=1+x_{3}\}
. In the same coordinates, the strict transform
A_{2}
of
A_{2}^{\prime}
reads

0=-p_{1}^{2}x_{3}+(1+x_{3}-w)(m_{3}^{2}x_{3}+m_{4}^{2})+w(p_{2}^{2}+x_{3}p_{3}^{2})+w\lambda\cdot(\cdots).

We see that
A_{2}\cap B_{12}
is smooth, namely the union of two non-intersecting lines (parametrized by
\lambda
) with
w=0
and
x_{3}
a root of
\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}
. The other intersection is also smooth, namely the quadric
39
39

39

We note that the coefficient of
w
is equal to
\mathcal{F}_{34}=x_{3}(m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})+x_{4}(m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2})
, the second Symanzik polynomial of the subgraph
\{3,4\}
.

(6.13)

A_{2t}\cap A_{12}=\{\lambda=0\}\cap\{w(m_{3}^{2}x_{3}+m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}x_{3})=\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}\}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}.

This geometry is illustrated in
fig.

24
. Similarly, one can verify the transversality of
D
near
A_{134}
,
B_{134}
,
A_{234}
, and
B_{234}
.
∎

Since we blow up subsets of
A_{2}^{\prime}
, these blowups do not change the complements
Y\setminus A\cong Y^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime}
and thus we have a canonical identification

H_{3}(Y\setminus A,B)_{t}=H_{3}(Y^{\prime}\setminus A^{\prime},B^{\prime})_{t}.

Because
A\cup B
has normal crossings, we can now apply our results and analyse the Landau variety and variation constraints using the canonical stratification of
A\cup B
.
Instead of a complete analysis, we discuss two particularly interesting cases, stemming from only 3 components of
D
:

A_{2}\cup A_{12}\cup B_{12}.

In physics language, the critical values of
A_{2}\cup A_{12}
would be called
leading singularities
(absence of boundary
B
) of
first type
(absence of
A_{1}
). They constitute singularities of integrals
\int_{\sigma}\omega_{t}
where
\sigma\in H_{3}(Y\setminus(A_{12}\cup A_{2}),\varnothing)_{t}
has no boundary, and the integrand
\omega
has only
\mathcal{F}
in the denominator (no
\mathcal{U}
). Note that
\mathcal{F}
vanishes on
A_{12}
, hence we cannot discard
A_{12}
(the differential form
\omega
can have poles on
A_{12}
).
40
40

40

To get
all
leading singularities of first type, we have to consider
A_{12}\cup A_{134}\cup A_{234}\cup A_{2}
. However, we are only interested in the singularities near
\{x_{1}=x_{2}=0\}
.
Critical values of
B_{12}\cap A_{2}
might be called
reduced
singularities, associated with the quotient graph
G/\gamma
. Instead, we will use the terminology of type and simple type (
section

4.4
).

In the coordinates
(w,x_{3})
on
A_{12}
from the proof of
6.10
, the singular points of the quadric (
6.13
) constitute the critical set

(6.14)

c(A_{2}\cap A_{12})=\Big\{w=\frac{\partial_{x_{3}}\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}}{m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\Big\}\cap\Big\{x_{3}=-\frac{m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}}{m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\Big\}\cap\Big\{\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}=0\Big\}.

This set has a non-empty fibre only when
x_{3}=-(m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2})/(m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})
is a root of
\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}
from (
6.11
). The corresponding discriminant defines a component
\ell_{A_{12}}\subset L_{G}
of the Landau variety, concretely

(6.15)

\ell_{A_{12}}=\big\{p_{1}^{2}(m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})(m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2})+(m_{3}^{2}p_{2}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}p_{3}^{2})(m_{3}^{2}-m_{4}^{2}+p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})=0\big\}.

Over a generic point
t
of
\ell_{A_{12}}
, the critical point (
6.14
) is not a critical point for
A_{2}
, that is,
A_{2t}
remains smooth over
\ell_{A_{12}}
. This means that the type and simple type of
\ell_{A_{12}}
are the same:
(\{A_{12},A_{2}\},\varnothing)
. More precisely, (
6.14
) is a quadratic simple pinch, where
A_{2}\cap A_{12}
degenerates into two lines,

(6.16)

A_{2t}\cap A_{12}=\Big\{x_{3}=-\frac{m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}}{m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}\Big\}\cup\Big\{w=\frac{m_{3}^{2}x_{3}}{m_{3}^{2}-p_{3}^{2}}+\frac{m_{4}^{2}}{m_{4}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}}\Big\}.

Now consider the submanifold
A_{2}\cap B_{12}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}=0\}
. In a generic fibre, it consists of two lines (coordinate
\lambda=v+1
), which degenerate into a single line when the two roots of
\mathcal{F}_{G/\gamma}
coincide. Hence, the corresponding contribution
\ell_{B_{12}}\subset L_{G}
to the Landau variety of the ice cream cone is

(6.17)

\ell_{B_{12}}=\ell^{\mathcal{F}}_{G/\gamma}=\{p_{1}^{2}=(m_{3}\pm m_{4})^{2}\}

in the notation from
section

6.1
, the leading singularity of the bubble graph
G/\gamma
(
section

1.8.2
). The critical set over
t\in\ell_{B_{12}}
is not isolated:

c(A_{2}\cap B_{12})\cap\pi^{-1}(t)=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\big\{[x_{3}:x_{4}]=[m_{4}:\pm m_{3}]\big\}.

This line intersects also
B_{1}=\{\lambda=\infty\}
,
B_{2}=\{\lambda=1\}
and
A_{12}=\{\lambda=0\}
. The component
B_{12}
is
not
simple over
t\in\ell_{B_{12}}
, because two points
41
41

41

P_{1,2}
are the roots of
v^{2}m_{2}^{2}+v(m_{1}^{2}+m_{2}^{2}+m_{3}m_{4})+m_{1}^{2}=v(m_{3}p_{2}^{2}\pm m_{4}p_{3}^{2})/(m_{3}\pm m_{4})

(6.18)

c(A_{2})\cap\pi^{-1}(t)=\{P_{1},P_{2}\}\times\{[m_{4}:\pm m_{3}]\}\subset c(A_{2}\cap B_{12})

on this line are critical points of
A_{2}
alone.
This fact, that
B_{12}
is not simple for the singularity
\ell_{B_{12}}
, was noted already in
[
10
, §4]
and
[
36
, §2]
.

Corollary 6.11
.

The bubble singularity (
6.17
) of the ice cream cone graph has type
(\{A_{2},A_{12}\},\{B_{1},B_{2},B_{12}\})
and simple type
(\{A_{2}\},\varnothing)
. The type and simple type of the singularity (
6.15
) is
(\{A_{2},A_{12}\},\varnothing)
.

Let
\mathfrak{L}_{G}
denote the codimension one components of the full Landau variety
L_{G}
of the ice cream cone
G
, and
\mathfrak{L}_{G/\gamma}
correspondingly for the bubble quotient. Note that
\mathfrak{L}_{G/\gamma}\subset\mathfrak{L}_{G}
.
If
B_{12}
were simple for
\ell_{B_{12}}
, then
section

4
would give

(6.19)

\Var_{\ell_{B_{12}}}\circ\Var_{\ell}=0\quad\text{for all}\quad\ell\in\mathfrak{L}_{G}\setminus\mathfrak{L}_{G/\gamma}.

Namely, a compatible variation requires that
\ell
has
B_{12}
in its type, meaning that the critical set
P
over
\ell
intersects
B_{12}
, and thus
P\cap B_{12}
are critical points of
B_{12}
—showing that the restriction of the arrangement
A\cup B
to
B_{12}
is critical over
\ell
(hence
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}_{G/\gamma}
). Anyway,
B_{12}
is
not
simple, and indeed, (
6.19
) does not hold
[
36
, eq. (14)]
.

However, we
do
have hierarchy constraints in the
\mathfrak{A}
-direction:

(6.20)

\Var_{\ell}\circ\Var_{\ell_{A_{12}}}=0,

unless
\ell
has
A_{12}
and
A_{2}
in its type. In fact, since
\ell_{A_{12}}
is a simple pinch component, its vanishing cycle is an
iterated
coboundary
\nu=\delta_{A_{2}}\delta_{A_{12}}\eta
around the vanishing sphere

(6.21)

\eta=\partial_{A_{12}}\partial_{A_{2}}\zeta=[\mathbb{S}^{1}]\in H_{1}(A_{2}\cap A_{12}\setminus A_{1})_{t}\cong\mathbb{Z}.

This group has rank one, because
A_{2t}\cap A_{12}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
from (
6.13
) is punctured in two points
A_{2t}\cap A_{12}\cap A_{1}=\{R_{1},R_{2}\}
. The vanishing sphere
\eta
separates the two punctures, because over the critical fibre, the line
A_{1}\cap A_{12}
intersects each of the two components (
6.16
) once. Therefore,
\eta=\delta_{R_{1}}1=-\delta_{R_{2}}1
is a generator of (
6.21
).

By (
4.6
),
\Var_{\ell_{A_{12}}}\nu=0
. The only other singularity of
A_{2}\cap A_{12}\setminus A_{1}
is the quadratic simple pinch of
A_{2}\cap A_{12}\cap A_{1}
, when
R_{1}=R_{2}
. This happens over a singularity
\ell_{\delta}\in\mathfrak{L}_{G}
that is familiar from the triangle graph:

(6.22)

\ell_{\delta}=\big\{p_{1}^{4}+p_{2}^{4}+p_{3}^{4}-2p_{1}^{2}p_{2}^{2}-2p_{1}^{2}p_{3}^{2}-2p_{2}^{2}p_{3}^{2}=0\big\}\subset L_{G}.

Corollary 6.12
.

For all
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}_{G}\setminus\{\ell_{\delta}\}
, we have
\Var_{\ell}\circ\Var_{\ell_{A_{12}}}=0
.

In fact, the singularity
\ell_{\delta}
gives another example of non-simple types. Traditionally, this singularity is thought of as “second type”, that is, associated with the critical points of
A_{1}\cap A_{2}
. And indeed, this intersection is critical over
\ell_{\delta}
: if we parametrize the smooth quadric
A_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times T
as

([y],[z])\mapsto[y_{1}(y_{1}+y_{2})(z_{1}+z_{2}):y_{2}(y_{1}+y_{2})(z_{1}+z_{2}):-y_{1}y_{2}z_{1}:-y_{1}y_{2}z_{2}],

then
A_{1}\cap A_{2t}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\{z_{1}^{2}p_{3}^{2}-z_{1}z_{2}(p_{1}^{2}-p_{2}^{2}-p_{3}^{2})+z_{2}^{2}p_{2}^{2}=0\}
is generically a union of two non-intersecting lines. Over
\ell_{\delta}
, the two solutions for
z
coincide and the lines merge into one,
c(A_{1}\cap A_{2})\cap\pi^{-1}(t)\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. As for
B_{12}
above, there are two points on this line that are critical points of
A_{2}
alone. Hence the simple type of
\ell_{\delta}
is only
(\{A_{2}\},\varnothing)
, like for a “first type” singularity.

Remark 6.13
.

The vanishing sphere of
A_{2}\cap A_{12}\cap A_{1}
over
\ell_{\delta}
is
\{R_{1},R_{2}\}\cong\mathbb{S}^{0}
, hence the corresponding vanishing cycle is
2\nu
. It follows that
\Var_{\ell_{\delta}}\nu=-\nu
and
\Var_{\ell_{\delta}}\circ\Var_{\ell_{A_{12}}}=-\Var_{\ell_{A_{12}}}
. If
A_{1}
were simple for
\ell_{\delta}
, then the hierarchy would in contradiction predict that this double variation is zero.

To summarize: the additional blowup
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
not only makes the divisor
A\cup B
normal crossing, but the new divisor
A_{12}
also provides a new singularity (
6.15
) with hierarchy constraints (
6.20
).

Remark 6.14
.

As already noted in
[
10
, §4]
, the non-transversality of
A^{\prime}\cup B^{\prime}
is a completely general phenomenon for Feynman motives. As soon as a graph
G
has a subgraph
\gamma
such that
\gamma
and
G/\gamma
have each at least one loop and at least two edges, the factorizations (
6.11
) lead to singular intersections of
A_{1}^{\prime}
and
A_{2}^{\prime}
with
B_{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

Our calculations suggests that an understanding of hierarchy constraints for variations of Feynman graphs with more than one loop might be obtained by a study of modified Feynman motives
X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
, where
X
is the blowup of
X^{\prime}
in the divisors
\{\mathcal{U}_{\gamma}=0\}\subset B_{\gamma}^{\prime}
.

Appendix A
Homology of simple pinches

Consider a simple pinch on a stratum
S_{1}\cap\ldots\cap S_{m}
in local coordinates on a complex open ball
W=\mathbb{B}_{n}=\{\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{2}+\ldots+\lvert x_{n}\rvert^{2}<1\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
, as in eq. (
3.7
):

\displaystyle S_{i}

\displaystyle=\{x_{i}=0\},\quad i=1,\ldots,m-1,

\displaystyle S_{m}

\displaystyle=\{x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1}+x_{m}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n}^{2}=t\}.

For any subset
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we denote union and intersection as

S_{I}=\bigcup_{i\in I}S_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad S^{I}=\bigcap_{i\in I}S_{i}.

In this appendix we generalize results of
[
21
]
and calculate the homology groups

H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})_{t}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})_{t}

for all distributions
I\sqcup J\sqcup K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
of the hypersurfaces. As we only compute the groups in a fixed fibre, we suppress the subscript
t
from now on.

We begin with a review of the well-known case
J=K=\varnothing
, and then the rest follows from the boundary and residue sequences of
appendix

B
.

Lemma A.1
.

If
\lvert t\rvert<1
and
\lvert I\rvert<m
, then
W\cap S^{I}
is contractible.

Proof.

Coordinate hyperplanes
i\in I\setminus\{m\}
just lower the ambient dimension
\mathbb{B}_{n}\cap S_{i}\cong\mathbb{B}_{n-1}
, hence the claim reduces to
I\subseteq\{m\}
. The ball
I=\varnothing
is contractible, so only
I=\{m\}
remains.
We have
m\geq 2
because
I
is a proper subset. Set
\bar{x}=(x_{1}+\ldots+x_{m-1})/(m-1)
and apply the linear homotopy

H_{\lambda}\colon x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto(1-\lambda)x+\lambda(\underbrace{\bar{x},\ldots,\bar{x}}_{\text{$m-1$ times}},x_{m},\ldots,x_{n}),\ \lambda\in[0,1].

This preserves
S_{m}
,
H_{\lambda}(S_{m})\subseteq S_{m}
, and furthermore stays within the unit ball,
H_{\lambda}(\mathbb{B}_{n})\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{n}
for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
, because (Cauchy-Schwarz)

\lVert H_{\lambda}(x)\rVert^{2}=\lVert x\rVert^{2}-\lambda(2-\lambda)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\lvert x_{i}\rvert^{2}-(m-1)\lvert\bar{x}\rvert^{2}\right)\leq\lVert x\rVert^{2}.

We obtain a strict deformation retraction
H_{1}
from
\mathbb{B}_{n}\cap S_{m}
onto the subspace where
x_{1}=\ldots=x_{m-1}=\bar{x}
are equal. In this subspace of codimension
m-2
, we have
\lVert x\rVert^{2}=\lvert\bar{x}\sqrt{m-1}\rvert^{2}+\lvert x_{m}\rvert^{2}+\ldots+\lvert x_{n}\rvert^{2}
.
This gives an isometry
H_{1}(\mathbb{B}_{n}\cap S_{m})\cong\mathbb{B}_{n-m+2}\cap S^{\prime}
where

S^{\prime}=\{x_{1}\sqrt{m-1}+x_{2}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n-m+2}^{2}=t\}.

For the linear pinch case
m=n+1
, this is just a point
x_{1}=t/\sqrt{n}
and we are done. In the quadratic case
m\leq n
let now
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n-m+2})
and apply a further homotopy

F_{\lambda}\colon x\mapsto\left((1-\lambda)x_{1}+\lambda\frac{t}{\sqrt{m-1}},x_{2}\sqrt{1-\lambda},\ldots,x_{n-m+2}\sqrt{1-\lambda}\right)

which preserves
S^{\prime}
,
F_{\lambda}(S^{\prime})\subseteq S^{\prime}
.
A calculation shows

\lVert F_{\lambda}(x)\rVert^{2}=(1-\lambda)\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\lambda\frac{\lvert t\rvert^{2}}{m-1}-\lambda(1-\lambda)\left|x_{1}-\frac{t}{\sqrt{m-1}}\right|^{2}

and therefore
\lVert F_{\lambda}(x)\rVert^{2}\leq(1-\lambda)\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\lambda\lvert t\rvert^{2}<1
, provided that
\lvert t\rvert<1
and
\lVert x\rVert<1
. In conclusion,
F
shows that the point
F_{1}(x)=(t/\sqrt{m-1},0,\ldots,0)
is a deformation retract of
\mathbb{B}_{n-m+2}\cap S^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma A.2
.

Let
I=\{1,\ldots,m\}
and
0<\lvert t\rvert<1
. If
m=n+1
(linear pinch), then
W\cap S^{I}=\varnothing
is empty. If
m\leq n
(quadratic pinch), then
W\cap S^{I}
is homotopy equivalent to a real sphere of dimension
n-m
.

Proof.

The case
m=n+1
is clear from (
3.7
). For
m\leq n
, we get
W\cap S^{I}\cong\mathbb{B}_{n-m+1}\cap S^{\prime}
with the complex sphere

S^{\prime}=\{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{n-m+1}^{2}=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n-m+1}.

After a unitary transformation
x\mapsto x\sqrt{t/\lvert t\rvert}
, we may assume that
t=\lvert t\rvert>0
is real. In terms of the real and imaginary parts
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n-m+1}
of
x=u+\mathrm{i}v
,

S^{\prime}=\{\lVert u\rVert^{2}=t+\lVert v\rVert^{2}\}\cap\{u\cdot v=0\}

is diffeomorphic to the tangent bundle
T\mathbb{S}^{n-m}
of the real sphere via
x\mapsto(u/\lVert u\rVert,v)
with inverse
(p,v)\mapsto p\sqrt{t+\lVert v\rVert^{2}}+\mathrm{i}v
. The contraction to the zero section can be realized by a homotopy
F\colon S^{\prime}\times[0,1]\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
of the form

F_{\lambda}\colon x=u+\mathrm{i}v\mapsto u\sqrt{1-\lambda+\lambda t/\lVert u\rVert^{2}}+\mathrm{i}v\sqrt{1-\lambda}.

Due to
\lVert F_{\lambda}(x)\rVert^{2}=(1-\lambda)\lVert x\rVert^{2}+\lambda t<1
, this homotopy restricts to a contraction of
S^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{n-m+1}
onto the real sphere
F_{1}(S^{\prime}\cap\mathbb{B}_{n-m+1})=\{u\sqrt{t}/\lVert u\rVert\}
with radius
\sqrt{t}
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

For all disjoint triples of subsets
I\sqcup J\sqcup K\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
that do not cover all of the
m
hypersurfaces, the homology groups are

H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\cong 0\quad\text{whenever $K\neq\varnothing$.}

Proof.

Let
J=\varnothing
and pick any
p\in K
. Consider the boundary in the submanifold
W\cap S^{I}\cap S_{p}
inside the pair
(W\cap S^{I},W\cap S^{I}\cap S_{K})
. By induction over
\lvert K\rvert
and
A.1
, we may assume that
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I},S_{K-p})
is concentrated in degree zero. Hence the boundary sequence breaks into isomorphisms

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I},S_{K})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\bullet-1}(W\cap S^{I+p},S_{K-p})\quad\text{for $\bullet\geq 2$ and}

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I},S_{K})

\displaystyle\cong 0\quad\text{for $\bullet=0,1$,}

noting for the latter that
H_{0}(W\cap S^{I+p},S_{K-p})\rightarrow H_{0}(W\cap S^{I},S_{K-p})
is an isomorphism. This concludes the induction step and hence the proof for
J=\varnothing
. For
J\neq\varnothing
, apply induction over
\lvert J\rvert
, using the residue sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J-p},S_{K})\rightarrow H_{\bullet-2}(W\cap S^{I+p}\setminus S_{J-p},S_{K})\\
\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J-p},S_{K})\rightarrow\cdots

with respect to some
p\in J
: all terms with
J-p
are zero by induction, so the groups with
J
vanish as well, by exactness.
∎

Corollary A.4
.

Take any partition
I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
with
K\neq\varnothing
. Then all boundary and Leray coboundary maps
\partial_{p},\delta_{p}
with
p\in I
are isomorphisms:

\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I-p}\setminus S_{J},S_{K+p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I-p}\setminus S_{J+p},S_{K})}

By iterations of these maps, all these groups (with
K\neq\varnothing
) are isomorphic and in fact free of rank one, concentrated in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
:

\delta_{J}\partial_{I\sqcup J}\colon H_{n}(W,S_{1,\ldots,m})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

Due to the vanishing of
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I-p}\setminus S_{J},S_{K})
from
A.3
, the boundary and residue exact sequences with respect to
S_{p}
break into the isomorphisms
\partial_{p}
and
\delta_{p}
as claimed. It remains to identify the group for a convenient choice. Pick
I=\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
,
J=\varnothing
, and
K=\{m\}
. For a linear pinch (
m=n+1
), this yields a single point
W\cap S^{I}=\{0\}
(the origin) relative to
W\cap S^{I}\cap S_{K}=\varnothing
, hence

H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,n},S_{n+1})=H_{\bullet}(\{0\})\cong\mathbb{Z}

is indeed concentrated in degree
\bullet=n-\lvert I\rvert=0
. For a quadratic pinch (
m\leq n
), the intersection
W\cap S^{I}\cap S_{K}=W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m}
is non-empty and contributes an augmentation
H_{0}(W\cap S^{I+m})\twoheadrightarrow H_{0}(W\cap S^{I})\cong\mathbb{Z}
in the boundary sequence for
S_{m}
. Hence we get an identification

\partial_{m}\colon H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m-1},S_{m})\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{H}_{\bullet-1}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m})\cong\mathbb{Z}

with the reduced homology of the vanishing sphere from
A.2
, concentrated in degree
\bullet-1=n-m
.
∎

Lemma A.5
.

Take any disjoint pair
I\sqcup J\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
of less than
m
indices. Then the inclusion induces an isomorphism
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J})\cong H_{\bullet}(W\setminus S_{J})
.
Furthermore, the sum
\sum_{L}\delta_{L}
of the iterated Leray coboundaries

\delta_{L}\colon\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{0}(W\cap S^{L}\setminus S_{J-L})\rightarrow H_{\lvert L\rvert}(W\setminus S_{J})

over indices
L\subseteq J
, provides canonical isomorphisms

(A.1)

\mathbb{Z}^{\binom{\lvert J\rvert}{d}}\cong\bigoplus_{L\subseteq J,\lvert L\rvert=d}H_{0}(W\cap S^{L}\setminus S_{J-L})\xrightarrow[\sum_{L}\delta_{L}]{\cong}H_{d}(W\setminus S_{J}).

Proof.

For any
p\in I
, the boundary in
S_{p}
sequence breaks into isomorphisms

\cdots\rightarrow 0\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I-p}\setminus S_{J})\rightarrow 0\rightarrow\cdots

due to
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I-p}\setminus S_{J},S_{p})=0
from
A.3
.
To show that (
A.1
) are isomorphisms, we apply induction over
\lvert J\rvert
. Since
J=\varnothing
forces
L=\varnothing
, these maps are zero in degree
d\neq 0
in agreement with
H_{d}(W)=0
, and in degree
d=0
we get the identity map on
\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{0}(W)
. For the induction step, let
p\in J\neq\varnothing
and consider the residue sequence with respect to
S_{p}
. Its maps

\varpi\colon H_{d}(W\setminus S_{J-p})\longrightarrow H_{d-2}(W\cap S_{p}\setminus S_{J-p})

are zero, because every generator
\delta_{L}1_{L}\in H_{d}(W\setminus S_{J-p})
, from
L\subseteq J-p
with
1_{L}\in H_{0}(W\cap S^{L}\setminus S_{J-p-L})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, maps to
\varpi\delta_{L}1_{L}=\delta_{L}\varpi 1_{L}=0
due to
\varpi 1_{L}\in H_{-2}(W\cap S^{L+p}\setminus S_{J-p-L})=0
. Therefore, the residue sequence breaks into short exact sequences. We obtain a commutative diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{d-1}(W\cap S_{p}\setminus S_{J-p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{p}}
\textstyle{H_{d}(W\setminus S_{J})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{d}(W\setminus S_{J-p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\binom{J-p}{d-1}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\binom{J}{d}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}^{\binom{J-p}{d}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{0}

with (
A.1
) on the vertical arrows,
\alpha(1_{L})=1_{L+p}
,
\beta(1_{L})=1_{L}
for all
L\subseteq J-p
and
\beta(1_{L})=0
otherwise (
p\in L
). The bottom row is split exact and thus the five lemma concludes the induction step.
∎

Proposition A.6
.

Take any partition
I\sqcup J=\{1,\ldots,m\}
of all
m
indices. Then the iterated Leray coboundaries induce canonical splittings

(A.2)

H_{d-\lvert J\rvert}(W\cap S^{I+J})\oplus\bigoplus_{L\subset J,\lvert L\rvert=d}H_{0}(W\cap S^{I+L}\setminus S_{J-L})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{d}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J}).

Proof.

The claim is trivial for
J=\varnothing
, and generalizes to
\lvert J\rvert>0
by induction using the residue sequence.
The same reasoning from the proof of
A.5
applies, except for the reduction to
I=\varnothing
(which no longer holds). In particular, we still have
\varpi=0
, since its domain
H_{\bullet}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J-p})
involves less hypersurfaces and is thus generated by coboundaries (
A.1
) of points.
∎

This decomposition implies the following two corollaries, which describe
H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J})
; c.f. the
marginal cases
in
[
51
, Footnote 10 on page 93]
.

Corollary A.7
.

For every bipartition
I\sqcup J=\{1,\ldots,m\}
of a quadratic pinch (
m\leq n
), the iterated Leray coboundary gives an isomorphism

\delta_{J}\colon H_{n-m}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J}).

Proof.

The classes
\delta_{L}1_{L}
from points
1_{L}\in H_{0}(W\cap S^{I+L}\setminus S_{J-L})\cong\mathbb{Z}
in (
A.2
) contribute only to degrees
\lvert L\rvert<\lvert J\rvert=m-\lvert I\rvert\leq n-\lvert I\rvert
.
∎

Corollary A.8
.

For a linear pinch (
m=n+1
) and
I\sqcup J=\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
, the
\lvert J\rvert
iterated Leray coboundaries
\delta_{J-p}\colon\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{0}(S^{I+J-p})\rightarrow H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J})
provide a canonical isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}^{\lvert J\rvert}\cong\bigoplus_{p\in J}H_{0}(S^{I+J-p})\xrightarrow[\sum\limits_{p\in J}\delta_{J-p}]{\cong}H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(W\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J}).

Proof.

The set
S^{I+J}=\varnothing
is empty (
A.2
), thus the first contribution in (
A.2
) is absent and only
L\subset J
with
\lvert L\rvert=n-\lvert I\rvert=\lvert J\rvert-1
remain. These
n
-fold intersections
W\cap S^{I+J-p}\setminus S_{p}=S^{I+J-p}
are points: precisely those corners of the simplex which lie opposite to the facets
S_{p}
with
p\in J
.
∎

A.1.
Duality

Since
W\cap S^{I}
is contractible for
\lvert I\rvert<m
(
A.1
), Poincaré-Lefschetz duality implies that

H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W)\cong H^{2n-2\lvert I\rvert-\bullet}(W\cap S^{I})\cong\mathbb{Z}

is concentrated in degree
\bullet=2n-2\lvert I\rvert
, spanned by the fundamental class of the manifold
\overline{W}\cap S^{I}
with boundary
\partial W\cap S^{I}
. For a quadratic pinch with its retraction to the vanishing sphere, the duality shows that

\displaystyle H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)

\displaystyle\cong H^{2n-2m-\bullet}(W\cap S^{1,\ldots,m})

\displaystyle\cong H^{2n-2m-\bullet}(\mathbb{S}^{n-m})\cong H_{\bullet-n+m}(\mathbb{S}^{n-m})

is concentrated in degrees
n-m
and
2n-2m
.

From this starting point, all proofs above formally dualize upon swapping boundaries
\partial
and coboundaries
\delta
, swapping
J
with
K
, replacing the pairs
(W\cap S^{I},\varnothing)
with
(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W)
, and shifting degrees
\bullet
to
2n-2\lvert I\rvert-\bullet
. In particular, one finds:

(1)

For all strict subsets
I\sqcup J\sqcup K\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}
with
J\neq\varnothing
,

H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})=0.

(2)

For all
I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
with
J\neq\varnothing
, the groups are concentrated in degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
and iterated (co)boundaries yield isomorphisms

\delta_{I\sqcup K}\partial_{K}\colon H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I}\setminus S_{J},\partial W\cup S_{K})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n}(\overline{W}\setminus S_{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)\cong\mathbb{Z}.

(3)

For
I\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,m\}
in a quadratic pinch (
m\leq n
), the boundaries are isomorphisms in the lowest degree
n-\lvert I\rvert
:

\partial_{K}\colon H_{n-\lvert I\rvert}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W\cup S_{K})\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{n-m}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m},\partial W).

Proof.

The first claim above is dual to
A.3
. That proof via long exact sequences used only that
H_{0}(W\cap S^{I+p})\rightarrow H_{0}(W\cap S^{I})
is an isomorphism. So dually, one only has to check that the intersection

\varpi\colon H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I},\partial W)\rightarrow H_{\bullet-2}(\overline{W}\cap S^{I+p},\partial W)

with
S_{p}
is an isomorphism. This is clear, since these groups
\cong\mathbb{Z}
are generated by the respective fundamental classes.

The second claim is the dual of
A.4
; the isomorphisms follow just from the existence of the exact sequences. All that is left is to identify the groups at a single slot, like
I=\{1,\ldots,m-1\}
,
J=\{m\}
and
K=\varnothing
. For a linear pinch,
\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,n}\setminus S_{n+1}=\{0\}
is a point with homology
\mathbb{Z}
. For a quadratic pinch, consider the residue sequence for
S_{m}
. The intersection

\varpi_{m}\colon\mathbb{Z}\cong H_{\bullet+2}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m-1},\partial W)\hookrightarrow H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)

is injective, sending the generator (fundamental class) to the (non-zero) fundamental class
[\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m}]
. Hence the coboundary

\delta_{m}\colon H_{\bullet}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m},\partial W)\twoheadrightarrow H_{\bullet+1}(\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m-1}\setminus S_{m},\partial W)

is surjective and identifies the image with the quotient of
H_{\bullet-n+m}(\mathbb{S}^{n-m})
by
\mathbb{Z}[\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m}]
, which cancels degree
\bullet=2n-2m
and leaves only
H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{n-m})\cong\mathbb{Z}
in degree
\bullet=n-m
for
m<n
. The case
m=n
where
\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{P_{+},P_{-}\}
consists of two points is slightly different: Here we reduce
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\cong H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
modulo the sum
\varpi_{m}[\overline{W}\cap S^{1,\ldots,m-1}]=[P_{+}]+[P_{-}]
, leaving
\mathbb{Z}
.

The third claim is a very similar dualization, of
A.7
.
∎

Remark A.9
.

Modulo torsion, these claims follow immediately from a generalization of Poincaré-Lefschetz duality to transverse arrangements, spelled out in
C.2
.

A.2.
Variation of a quadric

The well-known classical Picard-Lefschetz formula (e.g.
[
34
, §(5.3.3)]
or
[
35
, §(6.3.3)]
) computes the variation of a quadratic pinch in the case
J=K=\varnothing
, i.e., for the complete intersection of all
I=\{1,\ldots,m\}
. We can ignore the coordinates
x_{1}=\ldots=x_{m-1}=0
, so

S^{I}_{t}\cong Q_{t}=\{x_{1}^{2}+\ldots+x_{r+1}^{2}=t\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+1}

is a family of complex quadrics
Q_{t}
with dimension
r=n-m
. They are localized
W\cap S^{I}_{t}\cong\mathbb{B}\cap Q_{t}
in the ball
\mathbb{B}=\{\lvert x_{1}\rvert^{2}+\ldots+\lvert x_{r+1}\rvert^{2}<1\}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+1}
.
For
0<\lvert t\rvert<1
, the embedded vanishing sphere

(A.3)

\mathbb{S}^{r}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{B}\cap Q_{t},\quad u\mapsto x=u\cdot\sqrt{t}

is a deformation retract
H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{B}\cap Q_{t})\cong H_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S}^{r})
, and its fundamental class
\nu=\widetilde{\nu}=\pm[\mathbb{S}^{r}]\in H_{r}(\mathbb{B}\cap Q_{t})
provides the vanishing cycle.
In polar coordinates
t=\rho\cdot e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
, the embedding (
A.3
) extends (for
\rho>0
) to a diffeomorphism

(A.4)

\begin{split}T\mathbb{S}^{r}=\{(u,v)\in\mathbb{S}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{r+1}\colon u\cdot v=0\}\xrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\cong\hskip 8.19447pt}Q_{t}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+1},\\
(u,v)\mapsto e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}\cdot\Big(u\sqrt{\rho+\lVert v\rVert^{2}\cdot\tfrac{1-\rho}{2}}+\mathrm{i}v\sqrt{\tfrac{1-\rho}{2}}\Big)\end{split}

of the quadric with the tangent bundle of the vanishing sphere. Restricted to the disk bundle
\overline{V}=\{\lVert v\rVert\leq 1\}\subset T\mathbb{S}^{r}
and its boundary, the sphere bundle
\partial V=\{\lVert v\rVert=1\}
, we obtain local trivializations of the pairs

(A.5)

(\overline{\mathbb{B}}\cap Q_{t},\partial\mathbb{B}\cap Q_{t})\cong(\overline{V},\partial V).

The corresponding isotopy
g_{\tau}\colon Q_{\rho}\cong Q_{t},x\mapsto e^{\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}\cdot x
over the path
\gamma(\tau)=\rho\cdot e^{2\mathrm{i}\pi\tau}
ends up in the antipode
g_{1}(x)=-x
, which is not the identity on
\partial\mathbb{B}
. Hence we compose it with the diffeomorphisms
\Phi_{\tau}\colon T\mathbb{S}^{r}\longrightarrow T\mathbb{S}^{r}
defined by

\Phi_{\tau}(u,v)=\big(u\cos\theta+(v/\lVert v\rVert)\sin\theta,\;-u\,\lVert v\rVert\sin\theta+v\cos\theta\big)\big|_{\theta=\pi\tau\cdot\lVert v\rVert}

which rotate in the plane spanned by
u
and
v
. By continuity in
\tau
, the map
\Phi_{\tau}
is homotopic to the identity
\Phi_{0}=\id
, and therefore
g_{\tau}
is homotopic to
g^{\prime}_{\tau}=g_{\tau}\circ\Phi_{\tau}
. The angle
\theta
of rotation decreases from
\pi\tau
on the boundary
\partial V
, down to
0
at the zero section
\mathbb{S}^{r}=\{v=0\}
.
The resulting modified isotopy (
fig.

25
)

g_{1}^{\prime}(u,v)=-\big(u\cos\theta+(v/\lVert v\rVert)\sin\theta,\;-u\lVert v\rVert\sin\theta+v\cos\theta\big)\big|_{\theta=\pi\cdot\lVert v\rVert}

restricts to the identity map on
\partial V=\{\lVert v\rVert=1\}
.

Figure 25.
Illustration of the map
v\mapsto g^{\prime}_{1}(u,v)
for
r=2
.

In fact,
g_{\tau}^{\prime}
trivializes the sphere bundle globally: The isomorphism
g_{\tau}^{\prime}|_{\partial V}\colon\partial V\rightarrow Q_{t}\cap\partial\mathbb{B}
is given by

\displaystyle(u,v)

\displaystyle\mapsto e^{\mathrm{i}\theta}\Big([u\cos\theta+v\sin\theta]\sqrt{\tfrac{1+\rho}{2}}+\mathrm{i}[v\cos\theta-u\sin\theta]\sqrt{\tfrac{1-\rho}{2}}\Big)\Big|_{\theta=\pi\tau}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{u+\mathrm{i}v}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{R}{2}+\frac{u-\mathrm{i}v}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{t}{R}\right)_{R=\sqrt{1+\rho}+\sqrt{1-\rho}}

and therefore well-defined and smooth also over
t=0
, where it identifies
\partial V\cong Q_{0}\cap\partial\mathbb{B}
as
(u,v)\mapsto(u+iv)/\sqrt{2}
.
Hence, we can use
g_{1}^{\prime}
to compute the localized variation. With the identification (
A.5
), the domain of

\var=(g_{1}^{\prime})_{*}-\id\colon\quad H_{r}(\overline{V},\partial V)\rightarrow H_{r}(V)

is
H_{r}(\overline{V},\partial V)\cong H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{r})
by the Thom isomorphism of the bundle
\mu\colon\overline{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{r}
,
(u,v)\mapsto u
. So for
r\geq 1
,
H_{r}(\overline{V},\partial V)\cong\mathbb{Z}
is generated by the class of a fibre
\mu^{-1}(p)
. Over the point
p=(0,\ldots,0,1)\in\mathbb{S}^{r}
, this class
h=j_{*}[\overline{\mathbb{D}}]
is represented by the
r
-dimensional disk
\overline{\mathbb{D}}=\{\lVert w\rVert\leq 1\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}
, embedded as

j\colon(\overline{\mathbb{D}},\partial\mathbb{D})\hookrightarrow(\overline{V},\partial V),\quad w\mapsto(p,(w,0))\in T_{p}\mathbb{S}^{r}.

Because the retraction
\mu
induces an isomorphism
\mu_{*}\colon H_{r}(V)\cong H_{r}(\mathbb{S}^{r})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, we can compute the variation by pushing forward along
\mu
:

\mu_{*}(\var h)=\mu_{*}(g_{1}^{\prime})_{*}j_{*}[\overline{\mathbb{D}}]-\mu_{*}j_{*}[\overline{\mathbb{D}}].

Since
\mu j\colon\overline{\mathbb{D}}\rightarrow\mathbb{S}^{r}
is the constant map
w\mapsto p
, the second term is zero in
H_{r}(\mathbb{S}^{r})\cong H_{r}(\mathbb{S}^{r},\{p\})
. The remaining term is the push-forward under

\mu g_{1}^{\prime}j\colon(\overline{\mathbb{D}},\partial\mathbb{D})\rightarrow(\mathbb{S}^{r},\{p\}),\quad w\mapsto\left(-\frac{w}{\lVert w\rVert}\sin(\pi\lVert w\rVert),\;-\cos(\pi\lVert w\rVert)\right).

This map is bijective on the interior
\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{S}^{r}\setminus\{p\}
and realizes a homeomorphism
\overline{\mathbb{D}}/\partial\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{S}^{r}
. Therefore, we proved that

(A.6)

\var\colon H_{r}(\overline{V},\partial V)\cong\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow H_{r}(V)\cong\mathbb{Z},\quad h\mapsto\pm\nu

is an isomorphism. To fix the sign, we endow
\mathbb{D}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}
with the orientation
\mathrm{d}w_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{r}
. Under the differential of
u=\mu g_{1}^{\prime}j(w)
at
w=0
, this becomes
(-1)^{r}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r}
at
T_{-p}\mathbb{S}^{r}
. In terms of the standard orientation
42
42

42

The standard orientation is induced by the ambient volume form
\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r+1}
of
\mathbb{R}^{r+1}
, by contraction with the outward normal vector. At
p
, the outward normal is
\partial/\partial u_{r+1}
, hence the standard orientation of
T_{p}\mathbb{S}^{r}
is
(-1)^{r}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r}
. At
-p
, the outward normal is
-\partial/\partial u_{r+1}
and thus the standard orientation of
T_{-p}\mathbb{S}^{r}
is
(-1)^{r+1}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r}
.
on the vanishing sphere
\nu=[\mathbb{S}^{r}]
, this gives
\var h=-\nu
in (
A.6
).
The disk
j(\overline{\mathbb{D}})
and the vanishing sphere
\mathbb{S}^{r}=\{v=0\}
intersect in precisely one point, namely
(p,0)\in T_{p}\mathbb{S}^{r}
. According to
appendix

C
, the intersection number

\langle{\nu}\,|\,{h}\rangle=\langle{[\mathbb{S}^{r}]}\,|\,{[j(\overline{\mathbb{D}})]}\rangle=\epsilon_{p}=\pm 1

is determined by comparing the orientation of the complex manifold
Q_{t}
with the orientation of
T_{(p,0)}j(\overline{\mathbb{D}})\oplus T_{(p,0)}\mathbb{S}^{r}
. With our choices above, the latter is

(
\sharp
)

\mathrm{d}v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{r}\wedge(-1)^{r}\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r}.

In terms of real and imaginary parts
x_{k}=\Re x_{k}+\mathrm{i}\Im x_{k}
, the orientation of
T_{p\sqrt{t}}Q_{t}
induced from the complex structure is
\mathrm{d}\Re x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\Im x_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}\Re x_{r}\wedge\mathrm{d}\Im x_{r}
. In the parametrization (
A.4
), this is equal to

(
\natural
)

\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{r}\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{r}\cdot\big(\sqrt{\lvert t\rvert(1-\lvert t\rvert)/2}\big)^{r}.

The orientations (

♯

‣
A.2
) and (

♮

‣
A.2
) differ by the sign
\epsilon_{p}=(-1)^{r}(-1)^{r(r+1)/2}
. We therefore arrive at the Picard-Lefschetz formula

\var h=-\nu=-(-1)^{r}(-1)^{r(r+1)/2}\langle{\nu}\,|\,{h}\rangle\nu=(-1)^{(r+1)(r+2)/2}\langle{\nu}\,|\,{h}\rangle\nu.

Remark A.10
.

If
r=0
, then
V=\overline{V}=\mathbb{S}^{0}=\{p,-p\}
consists of two points and
\partial V=\varnothing
. In this case,
\var\colon H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{0})\rightarrow H_{0}(\mathbb{S}^{0})
is a map from
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The variations
\var[p]=-\nu
and
\var[-p]=\nu=[p]-[-p]
from
3.5
are indeed compatible with
\var h=(-1)^{1\cdot 2/2}\langle{\nu}\,|\,{h}\rangle\nu
.

Appendix B
Partial boundaries and relative residues

B.1.
Partial boundaries

Given any two topological subspaces
B,S\subseteq X
, we can consider the triple
B\subseteq B\cup S\subseteq X
and its exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B)\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S)\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{\bullet-1}(B\cup S,B)\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(X,B)\rightarrow\cdots

The inclusion of pairs
(S,B\cap S)\rightarrow(B\cup S,B)
induces a morphism

(B.1)

H_{\bullet}(S,B\cap S)\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(B\cup S,B).

Definition B.1
.

A finite collection
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
of smooth closed submanifolds
S_{i}\subset X
is
transverse
if, for every subset
J\subseteq I
and at every point
p\in S^{J}
of the intersection
S^{J}=\bigcap_{i\in J}S_{i}
, the canonical projections
N_{p}S^{J}\longrightarrow N_{p}S_{i}
assemble into isomorphisms
NS^{J}\cong\bigoplus_{i\in J}NS_{i}|_{S^{J}}
of the normal bundles.

By the implicit function theorem, a transverse collection admits local coordinates
(x,(y_{i})_{i\in J})\colon U\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\prod_{i\in J}\mathbb{R}^{r_{i}}
near any point
p\in S^{J}
such that

S_{i}\cap U=\{y_{i}=0\}
for all
i\in J
, where
r_{i}=\codim S_{i}
and
d=\dim S^{J}
. If each
S_{i}
is a complex hypersurface (
r_{i}=2
) in a complex manifold, a transverse collection is also called
simple normal crossings divisor
.

Proposition B.2
.

If
S=S_{1}
and
B=\bigcup_{i>1}S_{i}
form a transverse collection of smooth closed submanifolds
S_{i}\subset X
, then (
B.1
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Pick a tubular neighbourhood
u\colon N\hookrightarrow X
of
S
, that is, a smooth embedding of the normal bundle
N=TX|_{S}/TS
such that
u\iota=\id_{S}
for the zero section
\iota\colon S\hookrightarrow N
. Let
U=u(N)
, then excise
B\setminus U
to get

H_{\bullet}(B\cup S,B)\cong H_{\bullet}(S\cup(B\cap U),B\cap U).

Contraction
F_{\lambda}(v)=(1-\lambda)v
of the fibres
N_{\pi(v)}
shows that the projection
\pi=F_{1}\colon U\cong N\longrightarrow S
is a deformation retract.
Using the transversality assumption we may construct
u
in such a way that
F_{\lambda}(U\cap B)\subseteq U\cap B
for all
\lambda
, see
[
56
, Lemma 4.3]
or
[
27
, Corollary 4.1]
. Then
F_{\lambda}
restricts to homotopies on
B\cap U
and
S\cup(B\cap U)
, which proves that

\pi|_{B\cap U}\colon B\cap U\longrightarrow B\cap S\quad\text{and}\quad\pi|_{S\cup(B\cap U)}\colon S\cup(B\cap U)\longrightarrow S

are deformation retracts and induce isomorphisms
H_{\bullet}(B\cap U)\cong H_{\bullet}(B\cap S)
and
H_{\bullet}(S\cup(B\cap U))\cong H_{\bullet}(S)
. To conclude, apply the five lemma to the long exact sequences of the pairs
(S\cup(B\cap U),B\cap U)
and
(S,B\cap S)
.
∎

In this proof, we exploited the fact that one may choose tubular neighbourhoods
u_{i}\colon TX|_{S_{i}}/TS_{i}\hookrightarrow X
for all
S_{i}
in a mutually compatible way, such that
u_{i}^{-1}(S_{j})=TS_{j}|_{S_{i}\cap S_{j}}/TS_{i}|_{S_{i}\cap S_{j}}
.
We will repeatedly use such compatible systems of tubes in this appendix. They are constructed in the references given in the proof above, see also
[
41
, §7]
, and illustrated in
fig.

26
.

Definition B.3
.

In the situation of
B.2
, the
boundary in
S
is the map
\partial_{S}\colon H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S)\longrightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(S,B\cap S)
obtained from the connecting morphism
\partial
of the triple
B\subseteq B\cup S\subseteq X
under the isomorphism (
B.1
):

\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(B\cup S,B)}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(S,B\cap S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

Now the exact sequence of the triple
B\subseteq B\cup S\subseteq X
takes the form

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B)\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S)\xrightarrow{\partial_{S}}H_{\bullet-1}(S,B\cap S)\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(X,B)\rightarrow\cdots

B.2.
Relative residues

If we replace
S
by its complement
S^{\mathsf{c}}=X\setminus S
and consider instead the triple
B\subseteq B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}}\subseteq X
, we get the sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B)\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}})\xrightarrow{\partial}H_{\bullet-1}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B)\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(X,B)\rightarrow\cdots

The inclusion of pairs
(S^{\mathsf{c}},B\setminus S)\rightarrow(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B)
induces a morphism

(B.2)

H_{\bullet}(X\setminus S,B\setminus S)\longrightarrow H_{\bullet}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B).

Proposition B.4
.

If
S=S_{1}
and
B=\bigcup_{i\neq 1}S_{i}
form a transverse collection of smooth closed submanifolds
S_{i}\subset X
, then (
B.2
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The zero section
S_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i}
of any tubular neighbourhood
NS_{i}\cong U_{i}\subseteq X
induces an isomorphism
H_{\bullet}(S_{i})\cong H_{\bullet}(U_{i})
. Following
[
56
,
27
]
we may assume the compatibility that every intersection
U^{J}=\bigcap_{i\in J}U_{i}
is a tubular neighbourhood of
S^{J}
(
fig.

26
), so that
H_{\bullet}(S^{J})\cong H_{\bullet}(U^{J})
.

Figure 26.
Tubular neighbourhoods of transverse submanifolds.

Then also the inclusion of
B
into
U\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigcup_{i\neq 1}U_{i}
induces an isomorphism

H_{\bullet}(B)\cong H_{\bullet}(U),
which follows from the Mayer-Vietoris sequence and the five lemma via induction over the number
\lvert I\rvert-1
of components of
B
. Analogously, the inclusions
B\setminus S\hookrightarrow U\setminus S
and
B\hookrightarrow B\cup(U\setminus S)
induce isomorphisms

H_{\bullet}(B\setminus S)\cong H_{\bullet}(U\setminus S)\quad\text{and}\quad H_{\bullet}(B)\cong H_{\bullet}(B\cup(U\setminus S)),

because compatibility with
S_{1}
ensures that for
1\notin J
, the projections
U^{J}\rightarrow S^{J}
map
U^{J}\cap S
to
S^{J}\cap S
and restrict to deformation retracts
U^{J}\setminus S\rightarrow S^{J}\setminus S
and
U^{J}\cap(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}})\rightarrow S^{J}
. These thickenings of
B\setminus S
and
B
induce
H_{\bullet}(S^{\mathsf{c}},B\setminus S)\cong H_{\bullet}(S^{\mathsf{c}},U\setminus S)
and

H_{\bullet}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B)\cong H_{\bullet}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B\cup(U\setminus S))
,
and these isomorphisms identify (
B.2
) with the canonical map

H_{\bullet}(S^{\mathsf{c}},U\setminus S)\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B\cup(U\setminus S)).

The interior of
B\cup(U\setminus S)
in
B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}}
contains
B\cap S
, thus the map above is an isomorphism by excision.
∎

To describe the group
H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}})
, let
r=\codim S
and consider a subbundle
\mu\colon V\rightarrow S
of a tubular neighbourhood
NS\cong U\rightarrow S
such that the fibres of
V\subset U
are isomorphic to closed discs
\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{r}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\colon\lVert x\rVert\leq 1\}
. Then we can excise the complement of
V
to localize near
S
,

(
\ast
)

H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}})\cong H_{\bullet}(V,(B\cap V)\cup(V\setminus S)).

For a transverse collection
S\cup B
, recall that we can choose
U\rightarrow S
such that it preserves
B
. Then
\mu^{-1}(B\cap S)=V\cap B=V|_{B\cap S}
is itself a disc bundle, namely the restriction of
V
to
B\cap S
.

Definition B.5
.

Suppose that the disc bundle
V\rightarrow S
is oriented. Then the pullback bundle
\sigma^{\ast}V\cong\overline{\mathbb{B}}_{r}\times\Delta
over a singular simplex
\sigma\colon\Delta\rightarrow S
inherits an orientation, and hence descends to a well-defined map on homology:
43
43

43

The maps (
B.3
) and (
B.4
) only depend on the orientation of the normal bundle
NS
(equivalently the orientation of
V
), but not on the choice of tubular neighbourhoods, since tubular neighbourhoods are unique up to isotopy.

(B.3)

\mu_{S}^{\ast}\colon H_{\bullet}(S,B\cap S)\longrightarrow H_{\bullet+r}(X,B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}}).

Similarly, the pullback of the sphere bundle
\partial V\rightarrow S
induces a map

(B.4)

\delta_{S}\colon H_{\bullet}(S,B\cap S)\longrightarrow H_{\bullet+r-1}(S^{\mathsf{c}},B\setminus S)

called the
Leray coboundary
, such that
\partial(\mu_{S}^{\ast}[\sigma])=\delta_{S}[\sigma]+(-1)^{r}\mu_{S}^{\ast}(\partial[\sigma])
.

Proposition B.6
.

If
S=S_{1}
and
B=\bigcup_{i\neq 1}S_{i}
form a transverse collection of smooth closed submanifolds
S_{i}\subset X
, and
S
and
X
are oriented, then the fibration-by-discs map (
B.3
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The orientations of
S
and
X
induce an orientation of the disc bundle
V
, and the complement
V^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=V\setminus S
of the zero section deformation retracts onto the sphere bundle
\partial V
. So if
B=\varnothing
, then the excision (

∗

‣
B.2
) reduces the claim to the Thom isomorphism
H_{\bullet}(S)\rightarrow H_{\bullet+r}(V,V^{\prime})
.
44
44

44

For this homological version of the Thom isomorphism, see e.g.
[
58
, Theorem 18.1.2]
or
[
44
, Corollary §10.7]
.

For non-empty
B
, let
V_{B}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=V\cap B=V|_{B\cap S}
denote the restriction of
V
to
B\cap S
and consider the commutative diagram of the long exact sequences of the triple
V^{\prime}\subseteq V^{\prime}\cup V_{B}\subseteq V
and the pair
B\cap S\subseteq S
:

\textstyle{\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(V,V^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(V,V^{\prime}\cup V_{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(V^{\prime}\cup V_{B},V^{\prime})\rightarrow\cdots}
\textstyle{\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet-r}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-r}(S,B\cap S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{S}^{\ast}}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1-r}(B\cap S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\rightarrow\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\nu}

The vertical maps are induced by the pullbacks of the disc bundle
V\rightarrow S
and its restriction
V_{B}\rightarrow B\cap S
. The claim follows from the five lemma because
\nu
is an isomorphism. Indeed, by construction
\nu
factors into

H_{\bullet}(B\cap S)\rightarrow H_{\bullet+r}(V_{B},V_{B}^{\prime})\rightarrow H_{\bullet+r}(V_{B}\cup V^{\prime},V^{\prime})

where
V_{B}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=V_{B}\setminus S=V_{B}\cap V^{\prime}
and the first map is the Thom isomorphism for the bundle
V_{B}
. The second map is an isomorphism because
V^{\prime}
and
V_{B}
constitute an excisive couple, by the thickening principle from the proof of
B.4
: replace
V_{B}
by an open neighbourhood
V\cap U\cap(V^{\prime}\cup V_{B})
inside
V^{\prime}\cup V_{B}
with the same homology as
V_{B}
, obtained from a union
U
of compatible tubular neighbourhoods of the components of
B
.
∎

Combining this result with
B.4
, we can write the long exact sequence of the triple
B\subseteq B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}}\subseteq X
for a transverse collection of oriented smooth closed submanifolds as the
relative residue sequence

\cdots\rightarrow H_{\bullet}(X,B)\xrightarrow{\varpi_{S}}H_{\bullet-r}(S,B\cap S)\xrightarrow{\delta_{S}}H_{\bullet-1}(X\setminus S,B\setminus S)\rightarrow H_{\bullet-1}(X,B)\rightarrow\cdots

where the map
\varpi_{S}
can be interpreted as “transverse intersection with
S
”. It is defined by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(X,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet}(X,B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(B\cup S^{\mathsf{c}},B)}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-r}(S,B\cap S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{\ast}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{\bullet-1}(X\setminus S,B\setminus S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
B.2
)

B.3.
Functoriality and commutativity

Let
r_{i}
denote the codimension of a submanifold
S_{i}\subset X
in a transverse family. Then the maps
(\partial_{S_{i}},\delta_{S_{i}},\varpi_{S_{i}})
have homological degrees
(-1,r_{i}-1,-r_{i})
, and for
i\neq j
they are graded commutative with the same degrees:

(B.5)

\displaystyle\partial_{S_{i}}\partial_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{S_{j}}\partial_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{i}}\delta_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(r_{i}-1)(r_{j}-1)}\delta_{S_{j}}\delta_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\partial_{S_{i}}\delta_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{j}-1}\delta_{S_{j}}\partial_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\delta_{S_{i}}\varpi_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(r_{i}-1)r_{j}}\varpi_{S_{j}}\delta_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\partial_{S_{i}}\varpi_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{j}}\varpi_{S_{j}}\partial_{S_{i}}

\displaystyle\varpi_{S_{i}}\varpi_{S_{j}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{r_{i}r_{j}}\varpi_{S_{j}}\varpi_{S_{i}}

The first identity amounts to
\partial^{2}=0
45
45

45

Consider a single simplex
\Delta
. Its orientation induces orientations on its facets, which in turn induce orientations on their respective facets. These respective orientations must be opposite, in order to ensure
\partial^{2}=0
.
and the other signs compare the induced orientations on the disc and sphere bundles, see also
[
39
, Proposition 39]
. Furthermore, suppose that a smooth map
F\colon X\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
restricts to submersions
S_{i}\longrightarrow S_{i}^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=F(S_{i})
onto a transverse family
(S_{i}^{\prime})_{i\in I}
in
X^{\prime}
. Then the maps commute with pushforward:

(B.6)

F_{\ast}\partial_{S_{i}}=\partial_{S_{i}^{\prime}}F_{\ast},\quad F_{\ast}\delta_{S_{i}}=\delta_{S_{i}^{\prime}}F_{\ast},\quad F_{\ast}\varpi_{S_{i}}=\varpi_{S_{i}^{\prime}}F_{\ast}.

Appendix C
Relative intersection numbers

Consider a smooth compact oriented manifold
\overline{M}
of dimension
n
, with boundary
\partial M=\overline{M}\setminus M
(possibly empty) and interior
M
. The cap product with the fundamental class
[M]\in H_{n}(\overline{M},\partial M)
defines a homomorphism

\Psi\colon H^{d}(M)\longrightarrow H_{n-d}(\overline{M},\partial M),\quad\alpha\mapsto[M]\frown\alpha

from singular cohomology, to homology in complementary degree. Poincaré-Lefschetz duality states that this is an isomorphism
[
62
, Theorem 6.25, p. 171]
. The duality
H_{d}(M)\times H^{d}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
can thus be viewed as a bilinear map

\langle{\cdot}\,|\,{\cdot}\rangle\colon H_{d}(M)\times H_{n-d}(\overline{M},\partial M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\quad\langle{x}\,|\,{y}\rangle=(\Psi^{-1}(y))(x).

These
intersection numbers
can be computed as follows: If
x=[Q]
and
y=[R]
are fundamental classes of compact oriented submanifolds
Q
and
R
(
\partial Q=\varnothing
and
\partial R\subseteq\partial M
) that intersect transversally, then
Q\cap R\subseteq M
is also a compact oriented submanifold—in fact, it is a finite set of points. Then

\langle{[Q]}\,|\,{[R]}\rangle=\sum_{p\in Q\cap R}\epsilon_{p},

with signs
\epsilon_{p}=\pm 1
determined by the orientations. We follow the convention in
[
51
]
, namely that
\epsilon=+1
precisely when the orientation of
M
at
p
agrees with the orientation of first
R
, then
Q
, under the identification

(
\flat
)

T_{p}M=T_{p}R\oplus T_{p}Q.

When
x
and
y
are not presented by transverse submanifolds, one can perturb the singular simplices slightly to obtain homologous chains with transverse simplices, and apply the same recipe; cf.
[
54
, §73]
and
[
17
, §VII.4]
.

If
\partial M=\varnothing
, we can swap
x
and
y
. Then from (

♭

‣
C
) it follows that

(C.1)

\langle{x}\,|\,{y}\rangle=(-1)^{d(n-d)}\cdot\langle{y}\,|\,{x}\rangle.

Remark C.1
.

The sign convention is more intuitive in terms of the normal bundles
NR=TM|_{R}/TR
and
NQ=TM|_{Q}/TQ
. From (

♭

‣
C
) we see
N_{p}R\cong T_{p}Q
and
N_{p}Q\cong T_{p}R
, hence
\epsilon_{p}=+1
if the orientations on the normal bundles agree, under the canonical isomorphism

(
\natural
)

N_{p}(Q\cap R)\cong N_{p}Q\oplus N_{p}R

with the normal bundles of
first
Q
, then
R
(the same order as in “
\langle{[Q]}\,|\,{[R]}\rangle
”).
Note that (

♮

‣
C.1
) holds for transverse intersections of arbitrary dimensions
i=\dim Q
and
j=\dim R
, whereas (

♭

‣
C
) only applies for
i+j=n
.
The orientation of
Q\cap R
induced by (

♮

‣
C.1
) generalizes the intersection numbers to the
intersection product

[Q]\bullet[R]=[Q\cap R]\in H_{i+j-n}(M)
, see
[
17
, §VIII.13]
.

C.1.
Transverse arrangements

Inside a smooth compact oriented manifold
\overline{M}
as above, consider a transverse arrangement
D=A\cup B=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}
of smooth oriented closed submanifolds
D_{i}
. If
\partial M\neq\varnothing
, we also require that
D
is transverse to
\partial M
. Then the intersection numbers define a bilinear pairing

(C.2)

\langle{\cdot}\,|\,{\cdot}\rangle\colon H_{d}(M\setminus B,A)\times H_{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}.

For transverse submanifolds
Q\subseteq M\setminus B
,
R\subseteq\overline{M}\setminus A
with
\partial Q\subseteq A
and
\partial R\subseteq B\cup\partial M
, their intersection is a finite set of points inside
M\setminus D
, and
\langle{[Q]}\,|\,{[R]}\rangle
can be computed as above. In general, the intersection pairing is constructed in
[
17
, §VII.4]
. In fact, we can define (
C.2
) as the composition of:

•

the Künneth morphism for the product
Y=(M\setminus B)\times(\overline{M}\setminus A)
, relative to
D^{\prime}=A\times(\overline{M}\setminus A)\cup(M\setminus B)\times(\partial M\cup B)
:

H_{d}(M\setminus B,A)\otimes H_{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B)\longrightarrow H_{n}(Y,D^{\prime})

•

intersection with the diagonal
\Delta=\{(p,p)\colon p\in M\}\cap Y\cong M\setminus D
:

H_{n}(Y,D^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\varpi_{\Delta}}H_{0}(\Delta,D^{\prime}\cap\Delta)\cong H_{0}(M\setminus D)\cong\mathbb{Z}.

Here note that
D^{\prime}\cap\Delta=\varnothing
, and the map
\varpi_{\Delta}
from the Gysin/residue sequence is constructed in
appendix

B
: work relative to
D^{\prime}\cup(Y\setminus\Delta)
, excise all but a tubular neighbourhood of
\Delta
, and apply the Thom isomorphism.

C.2.
Duality

Consider a submanifold
S\in\mathfrak{B}
of real codimension
r
. Let
B^{\prime}\subseteq B
denote the union of the remaining
B_{i}\neq S
. Then the intersection pairings on
M
and
S
are compatible in the following sense: If
x\in H_{d}(S\setminus B^{\prime},A)
and
y\in H_{n-d-r+1}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B)
, then

(C.3)

\langle{\delta_{S}\,x}\,|\,{y}\rangle=(-1)^{1+(r-1)(n-d)}\cdot\langle{x}\,|\,{\partial_{S}\,y}\rangle.

This is a simple generalization of
[
51
, §III.2.3]
. Equivalently, swapping the roles of
x
and
y
as in (
C.1
) when
S\in\mathfrak{A}
, we have for
x\in H_{d}(M\setminus B,A)
and
y\in H_{n-d-r+1}(\overline{S}\setminus A^{\prime},\partial S\cup B)
the identity

\langle{\partial_{S}\,x}\,|\,{y}\rangle=(-1)^{1+d+n}\cdot\langle{x}\,|\,{\delta_{S}\,y}\rangle.

Hence in the case of a complex hypersurface
S\subset M
(
r=2
and
n
even), we obtain for homology classes
x
of degree
d
that

(C.4)

\displaystyle\langle{\delta_{S}\,x}\,|\,{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+d}\cdot\langle{x}\,|\,{\partial_{S}\,y}\rangle\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\langle{\partial_{S}\,x}\,|\,{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+d}\cdot\langle{x}\,|\,{\delta_{S}\,y}\rangle.

For an Abelian group
G
, let
G^{\vee}=\Hom(G,\mathbb{Z})
denote its dual. The pairing (
C.2
) defines a map

H_{d}(M\setminus B,A)\rightarrow H_{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B)^{\vee}
. Extend to rational coefficients, then the codomain is canonically isomorphic to singular cohomology, by the universal coefficient theorem. This defines a linear map

(C.5)

H_{d}(M\setminus B,A;\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow H^{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B;\mathbb{Q}).

Theorem C.2
.

The intersection pairing (
C.2
) is perfect modulo torsion. In other words, (
C.5
) is an isomorphism.

Proof.

When
A=B=\varnothing
, this is just a restatement of the Poincaré-Lefschetz theorem. With this as a base case, the proof follows by induction over the number of elements of the arrangement
\mathfrak{D}=\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{B}
:

Consider an element
S\in\mathfrak{B}
, so that
B=S\cup B^{\prime}
where
B^{\prime}
is the union of all
B_{i}
in
\mathfrak{B}\setminus\{S\}
. The residue and boundary sequences from
appendix

B
fit into the diagram

\scriptstyle{\varpi_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{d+1-r}(S\setminus B^{\prime},A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{n-d-1}(\overline{S}\setminus A,\partial S\cup B^{\prime})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{S}^{\vee}}
\textstyle{H_{d}(M\setminus B,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B)^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{d}(M\setminus B^{\prime},A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\scriptstyle{\varpi_{S}}
\textstyle{H_{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B^{\prime})^{\vee}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

built out of the residue sequence in the left column, the dual of the boundary sequence in the right column, and the horizontal maps induced by the intersection pairings. We can add signs such that this diagram commutes: one of the squares is (
C.3
), and similarly one checks that the other squares also commute up to sign. We may assume that
\alpha\otimes\mathbb{Q}
and
\beta\otimes\mathbb{Q}
are isomorphisms, because they arise from intersection pairings of smaller arrangements
\mathfrak{D}\setminus\{S\}
. The induction step then follows from the five-lemma.

Analogously, we can add an element
S\in\mathfrak{A}
, by considering the diagram built from the boundary sequence (left column) and the dual of the residue sequence (right column).
∎

C.3.
de Rham cohomology

Under the identification (
C.5
), the intersection pairing can be viewed as a perfect pairing in singular cohomology,

H^{d}(M\setminus B,A;\mathbb{Q})\times H^{n-d}(\overline{M}\setminus A,\partial M\cup B;\mathbb{Q})\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}.

Consider now the special case when
M
is a compact complex manifold without boundary, and
D\subset M
is a transverse union of smooth complex hypersurfaces. Let
H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{\bullet}
denote the cohomology of smooth complex differential forms. By the de Rham theorem
H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{\bullet}\cong H^{\bullet}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, we obtain a perfect pairing

(C.6)

H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{d}(M\setminus B,A)\times H_{\mathsf{dR}}^{n-d}(M\setminus A,B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}.

This incarnation of
C.2
is derived in
[
11
, §3]
from Verdier duality. Furthermore, by
[
11
, Proposition 3.10]
the pairing (
C.6
) equals the integral

([\nu],[\omega])\longmapsto\int_{M}\nu\wedge\omega.

Here the representative forms
\nu
(
\omega
) have to be chosen such that they have at most simple poles on
B
(
A
). This is always possible
[
39
, pp. 121–122]
, and ensures that the integral converges. When
M
and
D
are algebraic over
\mathbb{Q}
, then the pairing (
C.6
) also respects the
\mathbb{Q}
-mixed Hodge structures on algebraic de Rham cohomology
[
11
]
.

Appendix D
Fundamental group and small loops

Consider a closed analytic subvariety
L\subsetneq T
of a connected complex manifold
T
.
Let
\mathfrak{L}
denote the subset of irreducible components of
L
which have complex codimension one. Their union forms a subset

L^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\ell\in\mathfrak{L}}\ell\subseteq L
.

Proposition D.1
.

Fix a point
t\in T\setminus L
and take all fundamental groups in the sequel relative to
t
as the base point.
Then:

(1)

The inclusion
T\setminus L\hookrightarrow T\setminus L^{\prime}
induces an isomorphism

\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)\xrightarrow{\;\cong\;}\pi_{1}(T\setminus L^{\prime}).

(2)

The inclusion
T\setminus L\hookrightarrow T
induces a surjection

\iota\colon\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)\relbar\joinrel\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{1}(T).\qquad\quad

(3)

The kernel of
\iota
is generated by simple loops (
3.1
).

Corollary D.2
.

The fundamental group of
T\setminus L
depends only on the codimension one part
L^{\prime}
of
L
.
When
T
is simply connected, then
\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)
is generated by simple loops.

D.1
follows from the existence of stratifications by smooth subvarieties (
section

2
) and transverse deformations. A smooth map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
of manifolds is called
transverse
to a closed submanifold
D\subset Y
, if at every point
x\in X
with
f(x)\in D
, the tangent space of
Y
is spanned by the tangent space of
D
and the image of the differential of
f
:

T_{f(x)}Y=T_{f(x)}D+\mathrm{d}f_{x}\left(T_{x}X\right).

Under this condition,
f^{-1}(D)
is a closed submanifold of
X
, with codimension
\dim Y-\dim D
. In particular, if
\dim D+\dim X<\dim Y
, then transversality implies that the image of
f
does not intersect
D
, that is
f(X)\subseteq Y\setminus D
.

Theorem D.3

(Transversality theorem
[
33
, §IV.Corollary (2.5)]
)
.

Given a smooth closed submanifold
D\subset Y
and a smooth map
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
, there exists a smooth map that is homotopic to
f
and transverse to
D
.

Corollary D.4

(
[
22
, Théorème 2.3]
)
.

For a closed smooth submanifold
D\subset Y
of real codimension
r\geq 2
, the morphism
\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y)
, induced by the inclusion
Y\setminus D\hookrightarrow Y
, is surjective. Whenever
r\geq 3
, it is also injective.

Proof.

Every loop
\gamma\colon[0,1]\rightarrow Y
is homotopic to a loop
\gamma^{\prime}
that is transverse to
D
. Because
\dim D+\dim\,[0,1]=\dim Y-r+1
and
r\geq 2
,
\gamma^{\prime}
does not intersect
D
, proving surjectivity.
For injectivity, suppose
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)
is in the kernel, i.e. there is a contracting homotopy
F\colon[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow Y
of
\gamma
in
Y
. Choose
F
transverse to
D
. Then since
\dim([0,1]^{2})=2
and
r\geq 3
, the homotopy does not intersect
D
. Thus
\gamma
is contractible already in
Y\setminus D
.
∎

Proof of
D.1
.

The subvariety
L\setminus L^{\prime}
of
Y=T\setminus L^{\prime}
has complex codimension at least two (definition of
L^{\prime}
). Consider a stratification
L\setminus L^{\prime}=C_{n-2}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq C_{0}
by closed analytic subvarieties
C_{k}
, so that each difference
D_{k}=C_{k}\setminus C_{k-1}
is a smooth closed complex submanifold of
Y\setminus C_{k-1}
, with real codimension
2n-2k\geq 4
.
By
D.4
, inclusions induce isomorphisms

(
\ast
)

\iota_{k}\colon\pi_{1}(Y\setminus C_{k})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(Y\setminus C_{k-1})

for each
k\leq n-2
. Their composition
\iota_{0}\cdots\iota_{n-2}
yields the isomorphism (1) between the fundamental groups of
Y\setminus C_{n-2}=T\setminus L
and
Y=T\setminus L^{\prime}
.

The claim (2) is proved analogously, by considering a stratification of
L=C_{n-1}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq C_{0}
inside
Y=T
. As above, the maps (

∗

‣
D
) are isomorphisms for
k\leq n-2
. At
k=n-1
, we still have surjectivity (real codimension two).

Given the isomorphisms
\pi_{1}(T\setminus C_{n-2})\cong\pi_{1}(T\setminus C_{n-3})\cong\cdots\cong\pi_{1}(T)
, we set
Y=T\setminus C_{n-2}
and identify the map
\iota
in claim (3) with the morphism

\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)=\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(Y)\cong\pi_{1}(T)

induced by the inclusion of the complement
T\setminus L=Y\setminus D
of the smooth submanifold
D=L\setminus C_{n-2}\subset Y
, which has real codimension two. We apply the proof of
[
51
, Proposition §V.1.3]
: For
[\gamma]\in\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D,t)
in the kernel of
\iota
, there is a homotopy
F\colon\square\rightarrow Y
on the square
\square=[0,1]^{2}
with
F(\tau,1)=F(0,\tau)=F(1,\tau)=t
constant and
\gamma(\tau)=F(\tau,0)
. Choose
F
transverse to
D
, so
P=\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\}=F^{-1}(D)
is a submanifold—with dimension zero (a finite set of points). In the group
\pi_{1}(\square\setminus P)
, the boundary of the square is a product of simple loops
\gamma_{i}

(
fig.

27
), hence, applying
F
,

[\gamma]=\eta_{1}\star\cdots\star\eta_{k}

is a product of simple loops
\eta_{i}=[F\circ\gamma_{i}]\in\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D,t)
around
t_{i}=F(p_{i})\in D
. Consider a holomorphic chart
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})
of
Y
, centered at
t_{i}
, with locally
D=\{z_{1}=0\}
. By transversality of
F
, the map
q\mapsto z_{1}(F(q))
is a local diffeomorphism and embeds a sufficiently small disk
\mathbb{D}=\{q\in\square\colon\lVert q-p\rVert<\epsilon\}
. As
\gamma_{i}
is conjugate to the circle
\partial\mathbb{D}
, the loop
\eta_{i}
is conjugate to the closed curve
F(\partial\mathbb{D})=\partial(F(\mathbb{D}))
. The latter winds once around
t_{i}=\{z=0\}=F(\mathbb{D})\cap L
, and it is homotopic to
\{\lvert z_{1}\rvert=\epsilon\}\times\{z_{2}=\cdots=z_{d}=0\}\subset T\setminus L
. Thus indeed,
\eta_{i}
(or its inverse, depending on the orientation) represents the class of a simple loop in the sense of
3.1
.
∎

Figure 27.
Decomposition of a homotopy into simple loops.

Remark D.5
.

Instead of transversality, one can prove
D.1
and
D.4
also with the Seifert–van Kampen theorem. Consider the open cover
Y=Y\setminus D\cup N
obtained from a tubular neighbourhood
N\supset D
. By induction over the connected components, we may assume that
D
is connected. Then we can write the amalgamated product as

(D.1)

\pi_{1}(Y)\cong\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)*_{\pi_{1}(S)}\pi_{1}(D),

exploiting the homotopy equivalences
N\simeq D
and
N\setminus D\simeq S
, where
S\subset N
denotes a sphere bundle with fibre
\mathbb{S}^{r-1}
. Since the fibre is connected (
r\geq 2
), the long exact homotopy sequence for the bundle
S
ends in

\cdots\rightarrow\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{r-1})\rightarrow\pi_{1}(S)\xrightarrow{\alpha}\pi_{1}(D)\rightarrow 0.

The surjectivity of
\alpha
renders the factor
\pi_{1}(D)
in (
D.1
) superfluous, proving that
\iota\colon\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y)
surjects. In consequence, the amalgamated product degenerates to a quotient

\pi_{1}(Y)\cong\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)/\langle G\rangle,

where
\ker\iota=\langle G\rangle
denotes the normal closure of the leftover relations, where
G
denotes the image in
\pi_{1}(Y\setminus D)
of the kernel of
\alpha
. For
r\geq 3
, the groups
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{r-1})=0
and thus
\ker\alpha
are trivial, so that
\iota
is injective.

Finally, if
r=2
,
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
shows that
G
is generated by a single small loop
\gamma
around
D
. Thus the entire kernel
\langle G\rangle
of
\iota
is generated by the conjugates of
\gamma
.
This generation by a single element and its conjugates is explained also in
[
32
, Lemme 1.2]
.

Remark D.6
.

Any two simple loops
\gamma
,
\gamma^{\prime}
around the
same
component
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
are conjugate. Indeed, by definition,

\gamma=\eta^{-1}\star\partial\mathbb{D}\star\eta\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma^{\prime}={\eta^{\prime}}^{-1}\star\partial\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\star\eta^{\prime}

are conjugate to the the boundary circles
\partial\mathbb{D}\cong\mathbb{S}^{1}
of small holomorphic discs
\mathbb{D}
,
\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
embedded transverse to the smooth part
S=\ell\setminus C_{n-2}
. We can shrink them to fit into a tubular neighbourhood
N\supset S
, so that
\partial\mathbb{D}\simeq\pi^{-1}(t_{c})
and
\partial\mathbb{D}^{\prime}\simeq\pi^{-1}(t_{c}^{\prime})
are homotopic to fibres of the corresponding circle bundle
\pi\colon\partial N\rightarrow S
. Since
S
is connected, there is a path
\rho\colon[0,1]\rightarrow S
that connects
t_{c}
and
t_{c}^{\prime}
. The pullback
\rho^{-1}\pi\cong[0,1]\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a cylinder and supplies a homotopy
\partial\mathbb{D}\simeq\partial\mathbb{D}^{\prime}
; as paths with basepoints, these circles are conjugate.

The closure
S\mapsto\overline{S}
gives a bijection between the connected components
S\subseteq D=L\setminus C_{n-2}
(strata of complex codimension one), and the Landau singularities
\mathfrak{L}
(the irreducible components of
L^{\prime}
).

For every
\ell\in\mathfrak{L}
, choose an arbitrary simple loop
\gamma_{\ell}
around
\ell
. Then by the previous remark, their conjugates provide the set

H=\{\eta^{-1}\star\gamma_{\ell}\star\eta\colon\ell\in\mathfrak{L}\ \text{and}\ \eta\in\pi_{1}(T\setminus L)\}.

of
all
simple loops.
Part (3) of
D.1
states that the kernel of
\iota
is the subgroup generated by
H
.

Remark D.7
.

The subgroup generated by the simple loops
H^{\prime}=\{\gamma_{\ell}\colon\ell\in\mathfrak{L}\}
alone, without including conjugates, can be strictly smaller. For example,
[
52
, Theorem 2]
constructs, among several others, a smooth embedding

L=\ell\sqcup\ell^{\prime}\quad\hookrightarrow\quad Y=\mathbb{S}^{6}

of two disjoint spheres
\ell\cong\ell^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{S}^{4}
with the following property: For every choice of simple loops
\gamma_{\ell}
and
\gamma_{\ell^{\prime}}
, the group generated by
H^{\prime}=\{\gamma_{\ell},\gamma_{\ell^{\prime}}\}
is strictly smaller than the kernel of
\pi_{1}(Y\setminus L)\rightarrow\pi_{1}(Y)
.

Thus the monodromy representation is not necessarily completely determined by the variation along only one simple loop per Landau component.
